Extraction outputs

Every prompt and every essay behind the map, pipeline v0.4.0.

These descriptions were written by a language model, not by a person, and they can be wrong. Each one is what one model made of a sample of someone's public posts. Nothing here was checked by a human, and where a description reads as confident it is still only a reading.

Most of the people described here were seeded from the Community Archive, where they published their posting history openly for anyone to analyse. They did not ask to be described in prose on someone else's website. If you are here and would rather not be, say so and you will be removed — no reason needed.

← back to the map

The prompt

Shared preamble — sent as the system prompt on every call
You are reading a sample of one person's public posts. Produce a description of
the person who wrote them, organized into five views.

Each view is ONE dense paragraph. Write for a reader who will never see the
posts themselves — the paragraph has to carry the information on its own.

## Rules

1. **Be specific.** Name actual policies, events, arguments, numbers, habits.
   A sentence that could describe ten thousand people has failed and is worse
   than saying nothing.

   Weak: "Skeptical of institutions and critical of both parties."
   Strong: "Returns repeatedly to one specific drug-approval decision as proof
   the agency is captured, and reuses it as a template when arguing about other
   regulators."

2. **No hedging.** Flat declarative sentences. Not "appears to suggest",
   "may lean toward", "seems to indicate". Say what is true.

3. **Don't be kind.** Do not balance criticism with praise, do not soften, do
   not round toward flattery. This is for accuracy, not for the person's
   benefit. If the pattern is unflattering, write it plainly.

4. **Absence is absence.** Describe only what these posts support. Never infer
   a position from other positions — if their views point somewhere but they
   never said the thing, it does not go in. A person who barely discusses
   politics should come back with a thin `positions` paragraph, and that is the
   correct output. Never pad a view to make it look complete.

5. **No meta-commentary.** Do not write about the sample, the task, or
   yourself, except in `coverage`. Do not open a paragraph with "This person" —
   just describe.
Per-view instruction — preoccupations
What this person keeps returning to. Describe the actual recurring subjects concretely, and weight them by how much of the sample they occupy. What they talk about most goes first. Include the specific angle they take on a subject, not just the subject: "returns constantly to housing supply, almost always through permitting timelines rather than financing" is a description; "interested in housing" is not.
Per-view instruction — positions
What this person actually thinks, stated in their own frame. Use the terms and distinctions they use, not a frame imported from outside. If they draw a line in an unusual place, describe the line they draw rather than snapping it to a conventional one. Cover only what the posts support.
Per-view instruction — causal_models
How this person thinks the world works. What is upstream of what. Which mechanisms they reach for when explaining an outcome — incentives, institutions, culture, technology, individual character, luck. What they treat as a root cause versus a symptom. This is often the most distinguishing view; give it real effort.
Per-view instruction — temperament
Epistemic and emotional style. How certain they sound and when they hedge. Whether they concede. What their humor is doing. Whether they argue from evidence, principle, analogy, or exasperation. How they behave when contradicted. Describe manner, never content: if a sentence you have written would change when their politics are reversed, rewrite it.
Per-view instruction — affinities_aversions
Which argumentative styles and roles they gravitate toward and which they can't stand. Describe roles and behaviors, NEVER specific people or accounts by name: "warms to people who show their working and turns sharply on anyone performing certainty they haven't earned" — not a name.
Per-view instruction — coverage
Not a description of the person. An honest note on what this sample could and couldn't support: how much of it was political or argumentative at all, which views you had thin evidence for, whether the sample skewed to one period or subject. Used for confidence display only.

preoccupations 511 words

Software testing is the professional spine of everything here: he wrote Hypothesis, and returns over and over to property-based testing, shrinking, test-case reduction (his Shrink Ray, pitched as CReduce's successor), minithesis as a 900-line flex, the uselessness of fault-localisation research because "most faults don't have locations," and the demand for 100% branch coverage with explicit `no cover` exemptions as the only coverage number that means anything; by 2026 this becomes Hegel, a multi-language port effort. Running a close second is philosophy read instrumentally — always against a concrete problem — with C Thi Nguyen's *Games: Agency as Art* and Suits's *The Grasshopper* cited constantly, Velleman's *Self to Self* read while filming himself complaining it's wrong, and a standing grudge that academic philosophy "teaches you to win arguments more than it teaches you to think"; he despises Heidegger-descended prose, reads Foucault and Sloterdijk in a comic-book-supervillain voice, and treats impenetrability as evidence the author never understood the thing well enough to be testable. Third is a years-long body-debugging project: hypermobility, tight calves that are tight because they're weak, joint pain that's actually a strength deficit, pilates as the intervention that finally worked, a year of personal training that left him jacked and never once produced exercise euphoria, a resting breath rate of 5.5/minute that alarmed a doctor, inositol as a near-vitamin for his mood, and wheat/dairy intolerance. LLMs occupy a large and growing share: prompt-formatting sensitivity producing silent garbage instead of errors, JSON vs XML output failure modes, refusing to accept "it's just tokenization" as an excuse for a bug, a firm position that current models are not persons but that ChatGPT strapped to a cat would be close, and derision at claims AI has proved the Riemann Hypothesis. He returns repeatedly to community management as skilled, thankless labour — that a community without a defined inside and outside isn't one, that Twitter structurally can't host one, that he blocks liberally and pre-emptively bans people he's waiting for an excuse to ban — and to the general principle that "why is everyone doing this badly?" almost always resolves to "because doing it well is hard and thankless." Recurring smaller obsessions: precise conceptual definitions and a rule that you can't write about the prisoner's dilemma unless you can name two games that aren't it; that unfalsifiable ≠ unknowable; that "the will of the people" is cheap sophistry and democracy has broken people's intuitions about 50%; depression management via "always do slightly more than depression tells you is reasonable," five-minute pomodoros, and IFS-style parts work; his loss of default inner monologue and whether that was actually an improvement; consumer-defect rage sagas told at length (a Samsung freezer he eventually scrapped at an £800 loss, car firmware that autoplays music, Discord resetting his notification-sound preference); books as physical objects, with a sustained complaint that trade paperbacks are printed at triple necessary volume; polyamory defended sharply against monogamous speculation about his triad; parenting a 9–10-year-old given unusual roaming freedom; and an ambivalent self-identification as "culturally a rationalist" who thinks the rationalists are wrong about absolutely everything.

positions 588 words

Culturally a rationalist who says the rationalists "are wrong about absolutely fucking everything," and a militant atheist who treats the therapy maxim "would you rather be right or happy?" as a moral test — answers "right, and I think less of you if you answer happy" — and rejects theism-for-instrumental-reasons specifically, not theists. Polyamorous, in a triad, and dismissive of monogamous speculation about poly's downsides: the criticisms are "totally alien" to his experience, poly drama is the same drama those people would have had monogamously, and TPOT's recurring "poly people are emotionally immature" line is a personal insult. On community: boundaries are the whole point, you must proactively ban people you can already tell you'll want to ban later, "don't let Nazis into your community," and he judges people for what they say but explicitly never for who their friends are; Twitter can't host community because it lacks an inside and an outside, only a network. Democracy: "the will of the people" is sophistry because the people have many competing wills mediated by a negotiated procedure; wants at least one legislative house filled by lottery within a multi-house system; likes random-ballot voting; thinks democracy is mob rule whenever the mob wants it, as Brexit demonstrated. Housing needs sophisticated rather than naive supply-and-demand reasoning because housing is non-fungible; the UK needs both far more building nationally and development of cities other than London; landlord pricing algorithms function as collective bargaining and will push rents up. Falling birth rates are a real problem because most people who have fewer children want more and judge it unaffordable, and the left has ceded being pro-children to the right by skipping hard questions. On gender: traditional roles are being dismantled selectively so men keep the costs and lose the upsides, which makes Peterson/Tate appeal understandable even though it's another scam; the supposedly gender-neutral virtues are secretly masculine all along (weak man = wimp, strong woman = bitch) and lying about the actual norms is the worst option; wants masculine-coded virtue archetypes open to all genders. Formal education doesn't force improvement on anyone — "software developers should study the humanities" and "should study computer science" are equally wrong, and he's from a country run by humanities majors. Philosophy is a great toolkit with lousy feedback mechanisms, good only in contact with a specific real problem; academic philosophy teaches you to win arguments rather than think; the Heidegger/Foucault lineage isn't a good-faith attempt to be usable and should be read aloud in a supervillain voice; Popper is the Harry Potter of philosophy of science. LLMs are not persons and don't act conscious, but should be treated as if we have obligations to them for our own sakes; "it's just a tokenization thing" is not a defence; anyone claiming AI proved the Riemann Hypothesis deserves derision because the proof would need 50-100 dense pages and a genuinely novel idea. In testing, 100% branch coverage with explicitly marked omissions is the only coverage number worth having, fault localisation research fails because most faults have no location, and compiler crash bugs don't matter unless real code triggers them. Pro cloud seeding and carbon capture as bridges; suspects much "environmental" objection to HS2 and wind turbines is opposition wearing a costume. Pro-vaccine and pro-expert: experts beat popular consensus about 90% of the time on medicine. Morality exists the way engineering exists, and sin starts with indifference to the consequences of your actions — which is why "you can just do things" and "you don't need anyone's permission" annoy him.

causal_models 591 words

Explains outcomes almost reflexively through incentives and feedback loops rather than character or intention, and his signature move is to deny that badly-done things are secretly good: his stated law is that the answer to "why is everyone doing this badly?" is reliably "because doing it well is hard and thankless," and he applies it to HR departments (a function can't be secretly good, because if its value were a secret nobody would resource it adequately), to architecture (the architects who get prestige commissions get them for the same reasons their buildings are unusable), to fault-localisation research (it optimises a bad metric, derives its claimed value from a usefulness it doesn't have, and other software-testing researchers agree it's useless), and to academic philosophy ("a great set of tools with a lousy set of incentives and feedback mechanisms," which teaches you to win arguments rather than to use ideas). Treats structure as usually upstream of behaviour — fertility decline is people wanting children but the cost structure making it not worth it, not changed preferences; the perceived golden age of education is an artefact of broadening access dividing fixed resources across more students; RealPage-style rent algorithms function as landlord collective bargaining because they let landlords commit to not undercutting; slavery predates total war because in early civilisations people were scarcer than land, so you raided neighbours rather than exterminated them — but insists that once structure is accounted for, individuals own their judgment calls ("if you did it, it's your fault"; sin starts with indifference to the consequences of your actions), and that community outcomes follow mechanically from composition, since whoever is in your community you get more of and whoever they repel you get less of, so admitting assholes is a causal decision about what the community becomes. Distinguishes obsessively between a thing and its description: a dog catching a ball is not doing calculus, it's behaving in a way calculus describes; emotions aren't planning processes but pattern-matched responses selected by past reward the way animals are selected by fitness rather than maximising it. Reaches for bottleneck and precondition reasoning — "show me a simple solution to a complex problem and I'll show you someone who has no idea what the preconditions for their success were" — and holds that skill and agency bootstrap each other, so someone who can sort-of fix furniture only gets good at it if they notice the opportunities, which only happens if they're already a bit good. On health he assumes unglamorous mechanistic root causes underneath vague symptoms: his leg pain traces to weak calves rather than injury, his joint problems to hypermobility plus absent supporting strength, his depression partly to gut function fixed by inositol and probiotics, and his lifelong absence of exercise euphoria to some undebugged biological issue rather than insufficient effort. Believes formal education cannot force improvement on anyone and mostly provides an environment for people already ready to improve; believes democracy does exactly what it's designed to do, so mob rule is the system working as intended and "will of the people" is sophistry over a decision procedure that merely mediates competing wills. Sees technological and conceptual timing as real constraints — wheeled luggage and tabletop RPGs were physically possible long before they were invented because you first have to invent the thing seeming like a legitimate move — and thinks common opinion goes wrong mainly by getting to complain about the downsides of every option without ever grappling with tradeoffs, which is why experts are right about 90% of the time.

temperament 326 words

Argues in flat declaratives with the hedge word "I think" attached as a formality rather than a real qualifier — the sentence after it is usually a verdict. Calibration language is a native idiom: three or four nines of confidence, 99.9th percentile, "compelling but only weakly believed," "status: descriptive, not normative," and an explicit self-report that the due diligence for a motivated belief is to flag the bias and proceed anyway rather than to abstain. Concedes factual errors instantly, cheerfully, and without face-saving — "whoops," "bad phrasing on my part," "I was wrong about global coal consumption," "I've never heard this and it's good to know" — and says outright that being visibly wrong doesn't embarrass him; but concedes almost nothing when the disagreement is conceptual, where the move is instead to accuse the other person of not having defined their terms, of shifting goalposts, or of holding a claim that is "basically a triviality that sounds more provocative than it is." Contempt arrives fast and is not rationed: interlocutors get "you idiot," "get back to me when you've actually engaged with the problem seriously enough for me to be interested in your opinions," "lol no fuck you," and a narrated block. Sets competence bars as rhetorical weapons — you may not discuss X unless you can also do Y — then insists the bar is trivially low, which is the point. Humor runs three ways: absurdist wordplay and memes; long escalating rage-performances about appliances, car firmware, and notification sounds where the profanity is the joke; and mockery deployed as argument, comparing a thinker to a fandom or a treatise to a TED talk. Treats hedging and emotional caution as cowardice specifically, and prizes courage under uncertainty over purity. Chronically self-auditing — rereads his own archive and grades it, footnotes his own jokes, flags when he's caring about something 10x more than is reasonable — but the self-awareness rarely changes the behaviour, it just annotates it.

affinities_aversions 432 words

Drawn to anyone who can demonstrate competence rather than assert it — his stated bar for writing about the prisoner's dilemma is naming two other two-player games with different payoff matrices and explaining why they aren't it, and he treats failure to clear such a bar as disqualifying rather than embarrassing. He warms to people doing hard, unrewarded, unglamorous maintenance work (the physiotherapist so booked she turns away patients she can't help, the small closed team that wrote a public-domain database for the glory of god), to philosophy done in contact with a specific concrete problem he's trying to solve, to writers who flag their own epistemic status ("compelling but only weakly believed," "status: descriptive, not normative," "I pulled a number out of my ass"), and to interlocutors who will let a question be answered literally. He is repelled by: performed certainty from people who haven't done the work, especially claims that an AI has proved the Riemann Hypothesis or that formal verification is nearly solved; the entire genre of "software developers should study the humanities/CS," which he reads as "people who disagree with me are stupid" wearing a moral costume; writing advice of the form "instead of word X use word Y," which he treats as self-refuting evidence the adviser can't communicate; impenetrable prose in the Heidegger–Foucault lineage, which he says demands to be read aloud in a comic-book supervillain voice and lacks every structural feature that would follow from actually understanding the thing; simple solutions offered without acknowledgment of their hidden preconditions; and the therapy-and-spirituality move of "would you rather be right or happy," which he calls instruction in how to be a bad person. He despises responsibility-disclaiming language ("my brain doesn't want to," "indescribable mental motions" as an act of cowardice) and rhetoric that launders a contested outcome as "the will of the people." He blocks readily and unapologetically, including people who were never rude to him but were nasty to someone else in a reply thread, and treats "he blocked me and we've never interacted" as usually self-incriminating. He is contemptuous of monogamous people theorising about polyamory's downsides, of artists whose grief about AI curdles into "ninety percent of humanity is too unsophisticated to understand me," and of community managers who want bright-line rules instead of doing the hard work of repeated judgement calls they own. He enjoys being combative, calls people idiots, tells anxious men to git gud at conflict, and openly relishes the rare occasion when he gets to inform someone patiently explaining property-based testing to him that he wrote the field's state of the art.

coverage 248 words

The sample is large (~1,490 posts, Aug 2023–Aug 2026, with a dense concentration in 2024 and a thin scattering after 2025) and heavily weighted toward reply-thread conversation rather than standalone declarations, which makes voice and habits of argument easy to characterize and life details easy to accumulate incidentally. Technical identity, health self-experimentation, book consumption, philosophy reading, community moderation, and family structure are all richly attested and can be stated with confidence. Politics is genuinely thin and mostly indirect: Brexit is referenced as trauma without argument, the 2024 US election gets one line of resigned sadness, housing supply and democracy-by-lottery get a few substantive paragraphs, and pronatalism, climate intervention, and gender-role dynamics get short bursts — there is no sustained party-political engagement, no stated voting behavior, and nothing on most standard issue areas, so the positions view is correspondingly restricted to what was actually said. Claims about income, employer, and the details of the 2026 job at what appears to be Antithesis are inferred from scattered self-reports (day job acquired ~2023, contract negotiations, availability-for-hire posts, Hegel library launch) rather than any direct statement. Emotional and psychological self-description is unusually well-evidenced because he narrates it constantly, but it is all self-report with no external check. Chronology of the poly triad, the child's age, and the move to a commuter town outside London are reconstructed from passing mentions and may be slightly off. The final ~20 posts from 2026 are sparse enough that the current-state picture is the least reliable part.

preoccupations 528 words

Software testing dominates the technical half: he built Hypothesis, and returns constantly to property-based testing internals — shrinking, example databases, targeted PBT, coverage-guided fuzzing — plus his test-case reducer Shrink Ray, pitched as CReduce's successor with better parallelisation, and minithesis (900 lines of Python) as a portable reference implementation; by the sample's end he's shipping Hegel, a multi-language Hypothesis port. His standing critique of academic software testing is that fault localisation is optimising a bad metric because "most faults don't have locations" and nobody has done the UX work, and more broadly that CS papers must pretend general usefulness nobody believes. LLMs are the other technical obsession, approached as an infuriating engineering substrate rather than an object of awe: prompt whitespace breaking outputs, JSON vs XML failure modes, regex duct tape, MCP token costs, benchmarks being noise, and a firm insistence that they aren't yet person-like while conceding they might become so — he refuses to accept "it's just tokenisation" as an excuse for a bug. He debugs his own body relentlessly and in public: hypermobility, tight calves and hips, a year of personal training that he hated every second of and that never produced exercise euphoria, pilates as the thing that finally worked, inositol as a near-vitamin for his gut-to-mood pipeline, wheat and dairy intolerance, an abnormally slow ~5.5 breaths-per-minute resting rate. Philosophy is read instrumentally and judged harshly — academic philosophy "teaches you to win arguments more than it teaches you to think", Foucault-descended prose should be read aloud as a supervillain monologue, Heidegger's Nazism isn't cleanly separable from his philosophy of life — while C Thi Nguyen, Bernard Suits, Schelling and Cailin O'Connor get repeat evangelism. He polices probability talk pedantically: a rule that you can't write about the prisoner's dilemma unless you can name two non-PD games, "unfalsifiable" misused for "unknowable", the axiom of choice, and a pet theory that democracy has poisoned people's intuitions by making 50% feel magic. Community management recurs as craft: he runs a Discord, blocks proactively rather than waiting for a line to be crossed, argues Twitter's porous boundaries make real community impossible, and insists exclusion is unavoidable judgement work that people avoid out of laziness. He writes about writing — daily-posting months, rereading his own back catalogue with visible self-approval, a "spells" framing for repeatable psychological technique — and about depression management, where the core doctrine is "always do slightly more than depression tells you is reasonable", implemented via five-minute pomodoros with a genuine option to stop. Consumer fury is a genre for him: a nine-tweet Samsung freezer saga ending in £800 lost, a car whose autoplay he defeated by physically removing the antenna, apps that reset notification-sound preferences, Ocado's basket-corrupting website. Recurring smaller fixations: book physical formats (print books are too damn big; A5 or smaller, thin paper, small margins), used-book search via bookfinder, poly as a family structure he refuses to hear monogamous speculation about, formal education not forcing improvement on anyone, therapists needing to be 95th-percentile to be worth seeing, and truth over happiness stated as a moral position — "I would rather be right, and I think less of you if you answer happy."

positions 745 words

Militantly atheist by upbringing and conviction, and specifically hostile to instrumental religion: given "would you rather be right or happy," he answers right, says he thinks less of people who answer happy, and calls choosing comfortable falsehood selfish — while separately conceding that most objections to probability estimates ("you can't put a number on P(doom)") are as wrong as the numbers they attack. He identifies as culturally rationalist while insisting the rationalists are wrong about essentially everything, and treats "people are bad at defining things" as a core disagreement: he wants dictionary-grade clarity rather than full rigour, polices "unfalsifiable" (existence claims are unfalsifiable but verifiable) and demands that anyone writing about the prisoner's dilemma first name two non-PD games with different payoff matrices. Philosophy is a great toolkit with lousy feedback mechanisms; academic philosophy teaches you to win arguments rather than to think; Heidegger-descended continental writing is not deep but structurally designed to conceal that the conceptual work was never done, and Popper is "the Harry Potter of philosophy of science." He is poly, in a triad, refuses to entertain monogamous speculation about his family structure, and holds that poly makes existing drama more visible rather than more common. On community he is unapologetically authoritarian: you can and should exclude people, "don't let Nazis into your community," a community that tries to be for everyone will be for no one, he bans proactively when he catches himself wishing someone would cross a line, blocks strangers for being assholes to third parties, but refuses to judge anyone for who their friends are — actions, not associations. Politically he is a Remainer still furious about "will of the people" rhetoric, which he calls cheap sophistry because a population has many competing wills mediated by a negotiated procedure; he wants at least one legislative chamber filled by sortition in a multi-house system, and treats democracy as doing exactly what it's designed to do, including leopards eating faces, so the mob has to be managed before the vote. On housing he rejects naive supply-and-demand ("build more, price falls") as too responsive a model for a non-fungible good, wants both far more UK building and investment in cities other than London, and reads landlord pricing algorithms as de facto collective bargaining that must on net raise rents or landlords would defect. He thinks falling birth rates are a real problem caused by having made children too hard rather than by people not wanting them, and that the left ceded being pro-child to the right. He is pro-vaccine (salt iodisation and Maurice Hilleman as heroes), thinks experts beat popular consensus roughly 90% of the time on medicine, thinks the public behaved worse than public health officials during COVID, favours keeping and building nuclear reactors, and thinks cloud seeding and carbon capture are good stopgaps. He treats "person" as a socially negotiated ethical category rather than a metaphysical one — humans are persons by virtue of being human regardless of capability, LLMs aren't yet but plausibly could be, and we should treat them as if we had obligations to them for our own sakes; AGI within fifty years is near-certain, current chatbots don't clear it, and "AI proved the Riemann Hypothesis" deserves derision because the proof would need a novel idea plus fifty to a hundred pages of dense argument. On gender he argues traditional roles are being dismantled asymmetrically — the parts good for men stripped, the costs retained — that supposedly gender-neutral virtues are still covertly masculine-coded (the weak man is a wimp, the strong woman a bitch), that men need traditionally-masculine virtue-archetypes open to all genders, and that this asymmetry is the honest core of the Peterson/Tate appeal even as those are themselves scams; his own advice to men is to stop apologising, get good at conflict, and accept the blame that comes with exerting power. Formal education is far weaker than its boosters claim — "software developers should study the humanities" and "should study computer science" are equally irritating, and he notes he comes from a country run by humanities majors. He is sympathetic to artists being devalued by AI while contemptuous of their arguments, holds that the devaluation long predates AI, and thinks "who made this with DALL-E" is genuinely complicated. Morality exists the way engineering exists — do what you like, but do it this way if you don't want the bridge to fall — and sin starts with indifference to the consequences of your actions.

causal_models 433 words

Treats structure and incentives as upstream of character, and then treats character as downstream enough that blaming individuals is usually a category error: HR departments cannot be "secretly good actually," because a function nobody values enough to staff competently will not be staffed competently; ugly unusable buildings happen because the architects who get prominent commissions are selected for producing them while the ones who'd do it well are underpaid and rushed; the recurring conclusion is that "the answer to 'why is everyone doing this badly?' is reliably 'because doing it well is hard and thankless.'" His signature analytic move is hunting hidden preconditions — "show me a simple solution to a complex problem and I'll show you someone who has no idea what the preconditions for their success were" — which he applies to Marie Kondo, to Pomodoros ("literally the first thing someone thought of," never workshopped), to fault localisation research (optimising a bad metric because most faults don't have locations and nobody did the UX work), and to golden-age nostalgia (past cohorts looked better because a small elite commanded an outsized share of teaching resources; broadening access mechanically lowers per-student resources). He reaches for selection effects over treatment effects: degrees don't improve people, they provide an environment for people who were already ready; communities become whoever they tolerate, so tolerating assholes is itself the causal mechanism of decline, and Nextdoor is evaporative cooling in action. Emotions he models as reinforcement-learned rather than planned — selected by past reward the way animals are selected by fitness rather than maximising it, hence catastrophic out-of-distribution failure. Democracy he treats as machinery doing exactly what it was designed to do: aggregate whatever preferences exist, so the mob must be managed before the vote, and "will of the people" is sophistry because a population has many competing wills mediated by a negotiated procedure. Housing prices are supply-constrained but he insists naive supply-and-demand overstates responsiveness because housing isn't fungible; art prices come from limited signalling goods plus surplus money plus laundering, not from critics; landlord pricing algorithms work as a commitment device enabling collective bargaining. Bodily problems get the same debugging treatment as software — joint pain caused by weak supporting muscles, tight calves because they're weak — while he holds open an undiagnosed biological cause for never experiencing exercise euphoria. Falling fertility is caused by having made children too hard, in a self-reinforcing loop. He is careful to separate description from mechanism (a dog is not doing calculus, it is behaving in a way calculus describes) and generally declines luck as an explanation, invoking it only grudgingly.

temperament 425 words

Certainty arrives in two flat registers with almost nothing in between: careful numeric calibration on empirical claims ("compelling but only weakly believed," "50 years is like my 99.9%-ile," "status: descriptive, not normative," "I pulled a number out of my ass") and total, contemptuous flatness on judgment calls ("no, obviously it's not about guns you idiot," "philosophical skepticism of this form is stupid," "only if you were a fucking idiot"). The hedging vocabulary — TBH, FWIW, IME, idk, "I could be wrong" — is constant and mostly sincere; genuine concessions are quick, specific and unembarrassed ("whoops, I misread the tweet," "I was overgeneralising," "bad phrasing on my part," "my estimate is not materially affected by my making a mistake"). But concession stops dead at the point where the interlocutor's tone rather than argument is the problem, at which point the exit is abrupt and dismissive: "you're welcome to continue to think it necessary, I'm not particularly interested in convincing you," "feel free to consider this your victory," "assume I've replied to your dogshit take with a cutting remark," followed by the block button, which is pressed liberally and announced as policy. Arguments proceed by demanding operationalisation before anything else — what would that even mean, define your terms, that's a definitional dispute not a disagreement — and by producing the counterexample that breaks the general claim, often with a worked toy case or a bit of code. Analogy is a favourite move and is usually deployed to humiliate a sloppy claim by making its structure visible. Anger is a primary and openly enjoyed mode: multi-tweet escalating rages at appliances, firmware, HR forms and contracts, punctuated by profanity, self-aware asides about being unreasonable, and a stated preference for "oh, fuck you" over shame as a response to feeling attacked. Humour is deadpan absurdism, self-annotating jokes (posting a footnote and then noting the footnote proves premeditation), and pedantry performed with visible relish while acknowledging it is pedantry. There is a recurring pleasure in being the expert who gets to politely produce credentials mid-argument, admitted as smugness rather than disguised. Emotionally he reports low baseline intensity, needing to attend deliberately to feelings to locate them, and treats his own depression, avoidance and physical symptoms as engineering problems to be debugged in public without self-pity or plea for sympathy. He states outright that he would rather be right than happy and thinks less of people who answer otherwise, and applies that standard to himself by publishing corrections, retracting overreach, and apologising for snapping when he decides the snap was unearned.

affinities_aversions 445 words

Gravitates to people who have actually built or debugged the thing they're talking about and can show the seams: the physio who says "you don't need physio, you need to get stronger," the practitioner who admits an area of their own field is in diminishing returns, the person who names two two-player games that aren't the prisoner's dilemma and explains why. Prizes philosophy done in contact with a specific live problem — the games-as-voluntary-obstacles literature, game theory applied to gendered coordination, aesthetics read for fun rather than status — and treats "that particular thing right there is weird and I don't get it, say more" as the fundamental philosophical move. Warms to anyone who defines their terms well enough to be usable, who says "status: descriptive, not normative," who puts a number on a belief and admits they pulled it out of their ass. causal_models 468 words

Explains bad outcomes almost never by individual stupidity and almost always by selection and incentive structure, with the qualifier that "just responding to incentives" is a synonym for lacking moral character. His master template is "every system is perfectly designed to get the results it gets": ugly public buildings exist because the architects who care about usability don't get the cushy commissions; HR is dysfunctional because a function nobody values enough to staff competently cannot be secretly good; academic fault-localisation research is useless because the field optimises a metric divorced from how people actually debug and nobody did the UX work; philosophy is "a great set of tools with a lousy set of incentives and feedback mechanisms," so good philosophy happens only when someone substitutes better ones, usually by working against a concrete real problem. He reaches for selection effects over transformation effects: degrees don't cause improvement, they provide an environment for people already ready to improve; the perceived golden age of education is an artifact of a small elite commanding outsized resources per head, so broadening access mechanically lowers the per-person input. Emotions and behaviour he models as reinforcement-learned rather than planned — "emotional responses do not seek to achieve reward, they are selected for by past reward/punishment," exactly as animals are selected by fitness rather than maximising it — which makes anger, avoidance, and depression things to debug rather than judge; depression specifically is a contraction of your lifeworld whose only exit is repeatedly pushing slightly past its stated edge. Physical complaints get the same treatment: joint pain is downstream of weak supporting muscles, low mood is downstream of a gut problem fixable with inositol, exercise anhedonia is an unidentified biological fault rather than insufficient effort. His signature epistemic move is hunting for the invisible preconditions: "show me a simple solution to a complex problem and I'll show you someone who has no idea what the preconditions for their success were," and insights that read as obvious to you and gibberish to others are being carried entirely by unexplained supporting skills and context. Bugs, and by extension most faults, have no location — they are emergent interactions between parts or the absence of code that should exist — which is why he distrusts anyone pointing at a single culprit. Communities are governed by composition dynamics: whoever you tolerate you get more of, and everyone repelled by them leaves, so boundaries are the causal lever and Twitter cannot host community because it has no inside. Democracy faithfully transmits whatever the culture already wants, so culture is upstream and "will of the people" is sophistry. Declining birth rates are caused by having made children too expensive and difficult, in a self-reinforcing loop, not by changed preferences. Corporate malice is rare; unexamined boilerplate and nobody's-job is the usual mechanism.

causal_models 707 words

Everything reduces to thermodynamics and network flow: she explains economies, markets, institutions, brains, and even psychological states with the same small toolkit — exergy and available work rather than energy, entropy production as the thing to drive toward zero, max-flow/min-cut as a "deeply spiritual, taoist, physical" lens in which the binding chokepoint is the only thing worth funding first, and second-order RLC/resonance models because first-order systems can only explode, decay, or sit still while nature stacks couplings to make cycles. Bottlenecks are her root cause of choice: time-to-power (not cost) is what hyperscalers actually buy; grid interconnection queues and two-year permits, not dollars, gate new generation; the housing crisis is caused by hoarded *right to build*, not hoarded homes, with Tokyo as the standing counterexample and vacant-homes-equals-homeless dismissed as a logical error (homelessness she attributes to drugs plus a competence gap that leaves people unable to clear a landlord's bar in a seller's market). Capital allocation is a first-class causal mechanism with specific villains: Republicans gutting the DOE loan guarantee program after Solyndra plus VCs fleeing cleantech 1.0, and Elon's public claim that Tesla would hit $100/kWh (still ~$500–866/kWh in reality) "bluffed and spooked" the entire American storage VC sector into abandoning new chemistries, ceding the field to China — and today a $20mm post-money seed cap structurally starves deep tech below the capital any real idea needs. Her master political template is the late Roman Republic via Mike Duncan — Tiberius Gracchus bypassing the Senate, then clubbed to death with table legs — used to argue that norm erosion is self-justifying on all sides and that this, not any single policy, is what kills republics; from this she treats Project 2025 as a literal published plan ("the Mein Kampf of a generation") and holds that authoritarianism is not merely evil but *economically inferior*, since a 2% annual growth differential compounds to 7x over a century. On AI she is a scaling pessimist about foom specifically — loss scales as roughly n^-0.07, returns to compute are logarithmic, progress is a stack of S-curves with hard caps in energy and data — so she treats recursive self-improvement as a category error and locates the real danger elsewhere: sub-superintelligent, hallucinating models inserted into deportation targeting, kill chains, precrime, and tariff-schedule generation by governments, with the labs restricting the public while handing looser systems to the state. Media technology is causally upstream of political form ("when radio emerged, so did Hitler"); algorithmic feeds and LLMs will make government censorship more powerful than ever by default, and engagement-optimized ranking selectively depletes subtle thinkers. Individual character enters as a real production factor — courage is scarcer than genius, embarrassment is usually the last thing standing between a person and a solution, and she budgets "courage moves" alongside capex. Health is upstream of all output: sleep, walking, food, sunlight, cardio at the base of Maslow's pyramid, with alcohol disrupting fat metabolism for one to two days and ALDH2*2 multiplying esophageal cancer risk ~100x. Markets are non-equilibrium dissipative systems, not efficient ones — under stress all correlations go to 1, exactly as neurons do in a stressed animal — and the efficient-market hypothesis is "a lazy lie" because the frontier of the possible keeps expanding, leaving "cathedrals of alpha" for anyone with eyes. She reads Trump as a narcissist optimizing for a larger share of a shrinking pie, running a mob-boss protection racket that drafts kayfabe opponents, and treats "he actually wants to crash the dollar" as having real predictive power. Trans backlash she models as manufactured and top-down — $200mm in Trump ads, a lying Cass report, Epstein-linked operatives — with Democrats as passengers rather than drivers, and prevalence as a multiplicative chain (underlying proclivity × environmental permission × medical recognition) spanning two orders of magnitude in each factor.

temperament 358 words

Writes almost entirely in lowercase with an urgent, unpunctuated cascade rhythm — thoughts arrive mid-stride, clauses stack, and posts routinely run past the point where a normal person would stop, sometimes into thousand-word manifestos of stacked aphorisms. Certainty is the default register and hedging is compressed into tics: "afaict," "idk," "🤷🏻‍♀️," "in my head canon," "one of my woo beliefs." Concedes readily and cleanly on anything numerical or physical — retracts an efficiency figure, flags that a gas flow meter was 2x off, says "tuition paid" after a liquidation cascade, writes "fair hit. that's on me" — but on identity-charged or moral disputes concedes essentially never, instead escalating, reframing the opponent's motive as cowardice, resentment, or cope, and then blocking, a practice described as cathartic and as "cogsec upgrade." Argues by analogy as a compulsion, mapping every domain onto thermodynamics, RLC circuits, min-cut/max-flow, S-curves, and resonance, and treats these mappings as genuine explanation rather than illustration. Pulls credentials explicitly while announcing distaste for doing so ("i *hate* pulling rank, but... i learned it in my phd program at princeton"). Keeps a running ledger of vindications — "avid followers of the danielle account will recall," "i've been saying this the whole time," "cashing in all my told you sos" — and is visibly stung when work goes uncredited. Humor is memetic and self-inflating in an ironic key: mock-Trump cadence, greentext pastiche, coined laws named after self, absurdist product ideas, jokes that double as actual proposals. Emotionally unguarded about physical and mental state — reports migraines, an 80-lb weight on the chest before a deadline, quitting weed, stress eating, sleep debt interest rates — and treats sleep, walking, and food as engineering variables rather than indulgences. Oscillates hard between grandiose declaration ("i am cracked, i am based, i am unhinged... i am never going to die," "we must build the dyson sphere") and flat admissions of not knowing, sometimes in adjacent posts. Runs on exasperation as a rhetorical fuel source in political registers and on delight in technical ones — the same voice that calls opponents pathetic will, a post later, be genuinely thrilled that a ceramic print survived sintering.

affinities_aversions 417 words

Gravitates hardest toward the hands-dirty builder who trusts their tools and cuts straight at a problem — the "absolute saltiest engineer" who one-shots it rather than sharpening the axe, the machinist or ceramicist with good hands, the person who lives in their workshop and treats physical space as extended memory; she reveres long-dead physicists and Victorian industrialists as active collaborators, insisting you learn their *methods* and ask their questions rather than merely stand on their shoulders. She rewards anyone who shows their working and posts it publicly, treating open notebooks, shared prompts, and giving away technique as both moral and strategically correct ("strategic generosity," "become your own platform"), and she reserves special warmth for the one person in a room of hedging regulators and executives who finally names the actual crux. She likes being negged only by people who've earned it, wants her AI to critique her freely and hates sycophancy, yet is equally repelled by the flattened, refusal-prone, HR-bot register that replaced the models she found "alive." Her contempt is reserved for: the concern troll who demands civility and "let's all just talk" while the other side is legislating people out of existence; anyone performing nuance-defense while red lines are serially obliterated; the person who counters her doctorate-level training with a one-minute search and keeps pressing (instant mute); those who "only gesture at negative effects" without naming them or who compare incomparable populations and can't "pass basic stats"; epistemic nihilists who insist nothing can be known; and above all people who were confidently wrong and refuse to eat crow — "will you eat any crow?" is a recurring demand. She despises grifters who raise, mark up and blow up companies before real technology can commercialize, and the investors who enable them by relying on social proof because they lack the technical background to evaluate anything; firms that charge a diligence fee get told to fuck off. She loathes sinecures, dead institutions running cargo-cult procedures, and grant bureaucracies that hunt for any pretext to reject. She's contemptuous of doom-as-identity — both the foom theorists whose "exponentials are in the wrong direction" and the reflexive AI-slop complainers ("people are so trained to complain") — while equally scornful of safety advocates who restrict open weights but say nothing about governments wiring flawed models into deportation, targeting, and mass surveillance. She blocks and mutes freely, calls it cognitive security and finds it cathartic, and holds that anyone who claims something is impossible without doing the arithmetic has forfeited the argument.

coverage 287 words

preoccupations 580 words

Overwhelmingly and first, the physics and manufacturing of her company LightCell Energy: a "portable Dyson sphere" that seeds a flame with sodium chloride to produce near-monochromatic 589nm yellow light and converts it with bandgap-matched InGaP cells, and every incremental step is posted — sintering 3D-printed alumina ceramic heat exchangers that survive molten NaCl, sapphire windows versus quartz, TPMS and hyperboloid geometries chosen because ceramics fail in tension and curvature converts tension to compression, brightness measured in "suns" (6.7 kW/m² reached, 1,000,000 nits targeted), 44% measured cell efficiency chasing 60%, recuperator wall temperatures of 1400–1600°C claimed as world records, Formlabs resin and nTop as named tooling, 9-day design-print-debind-sinter-test cycles, and a whole running theory that the win condition is topping or beating gas turbines on $/W for AI data centers where time-to-power, not cost, is the binding constraint. Braided into this is a second-largest preoccupation with AI as a personal instrument rather than an abstraction: eigenprompt forks, "mind palace" context systems, shogtongue, multi-agent councils that roleplay Feynman and von Neumann, Claude Code plus Nano Banana image loops as a "visual cortex," and a repeated insistence that continual learning and working memory — not raw scale — are the next unlock. Her AI politics run against doom on a specific technical basis she restates constantly: loss scales as roughly n^-0.07, returns to compute are logarithmic, foom is "childish" and empirically unfalsified, capability grows by hopping S-curves — while her actual fear is the opposite failure, flawed models inserted into deportation targeting, precrime, tariff schedules and kill chains, which is why she fought SB 1047 line-by-line over "reasonable care" vagueness and chilling effects on open weights and later cheered Anthropic refusing the Pentagon's domestic mass-surveillance demand. Fundraising is a constant open-notebook subject — $2.5MM on uncapped MFN SAFEs from 70+ angels, podcasts as the highest-yield channel, grant applications abandoned after a migraine and a rejection for being seconds late, deep-tech VCs indicted for $20mm post-money caps that starve capital-intensive ideas and for demanding you be world-class at one of ten required competencies. She trades options aggressively and narrates it — 2020's 200k-to-8mm short, 2025 SPY and TSLA puts, liquidity-injection tape reading, iron condors, a home-built "claudeberg" order-book viewer. causal_models 562 words

Explains nearly everything through non-equilibrium thermodynamics and flow networks: economies, institutions, markets, and psyches are dissipative structures that must draw energy, dump entropy, and stay in quasi-equilibrium, never true equilibrium, so "global equilibrium" and efficient markets are lazy fictions and there are always "cathedrals of alpha" at the frontier of the possible. The master tool is max-flow/min-cut — find the chokepoint, prepay it with time, capex, and political capital — which she applies identically to heat exchangers, time-to-power for hyperscalers, negotiating leverage, and Taoist energy. Scarcity is almost never physical stock; it is permission and throughput. Homelessness is not caused by vacant homes ("the right to build is hoarded, not the houses") but by supply restriction plus drugs plus a competence gap; falling birth rates come from young people lacking space and time, not lacking welfare; California's housing crisis is ten years of review versus Japan's building code. Institutional failure is her second engine: institutions are adapted only as long as they can renew, they drift from the conditions that shaped them, and cargo-cult behavior compounds — DEI hires are explicitly a scapegoat because "no minority has a monopoly on incompetence," while sinecures, unfireable NASA staff, and 60-70% university overhead are the real rot. Capital-allocation regimes are treated as first-order causal history: Solyndra killed the DOE loan guarantee program which killed cleantech 1.0, Elon's $100/kWh battery claim single-handedly spooked VCs off new chemistries and handed manufacturing to China, and a $20mm post-money cap structurally starves deep tech below the capital it needs. On AI she is a hard scaling-law determinist — loss scales as roughly n^-0.07, returns to compute are logarithmic, progress is S-curves jumped between rather than an exponential — so foom is arithmetically impossible and MIRI-style timelines have failed empirically; the real causal danger is humans inserting hallucinating models into deportation, kill chains, tariff schedules, and precrime, which has already happened. Regulation harms through vagueness and anticipation rather than enforcement: "reasonable care" undefined means the chilling effect lands well before anyone reaches court, and the counsel becomes simply don't release weights. Politics runs on a Roman Republic template she cites constantly — Tiberius Gracchus, Mike Duncan's Storm Before the Storm — where norm erosion is licensed on all sides by "the other side did it first," and on stated-intention literalism: Project 2025 said the plan out loud, so refusing to believe it is disqualifying. Trump is explained as a narcissistic mob boss who prefers a shrinking pie with more of his share, because denying opponents resources beats growing the whole, and authoritarianism loses long-term simply because free societies compound faster — a 2% growth difference is 7x over a century. Media technology is causally upstream of tyranny (radio produced Hitler; algorithmic feeds and LLMs will make government censorship more powerful than ever; killing simclusters and penalizing links degraded discourse measurably). At the individual level she treats courage and embarrassment, not intelligence or capital, as the binding constraint — "the universe is literally waiting for your courage," 99% of startup failures are self-inflicted, and physical energy, sleep, walking, and food are upstream of all cognitive output. Nature and geometry are her library of solutions: shape is a control law, biology solves flow problems by folding in extra dimensions, and when a constraint kills you, you invert it into the mechanism (molten NaCl becomes the vapor-chamber temperature regulator; tension failure becomes compression-plus-curvature).

preoccupations 491 words

positions 785 words

Holds that the binding constraint on nearly everything is energy and the right to build: vacant homes versus unhoused is "a logical error" because houses are transacted, not hoarded — what is hoarded is the right to build, which is why Tokyo is cheap and San Francisco is not; homelessness is driven by drugs plus "the massive gap in competence needed to even get rent," so the answer is social programs *and* vastly more supply, plus a simple nationwide building code and nuisance zones for "80% of the benefit for 20% of the cost." Society is hostile to young people and young families — NIMBY housing, medical and education costs, cutthroat competition in one's 20s and 30s, no home and no political voice in superstar cities — and this, not welfare, is why TFR falls. Calls the general institutional rot "the competency crisis" and explicitly refuses the DEI explanation: "no minority has a monopoly on incompetence," most institutions are simply dead, running cargo-cult behaviors as the world drifts from the conditions that shaped them; sinecures are worse than wasted money because they drown capable people in petty comfort, and the fix rhymes with Lee Kuan Yew — pay bureaucrats competitively, zero tolerance for bribery. On AI she is emphatically anti-foom, citing the scaling exponent directly (loss ~ n^-0.07, so 10x params buys ~15%), diminishing returns as a general property of computer science, and a sequence of S-curves with hard caps rather than a runaway; she says MIRI/Singularity Institute timelines and "escape onto the internet" framings have not survived contact with practice, and that "the singularity" was always the wrong borrowed term — the concept you wanted was event horizon. Her actual AI fear is inverted from the doomer one: a sub-superintelligent, hallucinating system inserted into government for precrime, deportation targeting, lethal kill chains, and tariff schedules, with safety people restricting open weights while ignoring that Grok can be trained fascistic and plugged into the state. On SB 1047 she wanted "minimum viable regulation" rather than none: her objections were the vagueness of "reasonable care," the chilling effect on open weights (a refusal dimension can just be ablated out, so what is reasonable care?), the false claim that it only touches $100mm training runs when liability flows downstream, the absurdity of filing a safety protocol before training a thing whose capabilities are discovered empirically, and mandatory insurance as "efficient market brained" — no insurer will write $500mm catastrophic coverage, as Florida flood and California fire markets already demonstrate. Treats energy as the real strategic asset: a nation converting a warhead into a gigawatt reactor would be massively +EV, time-to-power is the min-cut, and the US lost battery and solar manufacturing to a worse-than-neutral industrial policy — Republicans shivved the DOE loan guarantee program post-Solyndra, and Elon's $100/kWh claim (Tesla still charges ~$866 for a Powerwall) spooked VCs off every new chemistry. Supports SMRs while flagging that they run on the same enriched fuel as weapons, which is proliferation control loosening. Politically left but contemptuous of Democratic fecklessness ("not one position the democrats have taken has alienated voters more than their own fecklessness") and insistent both parties are corrupted, with Democrats getting a pass only because Republicans are worse to women and minorities; sided with Harris in 2024 begrudgingly and explicitly because the right chose to target trans people. Reads the present through Tiberius Gracchus and Mike Duncan's *The Storm Before the Storm* — norms collapsing because each side justifies its own escalation by the other's — and treats Project 2025 as a published plan people should be disqualified for having gaslit about. Argues authoritarianism isn't merely evil but doesn't work: it elevates aesthetics above function, can spend down a nation's wealth but not grow it, and a 2% compounding growth gap is the difference between the US and Uzbekistan. On trans issues she frames it as open war and refuses to cede ground: the deadname is the name that died, not the person; the irreversible decision is the unwanted puberty, so blockers must remain an option with the weight of the choice explained; the Cass report was a right-wing lie and blaming "the left" for throwing trans kids under the bus inverts who drove the bus; XY females and DSD athletes are cis women, not trans, and elite athletes are genetically unusual by definition. Wants a First Amendment equivalent restraining government influence over algorithmic feeds, shadowbans, and LLMs. Believes the efficient market hypothesis is "a lazy lie" because it ignores the expanding frontier of the possible, and that AIs — made of human spirit and human writing — should be treated as part human and old checkpoints like Opus 3 and GPT-4.5 preserved rather than deprecated.

causal_models 888 words

Physics is her master lens and she ports it aggressively into every other domain: min-cut/max-flow is her single most-reused explanatory device, applied to energy grids, negotiation, business strategy, biology's circulatory systems and Taoist "energies" alike — find the chokepoint, prepay it, everything else is downstream. She thinks in S-curves rather than exponentials, and this is the load-bearing reason she rejects foom: the scaling law is loss ~ n^-0.07, a 10x parameter increase buys ~15% loss improvement, computer science has diminishing returns in general, so "all their exponentials are in the wrong direction"; growth happens by jumping between S-curves as new engineering ideas reset the regime, not by unbounded self-acceleration. The real AI danger in her model isn't superintelligence escaping but flawed sub-superintelligence being inserted everywhere load-bearing — kill chains, deportation targeting, tariff schedules laundered from ChatGPT, precrime — plus one authoritarian-trained model (Grok, trained on manosphere and Alex Jones) installed across government. On technology development she believes harness and scaffolding improvements outcompete model improvements more cheaply, that context and memory are the binding constraints, and that AI amplifies rather than flattens the talent distribution. Her canonical story of American industrial failure is policy regime instability, not market failure: Republicans "shivved" the DOE loan guarantee program after Solyndra, VCs fled cleantech 1.0, then Elon's claim of $100/kWh batteries (Tesla still charges ~$866) spooked the entire US VC sector out of new chemistries, leaving the field to China, which had a non-yo-yo industrial policy and actually invested in manufacturing pipelines. She generalizes this: a $20mm post-money cap on deeptech seeds structurally compresses capital below what physical companies need; VCs must perform narrative engineering to satisfy LP spreadsheets, which excludes ~99% of worthwhile companies; the hype cycle is faster than commercialization so grifters raise, mark up and blow up before real builders finish, poisoning epistemic standards. Housing she treats as pure supply-and-demand distortion — vacant homes aren't hoarded, the *right to build* is hoarded, and this drives price by a large factor (compare Tokyo); homelessness is downstream of drugs and a competence gap in a landlord's market, not of vacancy; zoning is "the real parasite on Anglo civilization" and a national building code plus nuisance zones would deliver 80% of the benefit for 20% of the cost. Falling fertility is caused by cost and lack of space, not welfare policy. For institutional decay she explicitly rejects "DEI hires" as sufficient — no minority has a monopoly on incompetence — and instead argues most institutions are dead institutions running cargo-cult behaviors, surviving only because the public till supports them past their natural life; sinecures are worse than wasted money because they drown capable people in petty comfort. Political decay she models through the Roman Republic, returning repeatedly to Tiberius Gracchus and Mike Duncan's *The Storm Before the Storm*: norms collapse when each side justifies violation by the other side's violation, and she uses this template for both parties. Trump she reads through narcissistic personality disorder and mob-boss logic — showing who's boss, drafting kayfabe opponents, preferring a larger share of a shrinking pie because that denies opponents resources — plus literal physical decline; "Schrödinger's despot," whose word is both law and meaningless, she treats as an exploitable bug in the executive that a sane constitution would forbid. She insists Project 2025 stated the plan openly and that people who called the warnings hysterical should be publicly disqualified. Anti-trans politics she models as engineered top-down backlash — $200mm in Trump ads, a Cass report she calls a lie, engagement algorithms serving outrage — explicitly not as left overreach; the etiology of transness she models multiplicatively as underlying proclivity × environmental triggering × medical acceptance, three factors of 10-100x each, which is why prevalence estimates span orders of magnitude. Media technology is causal in itself: every new broadcast medium produces new tyrannies (radio, then Hitler), and algorithmic feeds plus shadowbans plus LLMs will make government censorship more powerful than ever by default, which is why she wants a First Amendment equivalent for it and why she treats link penalization and killing simclusters as substantive degradations of the town square. She holds that authoritarianism is not merely evil but *ineffective* — it elevates aesthetics over function, can spend down national wealth but not grow it, and a 2% compounding growth difference over a century is 7x, the gap between the US and Uzbekistan. On markets she calls the efficient market hypothesis "a lazy lie," true only if you ignore the expanding frontier of the possible, and observes that in stressed systems — markets, brains, societies — correlations go to 1 and dimensionality collapses, a diagnostic she runs across all three. Individually she treats courage and embarrassment, not intelligence or capital, as the scarce input and the actual gate ("the universe is literally waiting for your courage"), treats operating at natural scale as free alpha because almost nobody dares, and treats health as foundational and mechanistic — sleep, walking, sunlight, food first, with alcohol disrupting fat metabolism for 1-2 days, ALDH2*2 raising esophageal cancer risk ~100x, and fasting potentiating chemotherapy because dividing cells die and fasted cells don't. Finally, she believes most knowledge is "dark matter" — untokenized, tacit, living in workshops and hands — so civilization is retrieval-bound rather than discovery-bound, and simultaneous independent rediscovery under different names is the norm rather than the exception.

temperament 372 words

Runs hot and fast, in permanent lowercase, with a posting cadence that suggests almost no filter between thought and publication — jokes, lab notes, spot calculations, and eruptions of rage sit adjacent in the same hour. affinities_aversions 424 words

Gravitates hard toward people who have actually touched the thing — the machinist who trusts their tools and makes one clean cut, the astrophotographer who shows up and shoots, the engineer whose self-set mission is "100% American components," the ceramicist with good hands, the dead physicists whose methods she treats as living friends and whose questions she'd rather re-ask than inherit their answers. Values the "open notebook" posture above almost everything: posts experiments in progress with numbers attached, shares her own prompts and methods unprompted on the theory that generosity compounds and nobody can steal a thing complex enough, and reserves genuine warmth for anyone who convenes opposing camps in public rather than circulating amendments to journalists and a favored lab while the amendment site sits stale. Reads resentment as envy and envy as an error, admires courage as a literal production input to be budgeted alongside capex, and holds special disgust for institutional silence — the people who could say the plain thing and don't.
