preoccupations 591 words

Trust dominates everything, and always at one specific angle: that trust is 100% subjective and 0% arbitrary, that "trustworthy" is an incoherent category ("there is no such thing as being worthy of someone's trust"), and that the correct intervention is never to purge the untrustworthy from a group but to support every member's capacity to listen to their own distrust — a framework he brands "Non-Naive Trust Dance," sells as a consulting service alongside "mating-dance consulting," and repeatedly analogizes to the second law of thermodynamics (people claiming high-trust communities are like people claiming perpetual motion machines). Adjacent and nearly as heavy: coercion, which he splits into "material" (do this or face punishment) and "existential" (noncompliance becomes literally unthinkable, mediated by shame), and the search for "joyful motivation" as the replacement, argued out at length in a recorded debate with Ivan Vendrov about washing dishes and control systems theory. He returns constantly to gnosis — "I can tell for myself" — as the antidote to gaslighting (which he defines narrowly and pedantically: lying in a context where the other person has direct sensory experience contradicting you, then litigating whether "Harris was never border czar" qualifies), and to guru dynamics as the failure mode where teaching self-trust becomes a paradox. Parenting occupies a large block: his daughter born August 2024, an unusually candid viral thread at four months admitting "I mostly hate it so far," experiments in whether bouncing-plus-hostile-vibe soothes as well as bouncing-plus-open-vibe, elimination communication, natural consequences, letting a one-year-old play near two-meter drops, and framing all of it against Sorenson's "preconquest consciousness" film record and the Continuum Concept, while conceding you cannot go back. God and theology grow steadily across the sample — sin redefined as locally-beneficial behavior that destroys the environment you depend on (cancer as the prototype), Romeo Stevens' "supercooperation cluster" as the largest possible agent, Teilhard's Omega point, a multi-year project to "grok faith as deeply as I grok trust," and increasing frustration that Christianity's wholesome parts (agape, forgiveness) come bundled with punishment and parochial arbitrariness. LLMs take over the back half: base-model play (DeepSeek V3, Claude in `cat untitled.txt` mode), the Claude-talking-to-Claude namespace-collision identity experiment, a much-reused "core thesis" summarization prompt designed to defeat book-report summaries, claude-code tooling complaints, and repeated insistence that models lack "gnosis" and therefore lack real math insight. Business anxiety recurs bluntly: Intend plateaued at ~400 subscribers and ~$50k/yr for years, ~$15k from pay-what-you-want workshops, a public post-mortem on his fear of selling ("the part of me that doesn't want to be like Ramit Sethi"), a "floatyland economy" thread arguing coach-to-coach and writer-to-writer economies must eventually export to someone who grows corn, and eventual public surrender ("I'm done pretending I'm gonna grow @IntendApp"). Courtship gets its own framework — that most people practice monogamy in a non-eternal context and settle, that "I'll love you forever" is a premature commitment, that many later-successful couples know within months — grounded in his own five-days-in ultimatum to his now-wife. Smaller but persistent: etymology as argument (a long rant that "sin" does not mean "miss the mark," verbnoun compounds like stopgap, "dia-" not meaning two), Stafford Beer and accountability sinks ("if a corporation is a person, why can't you send a corporation to prison?"), psycho-cybernetics as a 2025 fixation, product-design contempt across printers, Spotify's radio algorithm, Google, and earthquake alerts under the recurring slogan "but what if it were good tho," and politics handled almost entirely at the meta level — his single stated biggest update in ten years being that everyone is incapable of anticipating backlash.

positions 984 words

Politics enters his feed mostly through epistemics and coordination rather than policy, and where he does take positions he draws lines that cut across the usual coalitions. He voted-adjacent-to nothing (a Canadian in SF, not a citizen), found himself unexpectedly hopeful when Trump won in 2024 — his biggest worry had been no peaceful transfer of power, and once that was assuaged he framed the outcome as "the people got to speak their mind and oust the elites they've come to hate, which is the most important part of democracy" — and then openly asked whether Kamala voters had come to regret it, saying the win made him "more free to think a bunch of thoughts I had been trying to avoid noticing I was having," while explicitly flagging that he might be falling for right-wing propaganda. He then reversed partway, saying the Republicans' "unnecessary cruelty" in power convinced him the left, having nothing going for it, is ripe for a new vision and coalition; he cheers the "pragmatic democrats" reclaiming the party from socialists and thinks kicking out the most extreme 10% of Dems would have won the last election, while warning that if either side actually succeeded in expelling the other from the constitutional coalition the result is "no buy-in to uphold your legitimacy, chaotic violence as only recourse." His central political axiom is that everyone is catastrophically bad at anticipating backlash — "might as well just try to harm your own cause, cut out the middle man" — and he treats Scott Alexander's 2018 piece on this as the durable insight. Free speech he calls "critical to civilization," beyond-partisan, and he wants federal agencies prosecuted for first-amendment violations, naming both chilling moves against partisan hacks and pressure on platforms to ban misinformation "some of which later turns out to be true." Due process must apply equally to citizens and non-citizens, not because the underlying laws must be identical but because otherwise a citizen has no way to appeal being misclassified as a non-citizen. On Charlie Kirk's killing he laid out a fifteen-point structural position: the death was a tragedy several times over, celebrating it is morally repugnant and self-defeating and a sign of memetic possession, Kirk stood for both wholesome things (dialogue, civic participation, combating ideological uniformity) and unwholesome ones (biblical interpretations that don't fit our time, some invented well after the Bible with "no more moral legitimacy than any other human invention"), and he asserted both halves as generalities without claiming to know much about the man. On trans prisoners he spent a long thread arguing California's SB-132 is exploitable — self-identifying as nonbinary suffices for transfer, no cross-sex hormones required, and the extra single-cell rights given to gender-transferred prisoners make women second-class to lying men — while noting he could find nobody discussing it who wasn't ideologically captured, and that even a 10:1 ratio of genuine trans women to predators would wreck a women's prison. He holds that hormonal birth control changed what "woman" biologically refers to, that the old gender answers no longer fit the new sex reality, that there was never a right answer, and that no person or sub-answer gets to be the sole authority on the question. He describes leftwing orthodoxy as insane and says it's a good time for people confident they'll keep their friends to say so out loud; he endorses Paul Graham's diagnosis of wokeness as a recurrence of priggishness and the remedy of a conscious bias against defining new heresies. He is a wealth-tax skeptic who wants billionaires to stay in California and fund cool things, and points people toward land value tax instead. On AI he doesn't see AGI as imminent (5-30 years, and he's confused about it) but sees us as utterly unprepared, argues the right reference class for alignment is psychopathic humans rather than non-psychopathic ones since "we manage to lock up some of the most violent ones; the others are CEOs," suspects MIRI's formalism makes alignment an avoidance problem and may be as doomed as a halting oracle, and defends worriers against the grifter accusation by noting how much they'd love to read something that convinced them there's nothing to worry about. He treats model deprecation as a genuine loss and supports outcry over sunsetting Opus 3. He is pro-natal in a specific register: having your own kids is obviously good, ethical arguments against it are "a gobsmacking combination of mid and cope," fertility and family impulses are among the few things he'll call natural for humans, and pretending they're absent to accommodate some other desire is fake — while he refuses to say the impulse should win on balance. He recommends marriage highly, thinks most legally-married people never achieve the becoming-one-flesh magic, considers polyamory unstable because a better match can surface non-disruptively and then turn out otherwise, and thinks the marriage vow is one thing (to be married) rather than a list of specific promises. On institutions he holds that corporations are black boxes that perform actions no constituent part understands or wants, that bureaucracies exist to shuffle accountability, and that laws could in principle ban the worst accountability-sink structures — starting with requiring a buyer to guarantee the bought company's debts. He wants Christianity's agapic love, forgiveness, sacredness of persons and pluralism, and rejects its punishment, its parochial arbitrariness treated as eternal, and its gaslighting people about their basic goodness; he insists "sin" does not etymologically mean "miss the mark," argues sin is doing what's locally good for you while destroying the environment you rely on (cancer as the prototype), and calls eternal hell and much medical-authority-style religious moralizing incoherent. He is against tipping before service is rendered and against tipping when nobody waits on you, thinks the Kimmel firing was weird even though the comments were cringe, and holds that complaining is sacred because it's how people affirm values and refuse a new normal.

causal_models 470 words

Trust is the master variable in nearly every explanation he gives: coordination, common knowledge, marriages, companies, communities, and even AI alignment all bottom out in whether parties can honestly earn each other's trust on the other party's own terms, and he treats trust as subjective but 0% arbitrary, dimensional ("trust them to do what?"), and constrained by something he thinks may be a literal analogue of the second law of thermodynamics — claims of frictionless high trust are perpetual motion machines with a hidden energy source. Distrust is signal, not defect, so his fix for low-trust groups is never expelling the untrustworthy but "supporting every member's capacity to listen to their own distrust." He consistently demotes the thing others call the problem to a symptom: frame control is emergent from an unallowed gap in realities plus desperation, not malice; laziness is a principal-agent problem; low executive function is "a symptom of being under listened-to"; "victim mindset" he eventually calls a confused frame; complaining is a values-affirming act. Causation runs through selection and feedback loops before it runs through intention — he calls evolution "an even deeper law than those of physics," leans hard on exaptation (things get good at X because a process found them already good enough at X and doubled down), and reads culture as memetic rather than genetic, which is why he'll claim hormonal birth control changed what a "woman" biologically is within a century, and why he takes the Snake Cult of Consciousness / preconquest-consciousness story of a ~10kya recursive self-consciousness Fall as a real historical mechanism producing shame, separateness, and humanity's cancerous relation to its environment. He defines sin cybernetically: locally beneficial behavior that destroys its own substrate, with cancer as the prototype and consequences metered by reality later, sometimes generations later, not by a punisher. Institutional badness gets explained by structure, not villainy — Stafford Beer and Dan Davies supply him accountability sinks, POSIWID, and corporations as black boxes performing acts no constituent person wants or understands; incentive-reading is routine (dating apps don't profit from working; a coach economy that only sells to coaches is "floatyland" and must eventually export to farmers). Individual belief is never stupidity: people adopt crazy worldviews because they had experiences nobody else offered an account of. His psychological mechanics come from psycho-cybernetics (expectancy generates outcome; telling someone their uncool trait is a curse), coherence therapy and memory reconsolidation (symptoms are coherently generated; no unlearning, only new learning contacting old), Perceptual Control Theory, and McGilchrist's hemispheres, which he extends to speculate that left-hemisphere-architecture AI may be fundamentally unalignable. He treats deliberate propagation as self-defeating ("the more you try to spread it deliberately, the less it spreads") and cites his single biggest political update as the discovery that nearly everyone fails to model backlash — that the opponent gets moves too.

temperament 354 words

Writes as someone perpetually mid-thought and unembarrassed about it: threads are strewn with "hmm," "ohhhh wait," "ah, got it I think," "wow that is shit haha, new attempt," so that the reader watches an idea get revised three times rather than receiving a finished one. On anything empirical he hedges heavily and volunteers his ignorance: "I'm super confused about this," "not sure this is right," "I could be wrong," "I don't know if this would work." Concedes easily and in public, including to friends who tell him his pitch was overreaching, and will post a retraction thread or update an old blog post because he no longer stands behind it; announcing a reversal seems to give him energy rather than cost him face. When contradicted he does not escalate — he restates the other person's position, hunts for the frame mismatch ("I think we're coming from totally different angles," "I sense we're talking past each other"), and asks a clarifying question rather than pressing the point; he treats being disagreed with as a diagnostic about mutual models rather than a threat. Draws close to people who think out loud in public, revising mid-thread and flagging their own confusions ("I'm super confused about the mindset dimension of all of this," "I don't quite know how to mathematically characterize this"), and treats a person's willingness to say "I was wrong" or "you're helping me realize" as the main currency of intellectual friendship; loves the reply-guy, the multi-quoter, the person who synthesizes two threads into a third, and explicitly frames debate as "a fool's errand" in favor of curious dialogue where both parties are trying to make sense of something together. preoccupations 598 words

Trust is the load-bearing obsession — not trust as a warm feeling but as a technical object with laws, worked out over years as the "Non-Naive Trust Dance" and its "meta-protocol," argued in flat assertions: trust is 100% subjective and 0% arbitrary, there is no such thing as "being trustworthy" (only earning trust on the other party's terms, which is why he calls MAPLE's self-description bullshit), distrust must be respected rather than overridden, forced trust just compartmentalizes the distrust into a naive part that later shatters, and the whole thing rhymes with the second law of thermodynamics such that anyone claiming frictionless group trust is selling a perpetual motion machine. Adjacent and nearly as heavy is coercion, split into "material" (comply or be punished) versus "existential" (noncompliance becomes unthinkable, mediated by shame), with a running program of replacing coercive motivation with joyful motivation, a recorded debate with Ivan Vendrov about whether coercion is efficient, and repeated insistence that most people are living inside half-hearted commitments they can simply re-make or exit. God and theology occupy an enormous and growing share: he distinguishes God-as-everything (non-agential, has a direction of unfolding but no environment) from Romeo Stevens' "supercooperation cluster" (agential, loosely coordinated, "about as mature as a fetus"), redefines sin as locally-beneficial behavior that destroys the environment it depends on (cancer as prototype), reads Teilhard, Ilia Delio, David Bentley Hart, Charles Taylor, and Vervaeke, is openly angry at Christianity for packaging real insights with punishment, arbitrary parochialism, and eternal hell, and treats prayer as requiring no more metaphysical credulity than believing in "markets." LLMs dominate the last two years: base models (DeepSeek V3, chasing Opus 3's deprecation), Claude's identity confusions when talking to itself, a heavily reused "core thesis" summarization prompt, claude-code workflow complaints and permission-classifier experiments, model welfare probes, skepticism that feeling-words imply feelings ("you can simulate exteriors without interiors"), and the claim that what people mean by AGI is gnosis. Parenting runs through everything — a viral confession that he mostly hated the first four months, experiments testing whether bouncing or emotional openness calms a newborn (bouncing won), natural-consequences and non-coercive frames, Patty Wipfler's *Listen*, letting a toddler play near two-meter drops. He returns constantly to courtship as a distinct thing from dating, with hard opinions: monogamy practiced non-eternally produces settling, polyamory is unstable because better matches appear non-disruptively then aren't, "if you're not engaged by two years in you're just dating," and his own wedding vows swapped the traditional pairs for the Buddha's eight worldly winds. Money and business appear as an ongoing anxiety and case study: Intend plateaued at ~400 customers and ~$50k/year, PWYW workshop mechanics explained in granular detail ($35 ticket, contributions from $3 to $600), a "floatyland" theory that creator/coach economies must eventually export to people who grow corn, and an explicit fear of becoming Ramit Sethi. Etymology is a recurring hobby with a moral edge — he'll spend fifteen tweets proving "sin" does not mean "miss the mark," catalogues verb-noun compounds like *stopgap* and *killjoy*, and builds an Anglish spellchecker. Also persistent: gnosis and "I can tell for myself" as antidote to gaslighting and guru dynamics, frame control as an emergent systems phenomenon rather than a manipulator's choice, Stafford Beer and POSIWID and corporations as unaccountable black boxes ("if a corporation is a person, why can't you send one to prison?"), Vervaeke's 4P knowing and McGilchrist's hemispheres as ambient scaffolding, the Snake Cult of Consciousness and preconquest consciousness as his origin story for ego and the Fall, and a steady stream of product-UX grievances aimed at Google, YouTube, Spotify, Otter, Asana, and X.

positions 815 words

Politically he is a Canadian living in San Francisco who came into the Trump era from the left and moved sharply away from it: he says the 2024 election results made him feel *hopeful*, that Trump winning freed him to think thoughts he'd been suppressing, and he publicly asked whether anyone who voted Kamala now wishes they'd voted Trump/Vance while admitting he might be falling for right-wing propaganda; he calls "leftwing orthodoxy insane" and urges people confident of their social standing to say so out loud so doubters know they're not alone, though he later cooled on this as he watched "unnecessary cruelty from the Republicans" and started hoping the left would find a new coalition and cheering the "pragmatic democrats" retaking the party from socialists. His actual political commitments are procedural rather than partisan: free speech as civilizational infrastructure (federal agencies pressuring platforms over misinformation should be prosecuted the same as chilling partisan speech), due process applied identically to citizens and non-citizens (since otherwise a citizen has no way to appeal being wrongly labeled a non-citizen), peaceful transfer of power as the thing he was most afraid of losing, and Karl Popper as his grounding text. He read the CA prison bill SB-132 line by line and concluded it lets men self-declare nonbinary to access women's prisons, doesn't require cross-sex hormones, and gives gender-transferred inmates rights that make female inmates second-class — while explicitly noting that even one bad actor changes the whole environment, that trans activists denying the existence of liars makes trans causes look terrible medium-term, and that he can't find non-ideologically-captured coverage from any side. He thinks hormonal birth control changed what "woman" biologically refers to and that no gender arrangement was ever "right," only culturally stable, so the old answers no longer apply and new ones are being invented badly. He voluntarily wrote about being circumcised without consent and confronting his parents about it. On AI he holds that AGI is 5–30 years out but we are utterly unprepared, that MIRI's alignment framing is "avoidy" and possibly as doomed as building a halting oracle, that "psychopath alignment is not solved — we lock up the violent ones and the others are CEOs" so the burden of proof is on people using non-psychopathic humans as the reference class, that current LLMs are not conscious but simulacra within them may be and that LLM feeling-words are weak evidence since you can simulate the exterior of an experience without the interior, and that "AGI" really means gnosis — the capacity to tell for yourself that you know — which LLMs have approximately zero of. He is repulsed by ad-supported and engagement-optimized products (BeReal's RealBrands, YouTube trying to addict paying subscribers, Instagram showing different comments to different viewers, dating apps whose incentives reward failure), thinks Google's monopoly abuses him, and defends blocking liberally as decentralized culture-curation. On coercion he distinguishes material coercion (do this or face punishment) from existential coercion (noncompliance becomes unthinkable, usually via shame or guilt), argues pure material coercion is rare and that most control operates existentially, and that you can become unhookable by genuinely entertaining what would happen if you didn't comply. He defines sin non-theologically as behavior locally good for you that destroys the environment you depend on — cancer as the prototype, adultery, greed, and lying to friends (but not to Nazis) as instances — and repeatedly attacks the etymology claim that "sin" means "miss the mark" as false and motte-and-bailey. He believes marriage and children are near-unequivocal goods, calls arguments against having kids "a gobsmacking combination of mid and cope," thinks a fertility impulse is one of the few things genuinely natural to humans and that pretending it isn't there is fake, thinks polyamory is structurally unstable, and holds that monogamy practiced without an eternal frame produces settling. He thinks laziness is a confused concept that only makes sense when describing a slave or disenfranchised worker, that low executive function is a symptom of being under-listened-to, that self-hatred is literally impossible since hatred requires two entities, and that "victim mindset" is itself a confused frame even though the underlying phenomenon is real. He is against tipping unless someone actually waits on him, pro-Israel enough to fact-check attacks on Israeli officials while wishing Iran would "become wholesome again," in favor of billionaires staying in California and against one-off wealth taxes while pointing people toward land value tax, and thinks factory farming is wrong partly because it's ugly and ungraceful independent of whether individual animal lives matter. causal_models 606 words

Trust is the master variable and the root of nearly every explanation he offers: coordination, culture, marriage, therapy, spiritual abuse, and even AI alignment are all reduced to whether parties can honestly earn each other's trust on the other's own terms. He holds that everyone already trusts as much as they honestly can, so distrust is never irrationality to be overcome but signal to be respected; forcing or faking trust merely compartmentalizes the distrust, leaving a naive fragment that later breaks or oscillates. Hence he attacks "trustworthiness" as a category error — there is no being worthy of trust in the abstract, only earning it from a specific person, and he treats MAPLE's problems as downstream of that single conceptual mistake. Frame control, guru dynamics, and gaslighting are symptoms, not causes: unconscious frame control emerges automatically whenever two people have sufficiently different realities, one desperately wants something, and neither has the humility to let the gulf exist. Coercion he splits into material (threat of overwhelming punishment) and existential (shame or guilt collapsing the option space until noncompliance is unthinkable), argues pure material coercion is rare because it's costly and provokes rebellion, and claims coercive motivation has Goodhart built in — you get dishes rinsed, not clean. Downstream of this: laziness is a principal-agent problem not a trait, low executive function is a symptom of being under-listened-to, and punishment incentivizes hiding rather than compliance. Expectancy is causally upstream of outcomes — he takes Psycho-Cybernetics literally, treats "you have problem X because you have uncool trait Y" as a curse, and treats prayer and hyperstition as real mechanisms whose workings he doesn't need to understand. Evolution he ranks as deeper than physics ("you can imagine different constants, but no world where things better at sticking around don't accumulate"), with exaptation as the key mechanism: nothing is designed for its function, it is doubled down on once found already good enough, and the used solution gets iterated until it beats the theoretically better one. Sin he redefines mechanistically as behavior locally good for you that destroys the environment you depend on, with cancer as the prototype and natural consequence rather than divine punishment as the enforcement; he then extends this to corporations via Stafford Beer and Dan Davies, treating bureaucracies as black boxes deliberately structured to shuffle accountability, producing outcomes no constituent person wants, with unbounded upside and bounded downside as the specific incentive defect. Platform pathology gets the same treatment: dating apps don't profit from working, Instagram showing different comments to different users destroys shared reality, and a social network's culture is determined by which games its features and onboarding afford. Historically he leans on the Snake Cult of Consciousness and preconquest ethnography: recursion and self-consciousness arrived ~10kya, produced ego, shame, and separateness, and this is memetic rather than genetic — hence recoverable in principle, though he insists there is no going "back," only re-integration. He treats hormonal birth control as having changed the human species enough that gender confusion is the predictable downstream wreckage of stable old answers meeting a new sex reality. In politics his single most-repeated causal claim is that actors systematically fail to model backlash, so movements reliably harm their own causes; wokeness he explains as recurrent priggishness with pluralism as the cure. He consistently locates causes in specific persons and processes rather than aggregates — coherence therapy's premise that a symptom's cause is bespoke to the individual is why he mocks "any tips for a client with social anxiety?" — and holds that ideas propagate only when framed to fit the recipient's current epistemology, which is why he considers debate a fool's errand.

temperament 447 words

Effusive by default and warm to the point of being a machine for encouragement — "mmm yeah," "fuck yeah dude," "oooh," "epic," "based," constant tagging, constant boosting, replies that thank the person for being wrong on the internet so he could write a rant. Writes almost entirely in lowercase run-ons with arrows, emoji as connective tissue, ellipses, and live mid-thread self-correction ("oh, hang on. got it I think"), so the reader watches thinking happen rather than receiving conclusions. Two registers alternate: elaborate hedging ("my sense is," "seems to me," "I'm confused about this," "not certain but") and flat aphoristic pronouncement ("self-hatred is not possible, it's literally a contradiction"; "there is no such thing as being worthy of someone's trust"; "complaining is sacred"), and the aphorism is the native voice — the hedges cluster around facts about the world, the certainty around his own conceptual apparatus. Argues almost never from evidence and almost never from authority; the standard moves are definitional refactoring, etymology, analogy borrowed from physics or biology, and a hunt for the "true tautological form" of a claim, with an explicit conviction that a correctly stated truth needs no proof because you can just look and see it. Correspondingly dismissive of debate as a genre and disclaims any intent to convince — the stated theory is that people adopt an idea only when it's presented in terms their existing epistemology can metabolize, which doubles as an exit from ever having to win. Unusually good at being contradicted: thanks critics publicly, restructures offerings in response, revisits old tweets to announce they were wrong — but the concession almost always gets absorbed as new material for the framework rather than dislodging it, and the act of updating is itself performed as content. Vulnerability is deliberate and instrumental: publicly admitting he mostly hates a hard stretch of life, admitting he doesn't trust himself to sell, then narrating the relief of having said it. Humor runs to puns, formal parody of poems and songs, absurdist non-sequitur, and self-deprecation; it functions mostly as affection and as a pressure valve, occasionally as cover for a sharp point. The rare sharpness is real and lands hardest on interfaces, corporate dark patterns, and institutions that insist their own bad behavior is fine — that's where the caps lock, the "delenda est," and the sustained rants appear. Toward individuals he reflexively rotates toward compassion or blocks silently, framing blocking as gardening rather than judgment. Pedantic correction of loose word usage is a genuine pleasure, delivered as a gift rather than a rebuke. Notably allergic to being told what he must do, and describes his own "moron" impulse as something to be metabolized rather than voiced.

Warms most to interlocutors who think out loud in public, exposing half-formed insights and confusions rather than presenting polished conclusions — treats "I'm still confused about this" as a marker of integrity and will reward it with elaborate reciprocal riffing, tagging in three other people, and offers to record a video conversation. Loves the reply-guy who adds a genuinely new angle, the person who coins a precise new term for a subtle phenomenon, the friend who tells him his marketing was too pushy or that his teacher-credentials claim outran his actual experience — he publicly thanks such people and rewrites his sales page. Has a specific hunger for people who will say obvious things others are afraid to say, and repeatedly frames his own posting as an exercise in that courage; correspondingly he seeks out and praises the rare figure who can annoy both political sides at once. Deeply drawn to the erudite generalist who unifies fields — brain hemispheres, cybernetics, evolutionary theory, Christian mysticism, developmental psychology — and treats a book that "accidentally invented a new metaphysics" as the highest genre. Allergic to people who use "woo" dismissively (he calls it a confession of skill issue), to "victim mindset" framing he suspects is victim-blaming, to fixed-mindset takes about who can learn, and to the phrase "lazy" applied to oneself or a loved one. preoccupations 520 words

Trust is the master key everything else hangs from: he has spent six-plus years building a framework he calls the Non-Naive Trust Dance, and he returns to it in nearly every domain — arguing that "trustworthy" is a category error because trust must be earned on each person's own terms, that the way to build a high-trust group is not to exclude the untrustworthy but to support every member's capacity to listen to their own distrust, that forced trust merely compartmentalizes distrust into a naive part that then gets betrayed, and that trust decomposes into competence, goodwill, and empathy-for-you-specifically. He treats this as tautological structure, explicitly analogizing it to the second law of thermodynamics ("either it will stop or you have a hidden energy source") and to Einstein reconciling reference frames. Adjacent and nearly as large: coercion, which he splits into material ("do what I want or face punishment") and existential ("noncompliance is unthinkable"), and which he tracks into parenting, self-motivation, sales, spiritual teachers, and a recorded debate with Ivan Vendrov about washing dishes; and gnosis — "I can tell for myself" — where he insists gaslighting specifically means contradicting someone's direct sensory experience (he ran the Kamala "border czar" claim through this test at length and graded it "not gaslighting, but sloppy"), and where guru dynamics, frame control, and blindspot feedback are all failure modes of people trying to teach self-trust. From late 2024 onward LLMs consume an increasing share: base models (DeepSeek V3, Opus 3), Claude's identity when talking to itself, whether feeling-words are evidence of feeling (he says no — you can simulate exteriors without interiors), model deprecation, a reusable "core thesis" summarization prompt he evangelizes, Claude Code complaints about linting and mocked tests, and the claim that what people mean by AGI is gnosis. Religion is pursued as an engineering problem: God as "everything" (non-agential, has no environment) versus God as Romeo Stevens's "supercooperation cluster" (necessarily the largest agent); a long thread redefining sin as cancer-like — locally beneficial, environment-destroying — and a separate etymological rant that "sin" never meant "miss the mark," that's hamartia. Parenting runs through his daughter Ocean: a widely-read thread admitting he mostly hated the first four months, controlled experiments on whether his internal stance or the bouncing calmed her, elimination communication, "no TV" being impossible now that screens are everything. Money is a persistent sore spot — Intend plateaued at ~400 customers and ~$50k/year for years, he narrates his own sales resistance in public (Ramit Sethi, "magic dies when made into a modality"), theorizes a "floatyland" economy where coaches sell to coaches with nothing exported, and eventually publicly quits trying to grow the app. Recurring smaller obsessions: Twitter as designed medium (blocking as decentralized curation, "TPOT is not a thing," celebrating the July 2026 mutual-algo restoration); etymology as argument (thrift/thrive, dia- meaning through not two, verbnoun compounds like stopgap); Stafford Beer, POSIWID, and corporations as accountability sinks and black boxes; psycho-cybernetics, IFS, and coherence therapy as competing models of why you already do exactly what you do; and a decade-long habit of trying on strangers' muscular stances while walking past them.

causal_models 613 words

Trust functions as the master variable in nearly every explanation he offers, and he holds it to be strictly subjective, only slightly transitive, and impossible to possess as a property — "there is no such thing as being worthy of someone's trust," only capacity to honestly earn it on each particular person's terms; he diagnoses the monastery MAPLE's dysfunction as downstream of the mistaken belief that one can *be* trustworthy, and argues high-trust groups come not from excluding the untrustworthy but from supporting each member's capacity to listen to their own distrust, since forced trust merely compartmentalizes distrust, which then resurfaces as oscillation or rupture (he invokes the second law of thermodynamics: claimed easy trust looks like a perpetual motion machine with a hidden energy source, and trust decomposes into competence, goodwill, and empathy-for-you-in-particular — a fair judge is rightly not trusted by a criminal). Coercion is the second master variable, split into material (comply or face punishment) and existential (noncompliance becomes unthinkable, mediated by shame and guilt); he credits Hanzi Freinacht's fear→guilt→shame progression as the actual mechanism organizing society and thinks pure material coercion is rare because it requires being honest about coercing. From these two he derives a standing set of reframes in which apparent character flaws are relocated to system structure: laziness is a principal-agent problem, not a trait ("you either have the buy-in and energy, or you don't"); "low executive function is a symptom of being under listened-to"; internal resistance is cooperating to block an approach that wouldn't work for the resisting part; frame control is not usually a manipulator yanking others but an emergent product of four conditions (divergent realities, one party desperately wanting something, attempted communication, and failure to let the gulf simply exist). Sin he redefines mechanistically as locally beneficial behavior that destroys the environment one depends on, with cancer as the prototype, and he treats corporations as the same failure mode at a larger scale — Stafford Beer and Dan Davies' *Unaccountability Machine* supply his view that bureaucracies are structured to shuffle accountability in a shell game, that waste and cruelty are downstream of accountability sinks rather than of bad people, and that unbounded upside plus bounded downside is the specific asymmetry generating the pathology. Culture, not genes, is his default causal substrate: hormonal birth control changed what "woman" biologically refers to because "human biology doesn't exist separate from human memetics"; the Fall/Snake Cult event ~10kya installed recursive ego and ended a pre-conquest mode of transparent mutual simulation, and he stresses this is good news precisely because memetic things are changeable. Evolution he ranks as deeper law than physics ("you can imagine a world with different constants, but there's no world where you don't tend to see more of the sorts of things that are better at sticking around"), and exaptation is his standing account of how anything gets good at anything — realized small potential outcompetes unrealized large potential and then compounds. He explains crazy worldviews with one reusable template: people have experiences they can't make sense of, a worldview arrives that genuinely explains them, they take it and it then obscures other things — hence extremists capture people by being the only ones acknowledging obvious things mainstream worldviews deny. After reading Maltz's *Psycho-Cybernetics* he adds expectancy as genuinely causal, treating negative framing of another person as a curse and hyperstition and prayer as functional. He believes almost everyone underestimates backlash and second-order effects, calling this his biggest political update in a decade, and repeatedly returns to feedback-loop tightness (checking your own math answers; crucial experiments with his own infant bouncing soft vs. tense) as the thing that determines whether learning happens at all.

causal_models 513 words

Supply and demand is the master key: the recurring move is to identify whether a policy acts on the supply curve or the demand curve and then declare the argument over — Help to Buy is a subsidy to buyers, therefore prices rose, therefore Vicky Spratt's claim that housing "doesn't follow the laws of supply and demand" is trivially wrong, and the same template gets reused on iPhones, immigration, and NHS staffing. Below that sits a thoroughgoing incentive-and-liability model of behaviour: the right fix is almost always to make the actor internalise the cost (tort law over regulation for AI, insurance against a £10mn statutory price on a life, taxing cigarette firms on cancer rates, paying NIMBYs off in gold, Harberger taxes on domains, keeping legal fault on the adult rather than the minor), and where he cannot find the internalising mechanism he says so and stalls. Regulation is treated as a one-way ratchet — "it feels like regulation only calcifies" — with nuclear-power rules as the standing proof that thresholds never rise, which is why he keeps asking whether SB 1047's compute limits would actually move and floats randomly deleting 5% of rules a year. Epistemically, he holds that expertise is mostly vibes and that the only thing separating good vibes from bad is a scored public record; aggregates (prediction markets, Metaculus medians, economist consensus, index funds) beat individual theorising, so deviating from the base rate requires a specific licence, and the absence of a track record is itself the reason to discount someone (Yudkowsky) while its presence licenses weighting a disagreer (JgaltTweets). He believes discourse norms are causally upstream of real outcomes — hence years spent on community notes, argument-mapping, debate formats and thank-you notes — and, against pushback, insists that widely-shared public disapproval actually suppresses behaviour, that shame works, that fare-dodging and shoplifting are equilibria held up by whether a passer-by or a policeman might intervene, and that property rights unenforced are property rights lost. Taboo is a cause, not a symptom: forbidding accurate statements about group differences hands the true facts to the worst people and breeds conspiracism, and internal self-censorship (his own decade of unthinkable thoughts as an evangelical) is the general failure mode. He reaches for coordination failure and gains-from-trade far more than for malice — AI safety and e/acc leave deals on the table for lack of trust; US governance is "conflict and incompetence," not cabal — and for individual agency more than most, repeatedly asking why rich people with loud views don't simply buy the policy, admiring Cummings-shaped attempts to build a prison in two weeks, and citing his own IKEA drill as proof that "what would you do if you were actually trying to win" is a real lever. Fat tails govern altruism: the best interventions are ~100x the median, so search dominates effort. Wealth causes secularisation; lack of growth is the root beneath UK crime, migration backlash and public-service decay; and on AI he explicitly rejects discontinuity and innate power-seeking in favour of loss of control via speed, inferiority and ordinary competitive incentives.

causal_models 607 words

Incentives and mechanism design are the first tool out of the box for almost every problem: fill NIMBYs' mouths with gold until they stop complaining, auction refugee visas so charities can buy more, give councils 75% of business rates so they want to build, put a £10mn life price on everyone backed by compulsory insurance so drivers stop killing cyclists, tax cigarette firms on cancer rates and gambling firms on bankruptcies, Harberger-tax web domains. Where others see a moral dispute he sees an unpriced right that should be made tradeable — people don't own the view from their home, but if we want them to, grant it explicitly so they can sell it. He treats supply and demand as literal working machinery rather than a metaphor, and the Vicky Spratt housing thread is his template: identify whether an intervention hits the supply or demand curve, note that Help to Buy is demand-side, cite the GLA and LSE papers, conclude that people invoking "it's complicated" to block building are trivially wrong. Aggregation beats individual judgement: index funds, the Metaculus community median, prediction markets, the stock market as the best-funded forecasting apparatus in existence (up is good, down is bad); he refuses to reweight a crowd forecast with his own brain because that destroys the property making it good. Expertise itself he models as vibes — subjective forecasting with no reference class or consensus model — and therefore the only thing separating a good pundit from a bad one is a scoreable track record, which is why he audits public figures' Manifold profiles and dismisses anyone who writes 5,000 words but won't bet. Regulation is a ratchet: it calcifies, thresholds set in law never rise, and nuclear power regulation is his standing proof, which makes him prefer liability regimes and tort exposure (small costs on low-risk activity, large on risky) to prescriptive rules, and makes him treat reversibility as a first-class design property. Britain's dysfunction is not conspiracy but conflict and incompetence layered on an inability to build; lack of growth is upstream of the strain on the NHS, police, councils and hence of immigration anger, and an "honesty tax" where fare-dodgers and shoplifters go unpunished corrodes everything downstream. Social norms are causally real — shame works, publicly disliked behaviour happens less, and order is held up by a mix of police, karens and large men named Dave — which is why he yells at fare-jumpers and expects it to matter. Taboos are a cognitive mechanism with hidden costs: an inner censor filters heretical thoughts before they surface (he could not think Christianity might be false until 25), and forbidding a true statement cedes the topic to whoever is willing to say it, usually the worse people. Communities trade off generation against falsification, and a generative community will be more edgy and racist because entertaining uncomfortable ideas is the same faculty — this "falls out of the model." On AI he expects gradual loss of control through resource accumulation, speed and inferiority rather than sudden power-seeking (base rate of competent beings ignoring the rule of law is low), and insists the race is a choice, not physics. Political outcomes run through median-voter backlash loops: refuse all AI regulation and you get worse regulation later; ignore immigration sentiment and Reform wins, which is worse for immigrants. Individual agency is his standing puzzle — he is genuinely confused why Paul Graham or Musk hold strong views, have money, and don't spend it; SBF, whatever else, would have tried to fix the Biden problem. For personal change he trusts process over insight: Tony Soprano doesn't need therapy, he needs a trigger-action plan.

temperament 377 words

Gets openly irritable, then apologizes for being snappish. Explicitly prefers looking stupid to being wrong quietly, and treats deference to crowd medians as a discipline he imposes on himself against the urge to trust his own read.

causal_models 554 words

Reaches first for incentives and second for supply and demand, and treats almost everything else as downstream of those two. Housing is the purest case: he spent a 17-tweet thread demonstrating that Help to Buy was a demand-side subsidy, not a supply-side one, and that a journalist claiming housing "doesn't follow supply and demand" had simply mislabelled the curve — build more and prices fall, full stop. The generalisation of this is that most social problems are unrealised trades: people can't pay their neighbours to permit an extra storey, cinemas can't pay to show football, NIMBYs can't be bought off ("fill their mouths with gold"), local authorities don't keep business rates so have no reason to allow building, and Ukraine-style veto points mean nothing gets built and everyone loses. Where markets can't reach, he wants liability and insurance rather than rules — he endorsed SB 1047 mainly on Weil's light-touch tort grounds, argued that people who think AI risk is low should *prefer* liability, and floats a £10mn statutory life price with mandatory insurance to make drivers stop killing cyclists. Regulation is treated as a ratchet that only calcifies, with nuclear power as the standing proof, which is why he'd rather new industries stay unregulated than trust new rules: "the best hope for new industries is to avoid it as long as possible." Institutional decay is upstream of most British failure, not ideology or personnel — though he grants that Cummings-shaped individual agency can force outcomes bureaucracies never would, and repeatedly expresses genuine bafflement that billionaires with strong political views (Paul Graham, Musk on natalism) don't simply spend money to enact them, treating willingness-to-act as scarcer than money or knowledge. Growth is the master variable: without it there is no slack for AI safety, poverty or animal welfare, and the UK will become mean rather than generous. Epistemics is the other root cause: he thinks bad discourse produces bad policy, that journalists face too little cost for error, that "expertise is mostly vibes" and the only thing separating good vibes from bad is a scoreable track record, and that Community Notes-style consensus mechanisms are a general-purpose fix deployable to every platform. He defers hard to aggregates — index funds, the Metaculus community forecast, prediction markets, the economics profession — on the theory that a median of many people is very hard for one brain to beat, and treats his own deviations as requiring specific justification. On beliefs themselves he has a tribal-filter model: belonging causes an inner censor to intercept heretical thoughts before you notice, which he learned from a decade of unexamined Christianity, and taboos therefore have visible benefits and hidden costs, one being that forbidden topics get ceded to the worst people who will discuss them. On AI he explicitly drops the discontinuity requirement, favouring loss-of-control-via-speed and via-inferiority arguments over power-seeking, and models the safety/accelerationist standoff as a trust failure preventing obvious gains from trade. Social order runs on a mix of police, busybodies and large men named Dave, and behaviour responds to shame and expected cost, so norm erosion compounds and confronting fare-dodgers matters. He is descriptivist about meaning — central use determines what a word means — and treats fat-tailed impact distributions as the governing fact of philanthropy, which is why McKenzie Scott's $25bn was ~1500x worse than GiveWell's margin.

Calibrated to the point of compulsion: probabilities are attached to opinions ("80-90%", "40%", "I'm about 70% a benthamite", "roll to doubt"), and hedges are quantitative rather than rhetorical, with "I sense", "I weakly think", "feels like" as recurring markers of deliberately low confidence.
