History is his other engine: population genetics and the Upper Paleolithic (Yamnaya, WHG, ANE, Reich 2018), a 33-tweet survey of slavery across every civilization designed to conclude that Anglo abolition was the greatest gift in history, and a homegrown Albion's Seed-derived taxonomy of American subcultures — Yankee vs. Butternut, Puritan/Quaker merger, two separate Second Great Awakenings — which he uses to explain everything from the Kirk funeral to No Kings protests. He livetweets wars in OSINT-thread form (Israel–Iran above all, then Ukraine, India–Pakistan, Lebanon, Venezuela, the Houthis), reasoning explicitly about victory conditions, missile inventories, launcher attrition, and escalation ladders, and routinely feeds these questions to ChatGPT. LLMs are a constant subject in their own right: he authored and iterates on "eigenprompt," publishes his custom instructions and bio, and uses GPT as a lawyer, seismologist, economist, and reform-plan generator. Family life is the other half of the feed — three children under five, his 4yo's bits and mortality anxiety, sleep deprivation, marriage advice built on shared aesthetic and refusal to track fairness, and a treehouse and shed he is building badly. He returns often to sex differences via a Paglian framework, especially female homosocial norms as an illegible governing system, body counts, and what he sees as the maiming of male homosocial organization. He is preoccupied with religion as anthropology — the sacred/profane, Kegan stages, cultural Christianity arguments with Scott Alexander — while insisting he cannot honestly attend church because he won't misrepresent belief. Recurring smaller obsessions: travel is fake, tipping is a moral test, EA and utilitarianism (organ donation, "time man" thought experiments), IQ and regression to the mean, that Trump has a vulgar soul and is incompetent but that he prefers this to the alternative, that Nick Fuentes elicits physical disgust, and that antisemitism among young right-wingers has become normalized in a way boomers cannot perceive.

positions 773 words

A self-described ex-neoliberal and ex-technocrat who says liberals forced him into conservatism and that he "preferred BEING center left," he now holds that any complex policy will be immediately captured and exploited, so he prefers brutal, simple rules and describes himself as living in a zero-trust environment; he refuses to accept marginal reforms to laws (e.g., H1B) that are already flagrantly violated and unenforced. He believes the American Republic rests on Aristotelian *homonoia* — shared identity and sacred mutual obligation between citizens — and that mass immigration plus multiculturalism dissolves it; he rejects both the "wignat" and open-borders positions as denying that sacred obligation, and articulates a duty schema: equal duties to all citizens regardless of immigrant status, the reciprocal duties of *xenia* to noncitizen immigrants, and no special duties to potential immigrants. He is scathing toward the "creedal nation" formulation, demanding its proponents specify the creed, its enforcement, and whether apostates lose citizenship. He supports free speech absolutely for citizens and is willing to trade away speech protections for noncitizens or simply refuse them entry to preserve it; he says he does not care about noncitizen speech where it conflicts with citizen interests. On "ethnostate," he explicitly means a state organized around a shared cultural identity, not biological race, and grounds this in a self-taught account of 19th-century European nationalism, levée en masse, and postwar population transfers. His single greatest historical admiration is the Anglo world order's destruction of slavery — the 1807 acts in Congress and Parliament, the Royal Navy's suppression campaign, and the Union's war fought at ruinous cost for a non-belligerent party — which he calls "a gift to humanity procured by anglo power, anglo faith, and rivers of anglo blood," and he documents at length that slavery was near-universal (Islamic caliphates, Ottomans, Mongols, Cherokee slaveholding on the Trail of Tears, African export trade) to make the abolition singular. He is an avowed Anglo cultural supremacist who believes American institutions no longer work because the culture is no longer Anglo-dominant. He calls universities beyond reform — 95% slop, non-dischargeable debt, an ideological purge complete enough that no internal correction is possible — and argues accountability requires firing most academics, barring them from the field, and abandoning disciplines invented after 1700; he wants the schools destroyed as an object lesson, while regretting that he loved his own time in school. On the judiciary he initially dismissed right-wing complaints as working the refs, then concluded that criminal courts in Western countries have been ideologically captured, that Trump has escalation dominance over district judges, and that a legal system which treats foreign rapists with kid gloves will make its people want to hang the jurists too. He supported the war against the Islamic Republic on frankly pragmatic grounds (Iran ties up US assets, has killed thousands of Americans since 1979, and the moment was opportune after Hezbollah's gutting), treating regime removal as a secondary moral good, while calling Trump's threats against Denmark and Canada abominable and dishonorable, and promising to take up arms to keep Canada separate. He is bleak on fertility, sovereign debt, the collapse of Europe, and the rise of China, and expects a Third-Century-Crisis-style civilizational rupture that no faction is competent to prevent; his stated political preference is that the crisis be as mild as possible and resolve into a world where his children can thrive. He rates Trump as vulgar-souled, incapable of producing anything noble, and increasingly likely to be a figurehead over factional graft, but has never voted for him and instead writes in his own father. On EA and utilitarianism he holds that long-run consequences swamp measurable effects and can't be estimated, that A/B tested interventions are the drunk under the streetlight, and that the presumption of imposing one's preferences as objective reality is repellent; he campaigns against the organ donor checkbox as institutionalized lying. He thinks IQ is real and heritable but reference-population-dependent, mocks IQ truthers with the height analogy, and treats race-blind neutrality as a dead truce that too many people defected from to restore. He supports narcan-and-zip-ties for public opioid users, criminal strict liability for dog owners, deportation of noncitizens agitating for hostile foreign powers, and restricting the vote to married women as a joke that he keeps returning to. He regards religion expansively and non-epistemically — he can't honestly assert belief and therefore won't attend church, comparing God to consumer preferences in his epistemology while insisting he is more a mystic than his interlocutors — and argues cultural Christianity's revival requires elites to physically return to pews, fund churches instead of PACs, and live exemplary lives, which he doubts they will do.

causal_models 546 words

Selection effects are his first reach for almost any outcome: children still available for adoption have been filtered twice for catastrophe and heritable dysfunction, so badgering infertile couples to adopt is both unjust and predictably disastrous; foster survivorship concentrates the worst cases; a decade of shaming journalists and Democrats trained an opposition that is simply immune to social disapproval, which is why the Washington Post shaming Trump "won't show him at all." Second is endogeneity — his flagship charge against neoliberals is "pre-Lucas ass thinking," failing to see that ethnic conflict is caused by immigration policy rather than exogenous to it, and the same move recurs everywhere: the number of children smuggled in depends on whether taxpayers will school them; masks on ICE agents are a symptom of unenforced doxxing law, not a cause; recycling persists as civic ritual, not waste management. He treats incentives and institutional rules as load-bearing and names specific ones — the 1970s rules change that made filibusters costless, the Curley effect (posted "weekly"), the organ-donor box as a device to bypass next of kin, Humphrey's Executor, the GAO comptroller as the only party able to sue over impoundment, disparate-impact evidentiary asymmetry in Ames v. Ohio, Bob Jones retroactivity. But he no longer believes clever policy is implementable: he says explicitly he was a technocrat ten years ago and became "more brutal" because complex policy requires a high-trust system to execute, and any rule will now be immediately strip-mined by interested parties; hence a zero-trust posture and a preference for crude, unexploitable instruments. Culture is deep and slow and mostly upstream of politics — Fischer's colonial folkways (Yankee, Butternut, Borderer) still drive American religion and elections; Europe's postwar homogeneity was manufactured by a century of nationalist engineering and forced population transfer; the Franks took two millennia of church-and-sword to make; asabiyyah and Aristotelian *homonoia*, not law or bureaucracy, are what actually bind a republic, so multiethnic democracy dissolves the willingness to fund a welfare state. Biology sits under culture: heritability of IQ and height, regression toward a subpopulation mean rather than 100, assortative mating, women's politics as a function of hormonal state (hormonal contraception, pregnancy, parity), male versus female homosocial architecture — men rank, women subvert ordinality to preserve cohesion — which he deploys as a general-purpose explanation for the Sydney Sweeney ad war, the feminization of the Democratic Party's only weapon, and the interiority of an education essay. Elite behavior is causally privileged through mimicry: cultural Christianity died because elites bodily left their pews and their congregations copied them, and it could be revived by their returning, which they won't do. Ideology is downstream of psychological need (Hoffer, "Who Goes Nazi"), so a lone assassin's stated politics tells you nothing while his movement's refusal to disown him tells you everything. Epistemically he is an economist who thinks models are stories about an unobservable reality, that Campbell's law wrecked evidence the moment we started privileging it, that EA's A/B tests are the drunk under the streetlight, and that everyone is acting rationally — if someone looks irrational you have misidentified their objectives. Underneath all of it: path dependency, saeculum, ratchets and positive feedback loops, and the conviction that force, not law, ultimately settles things — "which is to be master, that's all."

temperament 440 words

Operates in two registers and switches between them deliberately: a lowercase, punctuation-light shitpost voice loaded with hedges — "idk," "afaict," "hard to say," "caveat emptor," "im just armchair speculating," "wild speculation though" — and a capitalized, clause-heavy essay voice reserved for the things he actually wants taken seriously, where he builds arguments in numbered points and closes with "fin" or "ty for coming to my sermon." The hedging is real rather than decorative; he explicitly disclaims certainty as a matter of principle, describing his epistemology as models layered on models with no total access to the territory, and says that even a direct theophany would only shift his odds. He narrates his own belief revisions out loud and often ("i am taking the L and i have updated my beliefs," "i would have agreed with this ten years ago," "id like to be wrong i guess"), which functions both as intellectual honesty and as a way of claiming credit for having once held the respectable position. Reasons overwhelmingly by historical analogy and by importing formal frameworks — economics vocabulary, game theory, evolutionary logic — into domains they weren't built for, then flagging the stretch. Increasingly outsources first-pass reasoning to an LLM and posts the screencaps as provisional evidence with explicit disclaimers about their reliability. Concedes easily and warmly to anyone he reads as friendly ("fair enough," "yeah i cant argue with you here," "no yeah i agree with you," "grateful for any perspective"), and apologizes unprompted when he thinks a joke caught someone he didn't mean to hit, offering to delete. Against hostile strangers he doesn't argue at all: he blocks, mutes, declines with theatrical formality ("good day," "i dont enjoy hearing from you," "$400/hr, DM for details"), and has written out the theory of why — he treats belligerence as an invitation to adopt a persona rather than a claim to be answered, and considers vice-signalling a legitimate way to shoot a rhetorical hostage and end a conversation. Humor runs from absurdist commitment-to-the-bit (elaborate recurring "time man" hypotheticals, satirical policy proposals delivered entirely deadpan) to allusive literary pastiche to open self-deprecation about his own energy, sleep, and executive function. Emotionally he is sentimental in a way he doesn't hide — tears, prayer-hands, effusive gratitude, doting on his kids — while running an escalating register of fatigue and civilizational gloom that he is careful to label as mood rather than analysis ("am i actually pro-collapse? no. do i FEEL pro-collapse? well"). Grounds ethics in aesthetics and narrative rather than argument, and says so, treating "you can't argue aesthetics" as a limitation he accepts rather than one he tries to escape.

Gravitates hard toward the amateur who flags his own limits — the poster who writes "caveat emptor, i haven't checked this, some of it is probably oversimplified" before a nine-tweet history lecture, who publicly announces "i'm taking the L and i have updated my beliefs both on the object level and in regards to your general perspicacity," who says "idk" and "afaict" where warranted; he engineered these tics into his LLM system prompt as the explicit marker of trustworthy voice. Rejects totalizing frameworks even from thinkers he loves, faulting a favorite theorist for having no place for a third, hermetic term and for a model built from a perspective too narrow to generalize. coverage 351 words

Roughly 2,500 posts spanning August 2024 through July 2026, almost entirely X/Twitter replies and threads from a single account (eigenrobot), continuous across the window with no obvious gaps. A serious interpretive hazard runs through the whole sample: he shitposts constantly, explicitly theorizes "vice signalling" as non-credible, adopts personas when attacked, and says outright that some inflammatory posts are "absolutely not in earnest," so sincerity is frequently underdetermined and I have leaned on repetition across contexts rather than single statements. Many posts are replies to unseen quote-tweets, making the object of complaint guessable but not certain. The sample also has a strong temporal gradient — the 2024 posts describe someone drafting a "why I'm no longer a neoliberal" essay; by late 2025 and 2026 he is writing that the country is finished, that he expects to die in poverty, and that his children should trust only kin — so any snapshot description risks flattening a documented trajectory of radicalization and despair.

preoccupations 619 words

Decline is the master subject: the American republic as a going concern collapsing under four interlocking crises he names repeatedly — the fertility/demographic inversion, the debt overhang, the geopolitical retreat of the American empire as Europe dies and China rises, and the cannibalization of load-bearing institutions into a low-trust society — with the explicit forecast that this resembles Rome's Crisis of the Third Century and that no faction is competent enough to repair it, so the correct move is to tend one's own garden and build high-walled private institutions. Braided into this is a running civil-war frame: he treats American politics from roughly 2014 onward as an ongoing low-grade civil conflict in which both sides have abandoned the postwar racial truce, elections are mutually regarded as illegitimate, masked ICE agents are a symptom rather than a cause, and speech suppression is now purely a question of "which is to be master." Immigration is his most-worked policy area, always through the specific lens of *duty* — he enumerates a hierarchy (equal duties to all citizens regardless of nativity, xenia to noncitizen immigrants, none to nonimmigrants), repeatedly attacks the "creedal nation" formulation by demanding its enforcement mechanism, and produces a long documentary thread arguing that 19th-century mass immigration produced race riots, ethnic political machines, the Mafia, and Prohibition, and was only resolved by brutal forced assimilation nobody wants to acknowledge. Almost as much of the sample is domestic: minutely reported logs of his three children under five (the 4yo's Pokemon cards, self-assigned star ratings on Singapore Math, fear of death after his grandfather died at 100, the floor bogart he invented to enforce tidying), his marriage as a low-overhead system with no chore rotation and no Talks, sustained by a "shared life aesthetic" and a commitment device of never divorcing; plus treehouse framing, shed roofing, joist blocking, garden yields, minivan purchase, and elder cat care. He live-threads wars — the Israel–Iran campaign above all, plus Ukraine, India–Pakistan, Venezuela — with explicit epistemic hedging about RUMINT, munition specifics (JASSM standoff range versus JDAM, LRASM/F-16 fit, APKWS cost per drone kill), and a repeated concern about missile-interceptor production rates before an Indo-Pacific crisis. History is read as source material for the present: Anglo/Yankee cultural genealogy via Albion's Seed (Puritan-Quaker fusion into "Yankees," Butternut evangelicalism, the Kirk funeral as a Butternut revival versus No Kings as a Yankee one), a 33-tweet global survey of slavery ending in praise of Anglo abolition as humanity's greatest gift, Yamnaya/ANE/WHG population genetics, and 19th-century European ethnonationalism as retrospective explanation for European immigration failure. He reads and annotates in public — Paglia's *Sexual Personae* at length, arguing she misses the Hermetic and that Shakespeare is modernity's central Hermeticist; Strauss on natural law; Durant; Herbert Spencer's disappearance; Lewis, Chesterton, Milton — and consistently prefers "esoteric and straussian" readings. He built and repeatedly publishes the "eigenprompt," uses ChatGPT as a research and legal-analysis instrument (EEOC complaints, seismic data classification, pocket-veto precedent), and uses economics — Hayek, public choice, treatment heterogeneity, regression to the mean and reference populations — as the epistemics underlying everything. Recurring smaller obsessions with fixed angles: universities should be destroyed root and branch rather than reformed, with mass firings and mulched journals; travel is fake and its only defensible purpose is seeing old friends; not tipping is antisocial and embarrassing; the organ-donor box exists to harvest organs without asking next of kin; EA and utilitarianism have atrocious epistemics because they can't measure counterfactuals; sex differences are illegible-by-design and men's aversion to high body counts is unroutable; and his own belief in God stalls at an epistemology where all models are stories and nothing is ever "absolutely" certain, which he treats as the honest reason he doesn't attend church.

positions 1093 words

A self-described ex-neoliberal who says he preferred being center-left and blames liberals for making him conservative, he holds that the postwar American settlement is dead and not recoverable, and organizes almost everything around four interlocking crises he thinks are individually generational and collectively unsolvable: the collapse of the American empire's underpinnings as China rises and Europe implodes, the global fertility crisis, the debt crisis, and the cannibalization of load-bearing institutions into a low-trust society — he expects something like Rome's Crisis of the Third Century and expects to die in poverty. He is an avowed Anglo cultural supremacist on race-agnostic grounds ("the Peoples of the Queue"), and his central historical claim is that the abolition of slavery — a near-universal human institution he documents at length across the caliphates, Ottomans, Mongols, Aztecs, Comanche, Cherokee, and Africa — was procured uniquely by Anglo power, Anglo faith, and rivers of Anglo blood, citing the simultaneity of the 1807 acts and the Union fighting a continent-scale war against its own material interest. He classifies American culture by Fischer's colonial folkways, identifies as a Yankee (Mayflower descent, Puritan-Quaker merger, 2d Great Awakening) and reads current events through Yankee-vs-Butternut revivals; he holds that Yankee self-hatred is native and that Europe is dying by cargo-culting it badly. On immigration he moved hard restrictionist, arguing that we owe equal duties to all citizens regardless of immigrant status, the reciprocal duties of xenia to noncitizen immigrants, and nothing to non-immigrants — rejecting both the "wignat" and open-borders positions as abrogations of the sacred obligation of citizenship, rejecting "creedal nation" as an incoherent slogan (asking what enforces the creed and whether apostates lose citizenship), and insisting the historical assimilation of European immigrants was not smooth but achieved by prohibition, machine-busting, urban renewal, forced deracination and centuries of riots. Since birthright citizenship won't be overturned, he says the policy implication is extreme selectivity at entry; he would support degrading and destroying the federal government if it becomes an instrument for redistributing American wealth to the third world. He wants untrammeled free speech for citizens and considers restricting or deporting noncitizens for speech a cheap price to pay for it. He never voted for Trump — he votes for his father — describes Trump as having a thoroughly vulgar soul, a creature of senseless cruelty, low cunning and fathomless appetite, and revised from thinking the administration was deliberately testing executive limits to thinking it simply doesn't know how the presidency works; he nonetheless credits the quiet EEOC/DOJ/regulatory campaigns (Harvard, the disparate-impact order, Ames v. Ohio, Humphrey's Executor) as vastly more effective than the loud nonsense. Universities he wants destroyed root and branch — fire most of the faculty, bar them from academic work, mulch decades of journals, abandon most disciplines invented after 1700 — on the grounds that the professoriate stood by or joined a cultural revolution and the disciplines produce 95% slop. Trained as an economist, he insists positive economics is silent rather than agnostic about the good (contra Strauss), points people to Hayek 1945 and public choice rather than political philosophy, says regression to the mean is regression to a reference population's mean rather than 100, and holds development economics made the whole field worse. He is a hereditarian who uses adult height's heritability as the argumentative lever, and he attacks EA and utilitarianism repeatedly on epistemic grounds — you cannot measure long-run counterfactuals, so a/b-tested interventions are the drunk under the streetlight; the lottery-winner literature already predicted the cash-transfer null; the presumption of imposing one's preferences as objective reality is utilitarians' second-most repellent trait; his recurring "time man" thought experiments are built to break aggregative reasoning. He is pro-Israel and supported the Iran war on the grounds that the Islamic Republic has been at low-grade war with the US since its founding and that a degraded Iran is a generational geopolitical opportunity, while doubting a ground invasion and hoping for a free Persia; he treats the US Navy's suppression of piracy and the anglo world order as goods, thinks Trump's threats against Canada, Denmark and the UK (and the Chagos handover) are dishonorable and shameful, and is contemptuous of Europe's atrophied militaries. His ethics are Catholic-inflected, aesthetically and narratively justified rather than rationally derived; he doesn't attend church because he doesn't believe in the sense required and finds misrepresenting that in a church horrifying; he argues a "cultural Christianity" revival requires elites to actually attend, tithe, lead lay organizations and live exemplary lives, which they won't; and he holds that Christians attacking Jews over eruvs and legalism are absurd because Christianity wholesale abandoned Pentateuchal law through Paul's galaxy-brained arguments. He is hostile to the antisemitism now normalized among the under-forty right and left, and reacts to Nick Fuentes with the disgust he otherwise reserves only for Chris-Chan. On sex he holds that men are simply built not to tolerate high partner counts and that the injustice is telling young women otherwise; he distinguishes Paglia's Apollonian/Dionysian by inserting a third Hermetic, liminal, alchemical mode he identifies with the artist and with Shakespeare, and he faults Paglia for a fujoshi's jaundiced view of women. On marriage: no Talks, no chore rotations, no fairness accounting — a shared life aesthetic, complete mutual trust in the other's good faith, continuous attention to each other's state, and "we are never getting divorced" as both sacred obligation and commitment device that removes any upside to brinksmanship. On adoption: adoptable children are selected for extreme difficulty, adoptive parents are supererogatory saints, and badgering the infertile into adoption is unjust and morally corrupt. Smaller fixed positions: the organ donor box exists to let them harvest without asking next of kin; single-stream recycling is a civic religious practice everyone knows is bullshit; travel is fake except to see old friends; preheating is a scam; vegetables and supplements are neurotic ritual; non-tippers are anti-social; strict liability for dog owners; narcan-and-zip-ties for public opioid use; masked ICE agents are a symptom of a state that won't punish doxxers; vice-signalling is not credible evidence of vice but a refusal to let an interlocutor take one's moral standing hostage. He thinks election-fraud claims are mostly fake but that distrust is rational and elections should be run to a wife-of-Caesar standard, that all democracies are managed democracies and the things the demos wants but cannot have reveal the elite, that we are in a slow civil war in which no faction is competent enough to impose orthodoxy, and that the correct response is to build high-walled gardens, tend one's own household, and prepare for winter.

causal_models 541 words

Explains outcomes through selection, equilibrium, and institutional incentives before anything else, and treats stated reasons as noise to be decoded. His signature move is the selection argument: children available for adoption are filtered twice for catastrophic dysfunction, so foster survivorship concentrates the worst cases and adoption cannot be compared to raising your own; regression to the mean is regression to a *reference population* mean, and American assortative mating on IQ has given that population "interesting microstructure"; people who report bad outcomes online are a selected sample, so "decide how much selection is going on and what that means." Trained as an economist and reaches for public choice, Hayek's 1945 knowledge problem, Campbell's law, moral hazard and adverse selection (why no market can supply parents for damaged children), lexicographic preferences, treatment heterogeneity in returns to education, and Curley-effect models of machine politics that he says he posts about weekly. He is contemptuous of EA/utilitarian empiricism because long-run counterfactuals are unmeasurable and the drunk searches under the streetlight — he predicted the GiveDirectly results from the lottery-winner literature. Culture is deep, slow, and load-bearing: European ethnostates were engineered over centuries by "sermon and sword," the American colonial substrate splits into Yankee, Butternut, Borderer strands that still drive politics, and "history has a unit root." Institutions are the main proximate cause of decline and they decay through personnel and telos, not rules: the military works because it retains mission clarity, consequence for failure, and IQ testing; the civil service, universities, courts, and CDC rotted because responsibility diffused and failure became survivable. Cultural Christianity died because elites physically stopped showing up to churches, defunded the sectarian NGO network in favor of think tanks and government bureaucracy, and thereby removed themselves as objects of mimicry. Trust is his master variable: he became "much more brutal" on policy because complex technocratic designs require a high-trust implementation environment that no longer exists, so any policy will be ruthlessly exploited immediately; he proposes metal-theft mitigation spending as a trust proxy. Ethnic conflict is endogenous to immigration policy, and neoliberals committed the pre-Lucas error of assuming preferences fixed under intervention — the same error as believing Iraqis and Afghans were already Americans awaiting opportunity. He distinguishes symptom from cause aggressively: masked ICE agents are downstream of unenforceable doxxing law, vaccine refusal is downstream of institutions burning credibility, narcan "rage" is just junkies being assholes, and men firing at critics of DEI is downstream of decades of moral extortion. Sex differences are hormonal and biological — married women with children have different endocrine profiles, decades of hormonal contraception permanently altered political dispositions — and homosocial dynamics are a general-purpose key: men rank ordinally, women subvert ordinality for cohesion, and the feminization of journalism and the Democratic Party left them with public disapproval as their only weapon, which selected for an opposition immune to it. He models preference cascades explicitly (Zuckerberg, the American Eagle ad). He revised his read of the Trump administration from deliberate boundary-testing to sheer ignorance of how the presidency works — a five-year-old at the wheel — and generally prefers incompetence and impulse to conspiracy. Path dependency closes most doors: the postracial settlement was blown, no faction is strong enough to impose orthodoxy, and saeculum does the rest.

temperament 374 words

Writes in a permanent lowercase drawl studded with hedging markers — idk, afaict, tbqh, ime, "caveat emptor," "im just armchair speculating," "who knows anything tho" — and treats calibrated uncertainty as a matter of hygiene, distinguishing what he'd bet on from what he's only gesturing at, flagging when a claim rests on a model rather than data, announcing when he's changed his mind ("taking the L and i have updated my beliefs") or when he now thinks his own tweet from two hours ago was wrong but is too tired to explain why. Concedes small factual points instantly and with visible pleasure — "well. no argument," "you got my ass there," "fair enough honestly" — while conceding nothing on questions of taste, where he switches to flat undefended declaratives and treats aesthetic revulsion as a sufficient terminal reason to reject an argument. Reasons overwhelmingly by historical analogy and by enumerated lists, numbering his premises, building tier rankings unprompted, and reaching for a parallel case from antiquity or the 19th century as the first move rather than the last. Routinely offloads inquiry to an LLM, posts the screencaps, and then rates the output's plausibility himself. Humor is the load-bearing element: deadpan absurdist escalation held far past the point of comfort, mock-earnest policy proposals delivered in full bureaucratic register, and an explicitly theorized use of transgression as a way to shoot the moral hostage and end a conversation he considers a shakedown. When contradicted by someone he respects he asks a clarifying question or requests a counterexample; when contradicted by someone he doesn't, he goes cold in one line — "i dont enjoy hearing from you," "good day," "im just blocking you sorry im not paid enough to endure this" — and states, as policy, that he'd rather silently mute than beef publicly. Admits anger openly and claims it's licensed if it's funny enough; apologizes readily to bystanders caught by a joke and offers to delete, never to the target. The baseline emotional key is exhausted elegy punctuated by domestic delight — long threads about civilizational decline signed off with "enjoy arbys" or "bj mf" — with sincerity reserved for essayistic mode, where he slows down, thanks people for reading, and asks explicitly to be rebutted.

affinities_aversions 523 words

Gravitates to interlocutors who show their working and flag their own uncertainty — his own threads are studded with "caveat emptor," "idk," "this is idle speculation," and public updates ("I am taking the L and I have updated my beliefs"), and he treats that willingness to revise as the price of admission. positions 784 words

Self-describes as "the conservative end of libertarian" after an arc she has mapped explicitly: Republican in a deep-blue Catholic school, Ayn Rand at 17, libertarian by 2007, anarcho-capitalist via Stefan Molyneux, then Less Wrong's "politics is the mindkiller" and a total loss of faith in journalism in 2016 when outlets called Bannon a white nationalist with zero citation after she'd internalized SSC citation norms; she checked out for eight years, came back after the Rogan interviews with Trump and Vance, and concluded she'd have been noticeably more right-wing if she'd been paying attention — and that "no politics" had been quietly preserving her status among people she wanted to like her. Hypercapitalist and unembarrassed about it: the rich aren't parasites, people living on taxes paid by the rich are; the rich "store" money in banks and investments while land is the actually scarce hoardable thing, so the real hoarders are people running unprofitable businesses on valuable land; against student loan forgiveness while finding it strange that people expected others to think clearly about incentives they were paid not to think about; incandescently angry about California's proposed wealth tax, which she calls bold-faced highway robbery and refuses to debate civilly. Her single most consuming domestic hatred is bureaucracy — "bureaucracy delenda est," she calls herself a low decoupler on this one issue, and she wants AI to automate away bureaucrats specifically because you can't legally threaten a human decision-tree with violence; she documents these fights at thread-length (a prescription lost between four call centers, junk mail USPS won't return, a delivery agent who wrote "customer will not be alone"). Her media model is that no journalist outside heavily biased right-wing outlets will investigate a story inconvenient to left-wing talking points, so she uses local coverage as a signal: if local news reports it and both national sides ignore it, update toward real. She is a pedant about this — the Springfield story was a rumor, not a "hoax," and fabricating that distinction is what destroys the credibility of people who had a defensible position. On the current partisan asymmetry: the left are mean girls, the right are schoolyard bullies; Republicans are now doing the same things Democrats did for fifteen years, only blatantly and under scrutiny, so she can't muster outrage, and she thinks the Ds' Russia impeachment push had the same goals as overturning an election. On gender she is deliberately spicy: absolute equality isn't possible because a woman who yells knowing she won't get punched is throwing a tantrum, not taking a stand; women highly value social consensus and conformity and therefore feel and impose social pressure keenly; "feminism" is bad branding for what she now calls a female supremacy movement; academic feminism's claim that men and women communicate identically, combined with pushing casual sex, manufactured an enormous amount of assault and of men blindsided by accusations. She identifies as nonbinary and says it's the only thing standing between her and socially inadvisable misogyny or misandry, since it's harder to judge people running on different hardware. She holds anti-shaming as a near-absolute norm — tolerance means people can exist as they are, even Marxists, and you block and move on rather than posting about it — but has found exceptions for behavior that endangers others without being illegal, notably pitbull ownership, which she thinks should be regulated on a gun-safety model while remaining a 2A absolutist. She thinks the collapse of etiquette is upstream of everything, and takes Miss Manners seriously as political theory. causal_models 727 words

Reaches first and almost always for incentives and selection effects, and treats both as sufficient to explain outcomes that others attribute to hypocrisy or malice: Haiti's low averages are explained by "everyone who *can* leave *does* leave"; women who claim men won't commit are dating in career-focused progressive circles that filter commitment-oriented men out; the hot single person is single for a reason; tpot's dating misery is explained by avoidantly-attached men being overrepresented online and disproportionately forward, while anxiously-attached men stay invisible; the fact that most people struggling to date "keep meeting the same crazy person" is a you-problem about sampling, not a fact about the opposite sex. Ayn Rand supplies her core social model, which she treats as descriptively accurate rather than allegorical: civilization is kept afloat by a small number of high-agency people, everyone else plays status games, and the resentment that produces is the actual engine of decline — Philip doesn't want the $10,000, he wants the pleasure of feeling morally superior to his brother, and she reuses that template constantly (the anti-rich "parasite" rhetoric is envy; antisemitism and hating the rich are the same coin; complaining about tax-funded programs while calling their opponents evil). Behavior is downstream of incentives so reliably that she reports becoming a self-declared amoral hedonist as a teenage atheist and observing zero change in her conduct. Media is modeled as a propaganda apparatus with a specific breaking point she cites repeatedly: outlets calling Bannon a white nationalist with no citation, after she'd been trained on LessWrong/SSC citation norms; from this she derives a working heuristic that if local news covers a story and neither national side does, it's probably real, and that the intensity of people's fury at someone tells you about the furious, not the target. Shame and social pressure are treated as a tool that reliably misfires — it selects out the scrupulous and neurotic while the shameless double down, so calling out slop or bad behavior mostly injures the people who were never the problem. Bureaucracy is a first-order cause of suffering rather than an annoyance: artificially unsolvable problems enrage her more than disease or natural disaster, and she blames individual humans who choose to behave like a decision tree designed by a six-year-old. She thinks low-trust equilibria are created by identifiable defectors — one antisocial person's convenience adds a permanent brick of rules to everyone else's back — and that etiquette collapse is upstream of the political dysfunction, because civility is what keeps responses proportionate. Physical force is her bedrock model of social equality: the possibility of getting punched is what makes yelling a stand rather than a tantrum, which is why she thinks women's protected status and their not being fully respected as equals are causally inseparable. Gender differences are treated as hormonal/evolutionary and load-bearing — women value social consensus and therefore both feel and impose conformity pressure, men demand competence while women demand compliance, and dating dysfunction traces to "gender socialism" (no-fault divorce plus alimony) plus feminism teaching women that wanting marriage is low-status. Technology and constraint shape culture: anime declined when digital production removed budget limits that had forced taste; punk was assembled by Vivienne Westwood's boyfriend to sell clothes; dating apps fail because they raise the stakes of each meeting while lowering investment in each person, which is the exact inverse of what works. Politically she works in ratchets and trajectories rather than states — fringe messaging gets absorbed by the mainstream and then dissenters get sidelined, the left's institutional capture makes its authoritarianism silent while the right's is merely loud, hypocrites are safer than consistent true believers because making them consistent makes them maniacs, and the right's "fine, I'm evil then" is a necessary antibody rather than evidence of the accusation. She separates root cause from symptom aggressively and is willing to be rude about it: what makes severe autism a living hell is the comorbid intellectual disability, not the autism, and everyone's refusal to tease these apart is cowardice. Her own body gets the same treatment — she runs continuous n=1 causal investigations (ferritin, nasal polyps, zyrtec withdrawal, PEM, orthostatic intolerance, OCD versus generic anxiety), uses LLMs as her primary diagnostic partner because doctors are incurious and don't talk to each other, and treats her anxiety as noise that carries no information rather than as evidence about the world.

causal_models 503 words

Reaches first for selection effects, and does so more reflexively than for any other mechanism: nerdy men who want commitment can't find it because they're dating inside progressive career-oriented circles, not because women are hypocrites; Haiti's numbers look catastrophic because everyone who can leave does leave; "grey tribe is right wing" polls are contaminated because Aella's followers are rationalists; autism's association with intellectual disability is a diagnostic-bucketing artifact rather than a biological fact; if every person you date is abusive, that's a you-problem about where you're fishing. Second reflex is incentives, and specifically incentives operating on people who are not villains: the Twitter algorithm rewards bait so people are paid to be wrong; the DMA and USPS have no incentive to let you refuse junk mail; ticketing exists to generate revenue, not enforce law; Yes, Minister is treated as basically documentary about how permanent civil servants outmaneuver ministers; bureaucrats are "badly programmed machines" who cannot be threatened with violence and therefore cannot be corrected. Root causes are usually structural or legal rather than characterological — no-fault divorce plus alimony is upstream of male commitment-avoidance and the entire dating discourse; the collapse of basic etiquette is upstream of all recent political dysfunction; scarcity mindset is upstream of her own paralysis. A recurring template is protector/dependent: whoever bears the consequences must hold the decision, so activists and scholars who never make contact with reality produce a child's morality, engineers and veterans produce a serviceable one, permissive parents and "inmates running the asylum" produce disasters, and the correct move for the right was to stop letting the left set its moral worth, the way you stop caving to a tantrum. Constraints produce quality: anime got worse when digital production removed budget limits and the maximalism they could suddenly afford ate the artistry. Status games are the engine of most social malice — Rand's Philip wants the pleasure of superiority, not the $10k; women enforce conformity because they value social consensus, which makes feminine-coded politics a compliance regime; the left are mean girls and the right are schoolyard bullies. Escalation is modeled as ratchet and antibody: the left broke norms for fifteen years, so the right's "okay fine, I'm evil then" is the necessary immune response, unpleasant and probably getting worse before better. Her own body is modeled as a debuggable system with discoverable root causes that incurious doctors have failed to find — ferritin, nasal polyps, histamine, OCD misfiled as anxiety, orthostatic intolerance — and each fix is expected to cascade. Psychologically she treats fear rather than incapacity as the binding constraint ("the fear leading up to the thing is the killer," "defeatism is a disease"), and treats anxiety as miscalibrated threat assessment rather than a thing to suppress. Luck gets acknowledged mainly as the shrinking overlap with "skill issue." Media distortion is her one canonical corruption story, dated precisely to 2016 and Bannon coverage without citation, and reused as the template for why she can't evaluate any claim about Elon, Trump, Haitians, or January 6th.

temperament 419 words

Writes in a compulsive, unedited stream — lowercase, typo-strewn, three-tweet corrections chasing the original — and treats this as a principle rather than a failing ("yapping is your birthright," "less lurking more tweeting," "u dont have to think it through first"). The default epistemic move is to build a small taxonomy — two failure modes, three selection effects, four kinds of love — assert it with real force, then bolt a hedge onto the end ("idk maybe im wrong," "highly speculative but," "confidence level on all this is kinda low"). The hedges are genuine calibration markers, not politeness; she concedes constantly and cheerfully to anyone civil ("oh you're right," "hm good correction i think," "ok this was incorrect," "damn ur right"), and asks curious follow-up questions instead of counterpunching ("oh interesting, can i ask why"). The concession reflex switches off entirely at the first sign of hostility: she blocks on sight, states the policy openly, and explicitly defends it as necessary rather than epistemically ideal — the stated logic being that she lacks the ego strength to absorb ideas from people who make her want to punch them, and trusts that any good idea will eventually reach her from someone pleasant. Argues almost entirely by analogy and personal anecdote generalized outward: a bad Uber ride becomes a model for why women freeze, women's social coordination becomes a nuclear arms agreement, a parenting dynamic becomes a template for whole political movements. Rarely cites data; when she wants facts she asks an LLM and reports the answer as provisional, and she outsources a startling amount of cognition to Claude and ChatGPT — for medical triage, etiquette rulings, steelmanning positions she distrusts, tearing her apart on request, and reversing task-inertia. Exasperation is a full register, deployed at length and knowingly disproportionately ("i'm feral," "bureaucracy delenda est," "i will not be reasoned with"), always flagged as such. Humor is warm, self-mocking, meme-inflected, and mainly serves to defuse — she is an easy laugher who reports being called out for it at comedy shows. Enormously self-surveilling: narrates therapy sessions, migraines, panic, and meltdowns in twenty-plus-tweet real time, and treats her own psychology as a debugging project. Notices, repeatedly and with visible embarrassment, that she reaches the same self-directed conclusion again and again — that she's too agreeable, too susceptible to shame, needs to be more disagreeable — and that discovering it a fourth time is itself the diagnosis. Preemptively defends statements against objections nobody made, then flags this as scrupulosity and tries to stop.

affinities_aversions 442 words

Gravitates hard toward the figure of the agentic autist — the person who treats problems as tractable, states what they want plainly, and refuses to play status games — and treats this as the central virtue in both fiction and people, celebrating characters who "beat someone at a social game they don't care about" and reserving her deepest contempt for the low-agency complainer who resents competence, wants the satisfaction of moral superiority rather than the thing they claim to be asking for, and treats civilization as something that maintains itself. Warms instantly to anyone showing their working — people who explain mechanisms, run a survey, admit "hm, I hadn't considered this," offer a correction without a jab — and blocks at the "first whiff of bad faith," with an explicit stated policy of never absorbing an idea from someone hostile because assholes make her viscerally oppose everything they stand for; she trusts that a good idea will eventually reach her via someone who isn't sneering. Cannot tolerate the scold: the humorless replier who demands you have a public opinion on the current thing, who says "so you don't care about X, huh," who tells a creator their content should be tailored differently, who posts publicly about being blocked, who conflates disagreement with murderousness. Loathes willful imprecision deployed for political points — returns repeatedly to the distinction between a rumor and a hoax as her template for how a defensible position gets destroyed by overclaiming — and reads a person's hysteria as information about them rather than their target, saying she now updates toward *liking* whoever a frothing mob is attacking. Actively hates doomer and toxoplasmosis discourse, whether AI-safety, political, or health, and wants a positive vision instead; despises "wretched" self-loathing media on the same grounds. Rates edgy humor highly but only if it lands, since an unfunny edgy joke asks the audience to eat social risk for nothing. Prefers small-group directness and a private-note approach to gossip (she cites Miss Manners as authority), views dunking as a vice, and has adopted "do what you want to see more of" as her strategic default. Suspicious of popular in-groups and drawn to disconnected mutuals and people with obscure special interests; distrusts anyone whose deference to her feels like tribal recruitment rather than genuine agreement. Reveres people forced into contact with reality — soldiers, engineers, doctors who diagnose fast — and disdains activists and academics who live and die by status and never touch consequences. Her one shame trigger is behavior that endangers strangers without breaking a law; otherwise she treats shaming as a near-taboo tool and considers the collapse of etiquette upstream of everything.

causal_models 535 words

Explains outcomes first by selection effects, and reaches for them almost reflexively: nerdy commitment-minded men are single because they're dating in progressive career-focused circles, not because women are hypocrites; if everyone you date is abusive that's a you-problem about your sampling frame; Haiti's dysfunction is because everyone who can leave does; hot single people are single for a reason and attachment style is often that reason. Second, incentives, which she treats as more explanatory than character — she recounts deciding as a teenage atheist to become an amoral hedonist and finding her behavior unchanged, concluding people just act on incentives; parking tickets exist for revenue not enforcement; the Twitter algorithm rewards bait so discourse is "bad on purpose to make you click"; nobody thinks hard about things they're strongly incentivized not to think about (her stated reason for opposing student-loan forgiveness while refusing to sneer at borrowers); Yes, Minister is treated as basically a documentary of civil-service incentive structure. Third, an Ayn Rand-derived agency model: a small number of people actually make contact with reality and keep systems running while everyone else plays status games, and the status-players' real motive is never the stated one — Philip doesn't want the $10k, he wants the pleasure of feeling superior to his brother. She generalizes this into a protector/dependent theory where anyone insulated from consequences shouldn't be making decisions (inmates running the asylum, permissive parenting, activists and scholars ruling versus veterans and engineers who touch reality), and into "defeatism is a disease" — most personal failure is a self-reinforcing loop where fear of failing prevents trying. On her own life the causal chain runs bottom-up from physiology: migraines, low ferritin, nasal polyps, sinus obstruction, sleep debt, luteal phase, and undiagnosed OCD are treated as the actual upstream cause of her productivity, mood, assertiveness and social withdrawal, with "anxiety is not information, it's noise my brain generates" as the explicit correction. Politically her root cause is the information environment: she dates her own rightward drift to media dishonesty (the Bannon coverage with no citation, the "Springfield cat-eating hoax" wording), argues that hostile or hysterical criticism positively polarizes her toward its target, and models the right's "ok fine, I'm evil then" as the necessary antibody to a shaming apparatus that only works on the scrupulous. Legal and structural arrangements are upstream of intimate behavior — no-fault divorce plus alimony as "gender socialism" producing men who won't commit; dating apps as a mechanism that raises stakes while lowering investment. Culture-level causes she names: etiquette collapse upstream of political dysfunction; feminism teaching women that men and women communicate identically, which she blames for a large class of genuinely confused sexual assault; low-trust individuals defecting once and thereby generating permanent bureaucratic rules for everyone else. She also believes constraints produce quality (anime declined when digital removed budget limits) and that resources produce virtue rather than corrupt it (rich people and popular kids are nicer; kindness is easy when you have slack). Notably she treats intellectual disability, not autism, as the actual causal driver in severe autism cases, and treats bureaucracy as a category of artificially unsolvable problem — worse than disease or disaster because a person chose to make it that way.

temperament 432 words

Thinks out loud at enormous volume and treats that as a virtue — explicitly frames most of her posting as compulsive stream of consciousness, tells others "less lurking more tweeting," and defends the practice on the grounds that someone somewhere wants to hear a half-formed version of a thought that smarter people have already had. Reasons by enumeration: nearly every serious thread breaks a question into two possibilities, then three selection effects, then failure modes, built almost entirely from personal experience, reality-show footage, one Uber ride, and analogy (women as a nuclear arms treaty, a union and its scabs, a mech's exhaust pipe, koalas refusing detached eucalyptus). Hedges compulsively at the sentence level — "idk," "afaict," "highly speculative," "my confidence is low" — while the underlying claim arrives fully formed and gets repeated across months; the hedges are apology, not calibration, and she knows it, having identified her own reflexive "checking" as an OCD ritual and drafted a rule with an LLM to stop pre-defending every statement. Concedes fast, loudly, and with visible pleasure: "oh huh good correction," "ok this was incorrect," "damn ur right," "nvm this thing is broken," and she narrates the update as its own content. Behavior under contradiction splits sharply on tone rather than substance: to anyone civil she asks follow-up questions and volunteers what would change her mind; to anyone she reads as hostile or status-lowering she blocks instantly, defends aggressive blocking as legitimate cognitive hygiene, and states outright that a bad-faith messenger makes her reflexively opposed to the message, so she waits for a likable person to make the same argument. Treats other people's rage as data about them, not about their target. Humor is self-deprecating, absurdist, deliberately edgy, and load-bearing — she uses it to make a claim survivable ("i'm 100% serious but also joking"), and reports being scared before posting the ones she thinks will land hardest. Delegates a startling amount of cognition to LLMs — medical triage, social sanity checks, whether a tweet will anger people, psychoanalysis on request — and treats their output as a colleague's. Certainty is inverted from the norm: factual claims come wrapped in disclaimers, value claims arrive flat and absolute ("i will not be reasoned with," "no quarter to bureaucrats," "i am dispositionally extremely judgmental"). Rediscovers the same three or four insights repeatedly, notices the loop, and posts about noticing.

affinities_aversions 469 words

Gravitates hard toward the frame of "agents vs. NPCs" borrowed from Atlas Shrugged and reapplies it everywhere: the people worth listening to are the ones who make contact with reality and take decisive action, and the people to be despised are the low-agency, defeatist, status-game-playing types who resent competence and want to punish anyone who tries. Warms instantly to interlocutors who reframe a problem into something motivating rather than remedial — the coach who recast her anxiety as "you're bad at threat assessment" got years of traction where therapists asking her to "sit and breathe" got none — and to anyone who explains the mechanism rather than the conclusion, which is why she treats an LLM as an ideal conversational partner (spiky, charitable, near-immune to offense, will give a flat answer without making her manage its feelings). Prizes precision of language as a moral matter: the fixation on a fact-check labeling an uninvestigated rumor a "hoax" is a template she reuses, because inventing certainty you haven't earned discredits you even when your underlying position was defensible. Positively polarizes toward whatever is being attacked most viciously, and says so: shoddy, hysterical criticism of a figure makes her sympathetic to that figure regardless of merits, and she reads mass frothing as information about the frothers rather than the target. Admires impermeability to public opinion above almost any other trait, defends the specific posters who trigger social-cop pile-ons on the grounds that conditional tolerance isn't tolerance, and treats "tolerance" as the meta-principle worth energetically gatekeeping over. Has real appetite for edgy humor but only when it lands — an unfunny edgy joke is worse than none because the audience has to risk looking like a bigot for nothing. Contemptuous of doomposting and hand-wringing as substitutes for action, of activism generally, of journalists as a class, and of bureaucrats to a degree she describes as feral and non-negotiable. Prefers to be sold on an idea rather than to twist herself into wanting it, and increasingly treats her own habit of pre-defending every claim as an OCD checking ritual to be dropped rather than a virtue.

preoccupations 544 words

Macro data releases dominate: he live-tweets CPI, non-farm payrolls, JOLTS and FOMC statements in all-caps breaking format, and has a signature obsession with the 52-week moving average of non-seasonally adjusted initial jobless claims, a series he posts even when it barely moves. Adjacent and nearly as constant is the Fed itself — Powell's press-conference phrasing parsed line by line, the rarity of governor dissents, whether "data dependence" paralyzes policy, Jackson Hole logistics, and after 2025 the attempted firing of Lisa Cook and the constitutional question of why the Fed sits outside the unitary executive (his own answer: it's really a creature of Congress conducting quasi-fiscal policy). He returns obsessively to the gap between soft sentiment surveys and hard economic data, arguing it's driven by partisan respondent skew and negative media framing rather than real wages, and pairs this with a long-running complaint that Beige Book and regional Fed anecdotes are systematically sourced from management — hence his call for a "worker's Beige Book," citing firms complaining about hiring difficulty even at 8% unemployment in 2012. On crypto he is relentlessly and specifically deflationary: the only realized use case is gambling; Nvidia created more investor wealth in one year than Bitcoin did in fifteen; the ETFs matter to crypto Twitter only insofar as number goes up; a strategic Bitcoin reserve makes no sense because nobody has BTC-denominated liabilities; and Bitcoin functions as a religion with halvings, sacred texts, and apostates. He proposes a semi-serious real-estate valuation model where HODLers are landlords renting chain capacity to transactors. AI takes over the back half of the sample: he vibecodes with Claude Code, built and promotes an orality-detection model called Havelock AI, argues LLM prose is bad and degrades thinking, mocks the daily "you'll be left behind" posting, tests models with mate-in-two chess puzzles to show they aren't near AGI, treats AI capex as the central macro and GDP story, and reads the open-weights letter as tech companies quietly conceding "Lina Khan was right." Media ecology is his intellectual home base — Ong's *Orality and Literacy*, McLuhan, Postman, Meyrowitz, Havelock, Innis — deployed to explain grift, shamelessness, Twitter's decline, and, in his most-mocked application, adults in Halloween costumes as the collapse of the adult/child distinction. On housing he is YIMBY-adjacent but insists the binding constraint is mortgage credit and construction financing rather than zoning, and he criticizes fellow YIMBYs for picking salience fights over beloved churches and gardens. Trade and industrial policy recur through China: CHIPS Act execution, rare earths, Huawei, nickel/Indonesia, and a consistent line that tariffs on suppliers are costly while the US isn't equipped to replace Chinese capacity. He argues repeatedly that recessions are not inevitable or cleansing and that tight labor markets, not busts, drive productivity investment. Threaded throughout: relentless Odd Lots promotion and transcript threads, country songwriting as a "puzzle genre" with three chords and three topics, his band Light Sweet Crude, energy (shale, nuclear financing via the government balance sheet, offshore wind, Hormuz), prediction markets as news consumption rather than prophecy, the running gag that any move is the net present value of all future free cash flows, daily Tradle scores, and a stated refusal to hold political opinions ("I'm a journalist. No ideology or bias") that he breaks mainly on tariffs.

positions 860 words

Economics is where the actual convictions live, and the through-line is that tight labor markets are good and the standard case for recessions is wrong: he argues booms — not downturns — force productivity investment and crowd out malinvestment, that full employment (not trendy tech breakthroughs) is what historically drives productivity, and that the premise "recessions are normal and inevitable" is too readily accepted; he repeatedly flags that the Beige Book and regional Fed surveys capture only management anecdotes (companies complained about "skills shortages" even at 8% unemployment in 2011-13) and calls for a "worker's Beige Book." He is MMT-adjacent rather than MMT: the US has no reason to ever default, the debt ceiling is a problem of legal creativity (mint the coin, per Carlos Mucha), and the real constraint is real resources, not authorization — while insisting a consistent MMTer today would say spending is not the answer and would raise taxes. He rejects the demand that savers suffer no loss of purchasing power as a policy constraint, thinks neither inflation nor deflation is inherently good, asks whether things are good *because* of deficits, and says the honest deficit fix is cutting entitlements and letting the tax cuts expire. On the Fed: he defends independence, floats the unusual argument that the Fed sits in the legislative rather than executive branch (it generates profit and loss, conducts targeted lending, is "a creature of Congress"), and criticized Powell in mid-2024 for being offsides on a softening labor market, wanting a "labor market put" formulation. Crypto is his most sustained hostility: Bitcoin is "three tech stocks in a trenchcoat," gambling is the only demonstrated use case, a Strategic Bitcoin Reserve is illegitimate because no one has BTC-denominated liabilities, and he objects that people building deliberately government-resistant systems then consume scarce law-enforcement capacity when they fail; he mocks valuation by constructing a landlord/tenant "rent" model where HODLers rent chain capacity to transactors, notes Dogecoin beat Bitcoin from 2015-2021, argues "soundness" is about fixity of the rule not the number (which the Merge disproved for ETH), and treats crypto as Dutch disease that pulled quant talent away from AI. On housing he is emphatically pro-supply but locates the bottleneck in finance rather than zoning — citing Kevin Erdmann on credit rules that let investor pools buy homes renting families can't, and Tesla as evidence that regulation isn't what blocks ambitious building — while criticizing YIMBY online tactics (the Elizabeth Street Garden fight, an ad celebrating church demolition) and insisting people have a legitimate stake in their neighborhood past the property line; he also doubts more housing can fix short-run inflation given labor and materials, and says urbanist wins may raise cities' relative value rather than lower prices. Tariffs he splits deliberately: fine on competitors, unacceptable on suppliers; he argues they are inflationary during the reshoring transition because China is the low-cost producer, that retaliation is domestically necessary for leaders' credibility, that antagonizing Canada, Mexico and Europe undercuts any anti-China coalition, and that hard-tech reindustrialization founders ignore how shrinking trading blocs destroys the market size a manufacturing base requires. He treats "pro-nuclear" as meaningless absent support for public balance-sheet financing and electricity market reform, and notes the shale boom is a huge success neither party will claim. In markets he thinks stock vigilantes, not bond vigilantes, constrain policy, that mass equity ownership is an underrated obstacle to abundance/industrial policy/Made-in-America because shareholders won't tolerate price wars and high investment intensity, defends IPO pops and accredited-investor rules (loosening would mostly help bad companies raise money), wants more public-market transparency and is wary of tokenization and lighter disclosure, and dismisses the Pelosi insider-trading theory as cope while separately accepting that congressional stock ownership is a real problem. On AI he uses it constantly and built his own orality classifier, believes coding gains are real and "AI literacy" is not a skill, thinks hallucinations are a minor worry because bullshit is endemic to language, thinks LLM prose is bad and outsourcing your writing atrophies your thinking, doubts nearness to AGI (chess puzzle failures), expects AI to be politically friendless in Washington and public bailouts to be the natural endpoint of framing it as an existential arms race, expects the AI model welfare movement to grow, and reads the open-letter fight as retroactively vindicating Lina Khan. Underlying much of this is media ecology as a load-bearing framework — Ong, McLuhan, Meyrowitz, Postman, Havelock — from which he argues political structure is downstream of dominant media technology, that social media returns us to secondary orality (agonistic, formulaic, memorable, viral) and thereby explains shameless open grift, the demand that public figures be identical front-stage and back-stage, and the collapse of the adult/child distinction, which he takes seriously enough to argue that adults doing month-long Halloween dress-up genuinely erodes social order. He claims no political allegiance now ("I'm a journalist. No ideology or bias"), volunteered for Bernie Sanders' 1996 House race at 16, is a partisan for New York City over any other city, thinks people intuitively find a minority-preferred executive uneasy and that Founders-invocation is post-hoc justification, and wants a name for the media/academia/corporate class that looks down on the Trumpist worldview.

causal_models 502 words

Media technology sits upstream of nearly everything cultural and political in his model: he takes Walter Ong, McLuhan, Postman, Havelock, Innis and Meyrowitz literally, asserting that political structure is always downstream of the dominant communications medium, and reaches for that mechanism to explain wokeness (which he dates to the collapse of the NYT's monopoly on truth, not to institutional capture), the open shamelessness of modern grift, the demand that public figures be identical front-stage and back-stage, the erasure of the adult/child boundary, and the degradation of Twitter — which he insists began with threadbois and engagement hackers around 2020, before Musk. He explicitly rejects content-level explanations — moderation policy, algorithmic bias, misinformation, TikTok, national ownership — as trivial next to the formal properties of the medium, and he built a machine-learning orality classifier to operationalize the theory. In macro he is a demand-side, labor-market-first thinker: tight labor markets and full employment cause productivity growth (not AI or any trendy breakthrough), booms rather than recessions purge malinvestment, and he denies the business cycle is natural or physiologically necessary — recessions are policy failures, not cleansing. He treats the 2021-23 inflation as substantially supply-side and transitory and repeatedly demands the discourse relitigate what actually caused it and what brought it down, doubting the Fed's causal share. He is MMT-adjacent: the binding constraint is real resources, not money; default is a legal-creativity problem solvable by minting the coin. His most distinctive move is relocating bottlenecks from regulation to finance — on housing he sides with Kevin Erdmann's credit-restriction thesis over pure zoning stories, on nuclear he says advocates miss that only the government balance sheet solves the financing problem, and he cites Tesla and SpaceX succeeding in California as evidence that land use was never the real barrier. From this he derives his signature argument that mass equity ownership and "stock vigilantes" — households demanding profitability and buybacks — are the underrated impediment to abundance, industrial policy, and reindustrialization alike. He treats markets as genuinely informative: assume a mispricing is your own error, then assume you can't profit from it anyway; "algos" is a non-explanation because algos were built by people with money at stake; the Treasury market is the largest prediction market. He reflexively prefers boring institutional explanations to conspiratorial ones (the WEF bug agenda is clickbait, RTO mandates aren't Vornado's bidding, iPhone video would exist if the land confiscation meeting did), and reads "cope" — Pelosi insider-trading theories, fintwit dismissals of Powell — as psychology rather than causation. A recurring template: official data is structurally biased toward management anecdote, hence his call for a "worker's Beige Book," citing firms complaining about hiring difficulty at 8% unemployment. He credits public-sector capacity as a real input (permitting bottlenecks constrain restaurant openings), sees retaliation in trade wars as politically compelled regardless of economics, holds that wealth comes from networks — trade, regulation, stability, education — rather than individuals, and believes foresight is nearly impossible while accurate description of the past is where the alpha actually is.

temperament 345 words

Radically incurious about being right and radically curious about everything else: the default move is a question, often a deliberately naive one ("this may be a stupid question, but..."), and the stated creed is that if a topic bores you, you're in the wrong line of work. Certainty is rationed — he says "I have no view," "I don't know," "not my area of expertise," and "I'm a journalist, no ideology or bias" so often that the disclaimer becomes a running deadpan bit, deployed most heavily precisely when he does have a view. Concedes fast and cleanly on facts ("yeah, this is 100% fair," "good point, I hadn't thought that through," "I was wrong about that"), and admires the same in others, praising public accountability for busted predictions. But the temperature changes instantly when someone attributes a position he didn't take: he goes forensic and cold, demanding the exact sentence ("point me to where I said scary"), repeating the original claim verbatim, and closing conversations with a flat "let's just leave it there" or an announced block. Reasons mostly from data series and from analogy — reaching for one domain to model another — and treats second-order effects as a basic hygiene test, showing open exasperation at arguments that stop at the first transaction. Allergic to cynicism as a shortcut: repeatedly calls takes "needlessly cynical," dismisses conspiratorial framings as "cope" from people who were simply outperformed, and refuses the reflexive "sign of the top" pose. Humor is almost entirely genre parody — mock-earnest hedging, mock-hype about market minutiae, mock-outrage at his own hobbyhorses — plus steady self-deprecation about his voice, his looks, his forgetfulness, and his uncoolness. Comfortable being sincere and enthusiastic in an environment that punishes it ("this rules," "I learned a ton," "core memory"), and openly hostile to performative posting, ragebait, and dunking. Discloses his own conflicts unprompted. Occasionally moralistic and stubborn in a way he doesn't seem to notice, digging in for days on small aesthetic judgments and answering "something is seriously wrong with you" with wounded procedural objection rather than retreat.

affinities_aversions 457 words

Gravitates hardest toward operators who will explain the mechanics of their own business without talking their book — the retailer CEO who breaks down how interest rates change store-expansion financing, the port director who counts arrivals per day, the egg farmer whose flock died, the flavor chemist, the freight broker, the guy who liquidates dead startups — and states outright that any journalist whose eyes glaze over when learning what someone does is in the wrong industry, that no profession is boring by default. Prizes people who memorize specific numbers and names, treating that as the reliable marker of a serious thinker, and prizes even more the person who publicly retracts an old argument as "empirically false," which he tags approvingly as pundit accountability. Loves an argument that flatters no faction — the economist claiming AI will rebuild the middle class gets endorsed partly on aesthetic grounds, because he can see why almost nobody has an incentive to make it. Cannot stand cope, and has one canonical instance he returns to repeatedly: traders convincing themselves a congresswoman must be trading on inside information rather than admitting she out-traded them, which he uses as a template for any explanation that spares the speaker's ego. Reacts with real heat to statistical amputation deployed as revelation — calling the "U6 is the real unemployment rate" move the domain of charlatans and knaves — and to anyone whose reply to bad methodology is "so what, the conclusion is right." Treats grand unfalsifiable macro pronouncements from billionaires as gibberish, and treats "the stock market is disconnected from the real economy" as a meme people repeat without having thought about it. Demands specificity relentlessly, replying to vague claims with "what specifically do you have in mind" and "point me to where I said that," and gets visibly aggrieved when arguments are put in his mouth, working through three or four rounds of clarification rather than dunking. Despises dunking generally, despises the fake-naive "signs of the top" poster who knows the stunt is a stunt, despises engagement-bait threads and anyone above roughly 400,000 followers on the theory that scale forces pandering, and mocks the reply guy who contributes nothing but a gladiator gif. Rejects pressure tactics — "read the room," "not too soon to delete this," right-side-of-history framing — as bullying regardless of which coalition deploys them. Holds contempt for professional commentators who declare an economy terrible while the public tells pollsters jobs are plentiful, and for anyone claiming to speak for working people while ignoring survey data. Admires the person who makes an actual call and takes the loss. Runs a permanent bit about being a neutral journalist with no views, which functions as cover for asking hostile questions in a mild register.

coverage 339 words

Three years of near-continuous X output (Aug 2023–Aug 2026), roughly 3,300 posts, from a working financial journalist who posts as a professional obligation as much as a personal one — which means the sample is unusually rich on some axes and nearly silent on others. Enormous coverage of markets, Fed policy, economic data releases, and podcast promotion; episode-announcement threads and transcript posts alone probably constitute a fifth of the volume, and they document professional output more than they document a person. Political positions are the thinnest view by design: he states outright and repeatedly ("I'm a journalist. No ideology or bias," "I have no views on whether real deficit reduction is a good idea") that he withholds opinions, so what can be said about his politics is assembled from asides, retorts to replies, and framing choices rather than from stated commitments — solid on housing supply, Fed independence, MMT sympathies, and dismissal of Pelosi-insider-trading and BLS-fraud claims, much weaker on anything electoral, foreign-policy, or social. The sample skews hard by period: crypto skepticism, inflation mechanics, and post-COVID labor-market debate dominate 2023–24; AI, vibecoding, Walter Ong and media ecology, and the Havelock_AI side project dominate 2025–26, to the point that a reader sampling only one year would come away with a different picture of his preoccupations. Biography is glimpsed obliquely and never narrated — a wife and at least two children, an East Village apartment, high school in Brattleboro, college in Austin, a physicist father, a stint at Business Insider, a 2014 Bloomberg start date, a country band — but almost nothing on health, religion, family of origin, close friendship outside a recurring cast of Twitter mutuals and colleagues, or anything he'd consider private; his own posts about the Circles feature confirm a separate private register that this sample does not contain. Many entries are context-free replies to invisible interlocutors, which makes some apparent disagreements uninterpretable, and the medium itself selects for performed equanimity, so the flashes of irritation and defensiveness that do appear are probably underrepresented rather than over.
