preoccupations 569 words

Macro data releases dominate: CPI prints, non-farm payrolls, jobless claims, Fed decisions and Powell press conferences, all live-tweeted with the headline numbers and consensus miss, plus a personal signature metric — the 52-week moving average of non-seasonally adjusted initial jobless claims — trotted out repeatedly even when it barely moves. Around that sits a sustained argument about the gap between soft and hard data: he insists consumer sentiment surveys are distorted by partisan respondents (contrasting Democrats' silence with 2017-era Republicans volunteering unprompted praise to pollsters), and he built a recurring complaint that official anecdotal evidence — the Beige Book, regional Fed surveys — comes exclusively from the management side, calling for a "worker's Beige Book" and noting that firms have claimed a "skills shortage" continuously since 2011-13 while still hiring. He returns to the Fed obsessively: dissent frequency as a test of a chair's internal credibility, the dot plot, whether labor-market weakness is non-linear, and after 2025, the constitutional question of Fed independence, where he floats the argument that the Fed sits in the legislative rather than executive branch. AI takes over the back half of the sample from three angles simultaneously — as a macro/capex story that dwarfs telecom and is reshaping electricity, memory chips and credit spreads; as a personal practice, where he vibecodes with Claude Code and builds Havelock AI, a classifier that scores text on an oral-to-literate spectrum; and as an object of media-theoretic analysis, where he insists LLM prose is genuinely bad, that hallucinations are the least interesting risk, and that "AI literacy" is nonsense because the tools keep getting easier. Media ecology is his intellectual signature: Walter Ong's *Orality and Literacy* (which he re-reads and recommends dozens of times), plus McLuhan, Postman, Meyrowitz and Andrey Mir, deployed to explain shameless grift, engagement-bait Twitter, adults in Halloween costumes for a month, and the collapse of front-stage/back-stage consistency in public figures. Crypto is a permanent sparring partner rather than an interest: he argues the only surviving use case is gambling, proposes valuing Bitcoin as "rent" transactors pay HODLers for chain capacity, opposes a Strategic Bitcoin Reserve on the grounds the government has no BTC-denominated liabilities, repeatedly notes Nvidia created more wealth in one year than Bitcoin in fifteen, and mocks the Pelosi-insider-trading theory as cope from people she out-trades. Housing recurs, but almost always through credit and financing constraints rather than zoning — he champions Kevin Erdmann's thesis that mortgage lending standards, not permitting, block starter homes, and needles YIMBYs for picking fights over specific beloved churches and gardens. Other steady returns: tariffs and reindustrialization (2025 is wall-to-wall Liberation Day coverage, with the argument that reshoring is costly precisely because China is the low-cost producer), China read through books rather than headlines (Ezra Vogel on Deng, Torigian on Xi Zhongxun, Kevin Rudd, Dan Wang), oil and energy financing (nuclear's real bottleneck is the government balance sheet and electricity market structure, not bees), the claim that recessions are not inevitable and that hot labor markets — not layoffs — drive productivity, prediction markets as a news-consumption tool whose real risk is monetizing confidential information, country songwriting as a "puzzle genre" of three chords and three permitted topics, Moby Dick, which he cites in unrelated contexts for a year straight, and a running deadpan bit about being an unbiased journalist with no opinions who tracks the net present value of all future S&P 500 free cash flows.

positions 781 words

Holds that the anecdotal inputs to official economic data are systematically management-biased and has campaigned for years for a "worker's Beige Book," citing manufacturers complaining about a skills shortage in 2013 with 8% unemployment as the template; from this flows a consistent pro-tight-labor-market stance — full employment, not recession, is what forces productivity-enhancing investment, booms crowd out malinvestment because cheap labor lets bad business models survive, and the premise that a business cycle with periodic mass job loss is inevitable is one he flatly rejects. Treats "I'm a journalist, no ideology or bias" as a running gag while holding sharp views: inflation itself rather than negative real wages explains sour consumer sentiment; media negativity is a real input to that sentiment; the deficit question should be inverted to "are things good *because* we're running big deficits"; the debt ceiling is a non-problem because default is unconstitutional and the only binding constraint on payment is legal creativity (mint the coin, per Carlos Mucha). Sympathetic-but-precise on MMT — defends it against strawmen, insists its core is real resource constraints rather than permanent dovishness, and notes that the mainstream worry about interest payments causing inflation differs from the MMT claim only in timing. On crypto, has settled on the position that gambling and evading regulatory friction are the only realized use cases, that crypto twitter's sole metric is whether BTC/USD went up, that a Strategic Bitcoin Reserve is incoherent because neither the government nor the private sector has BTC-denominated liabilities, and that if you want a valuation model the right analogy is rent — HODLers as landlords, transactors as tenants paying for temporary chain capacity. Housing: pro-supply but insists the binding constraint is financing and credit provision (Erdmann, Williams) more than zoning; thinks online YIMBYism is the cheapest possible activism and that picking fights over beloved specific sites like Elizabeth Street Garden or old churches is self-defeating. Repeatedly calls the Pelosi-insider-trading obsession pure cope from people she out-traded, while conceding COVID-briefing trades were real misconduct. Argues stock vigilantes, not bond vigilantes, are the real constraint on policy, that mass equity ownership is an underrated impediment to any abundance agenda or industrial policy since shareholders punish high investment intensity and price wars, and that a prolonged bear market would be treated as a violation of the American social contract. Defends Fed independence on the grounds that the Fed conducts what is effectively fiscal policy sitting in Congress's branch, not the executive's, and thinks that if inflation returns to target without a corresponding unemployment spike central banks should emerge with more credibility, not less. On tariffs he broke his no-opinions vow to land on "tariffs on my competitors are fine, tariffs on my suppliers have to go," argues that since China is the lowest-cost producer any substitution is costly during the transition, and that a shrinking trading bloc undermines the very reindustrialization tariff advocates want — while separately taking Hamilton's point that manufacturing is a precondition of sovereignty seriously. On AI: an enthusiastic heavy user who built his own orality classifier with Claude Code, dismisses hallucinations as a low-ranked worry (bullshit is endemic to language production generally), insists LLM prose is genuinely bad and that outsourcing your own writing is a mental mistake, is skeptical that "AI literacy" is a skill worth teaching, expects AI to be politically friendless by 2028 with attackers on both flanks, expects public money and bailouts as the endpoint once AGI is framed as an arms race, and read the open-weights letter as a straightforward "Lina Khan was right" antitrust story that its signatories won't say out loud. Grounds a large share of his cultural analysis in Ong, McLuhan, Meyrowitz and Postman — political structure is downstream of the dominant media technology, social media returns us to secondary orality, and that, not institutional capture, explains both wokeness and the shamelessness of open grift; explicitly rejects the claim that internal changes at the NYT drove the culture, since the paper's shadow has never been narrower. Takes the collapse of the adult/child distinction seriously enough to argue that a month of adults in costumes is an actual social harm rather than harmless fun. Views gambling restrictions as a de facto safety net cutting off left-tail outcomes for the addiction-prone; values prediction markets primarily as news consumption while arguing the real test is whether mispricings are easy to exploit, and worries they multiply the ways confidential information can be monetized. Defends financial-market transparency, accredited-investor rules as a brake on manufactured retail vehicles, and argues the IPO "pop" is simply the underwriting fee and that nobody inside the issuer is actually upset. Believes efficient markets practically: assume you're wrong, then assume you can't profit anyway.

causal_models 507 words

Media technology sits at the root of nearly every cultural explanation he offers: political structure is downstream of the dominant communications medium, and the shift from print literacy back toward "secondary orality" — via Walter Ong, McLuhan, Postman, and Meyrowitz — is his master key for the golden age of open grift, the collapse of shame, the demand that public figures be identical front-stage and back-stage, adults dressing up in costumes for a month, "lore," AI slop, and what gets called wokeness. He explicitly rejects rival mechanisms here: not algorithms, not moderation policy, not TikTok's Chinese ownership, not content. He notes wokeness peaked exactly when the NYT's monopoly on truth was weakest, which he takes as disproof that internal institutional change caused it. In economics, his signature move is to relocate bottlenecks from regulation to finance: he thinks the YIMBY story is incomplete because credit access — not zoning — determines whether starter homes get built, cites Kevin Erdmann and his bandmate PE Williams, and treats Tesla and SpaceX as proof that regulation wasn't binding since Elon simply had a cult willing to fund him. From this follows his most distinctive thesis, "stock vigilantes": mass household equity ownership (now a bigger share of net worth than real estate) punishes capex-heavy price wars, making shareholder discipline the underrated obstacle to abundance, industrial policy, and reindustrialization alike. Tight labor markets, not trendy technology, drive productivity — booms crowd out firms dependent on cheap labor while recessions merely let companies hire cheaply, and he rejects the physiological intuition that downturns cleanse an economy or that business cycles are inevitable at all. He treats official economic anecdote as structurally management-biased ("we need a worker's Beige Book"), noting firms complained of skills shortages at 8% unemployment in 2011-2013. He is MMT-adjacent in taking seriously that the US cannot be forced to default and that real resources, not dollars, are the constraint, and he consistently asks whether 2021-23 inflation came from stimulus or supply disruption, treating global synchronized disinflation without rising unemployment as evidence against the standard story. On crypto, ideology is post-hoc: the hard-money and decentralization arguments can't be causal because coins that can't bear those arguments move identically, so gambling is the only durable use case, and Bitcoin's real structure is religious (sanctified halvings, sacred whitepaper, apostates like Mike Hearn treated worse than nonbelievers). Elsewhere: public-sector staffing capacity constrains restaurant and retail expansion; the government balance sheet is what actually solves nuclear financing, which right-leaning nuclear boosters miss; retaliation to tariffs is economically unwise but politically mandatory for domestic credibility; land is finite so its accumulation genuinely harms others in a way underwear does not; tech concentrates wealth because few workers create the value, not because markets are private; and China's weak household consumption traces to 1980s reforms making provincial revenue depend on business, producing an intranational race to attract investment. He assumes markets are efficient by default — first assume you're wrong, then assume you can't profit anyway — and treats prediction markets as pricing conventional wisdom rather than forecasting.

temperament 409 words

Presents as a professional questioner rather than an arguer: the dominant move is to ask something plainly ("Genuinely curious what specifically you have in mind," "Can you explain that further?", "What's the best counter-argument?") and let the answer do the work, often prefacing with "I have no view on that" or the recurring deadpan disclaimer "I'm a journalist, no ideology or bias" — a joke that also functions as armor. Reasons from data series, survey internals, and named-guest anecdote far more than from principle; when someone asserts a trend, the reflex is to ask for the number, the timing, the counterfactual, or the mechanism ("Ok, but this just begs a further question of why"). Hedges heavily on causal claims and forecasts, then goes flatly declarative on process claims — that a chart shows X, that a survey respondent said Y, that a comparison is apples-to-apples — and defends those with unusual tenacity. When contradicted, splits into two modes: with good-faith interlocutors, concedes readily and cheerfully ("Yeah, that's 100% fair," "good point, hadn't thought that through," "I was wrong about this"); with anyone who paraphrases him uncharitably, becomes clipped and forensic, repeatedly demanding "Where did I say that?" and "Point me to where I said scary," refusing to engage until the misquote is retracted, sometimes ending with a curt "let's leave it there" or a block he later admits was an itchy finger. Humor is deadpan absurdist and mostly self-implicating — running gags about the net present value of all future free cash flows, ironic "nobody is more pro-X than me, but," faux-solemn concern-trolling about his own credentials — used to deflate people he reads as posturing, engagement-baiting, or claiming secret knowledge, which is the one category that reliably draws open contempt ("complete cope," "gibberish," "lowest tier posting"). Practices deliberate epistemic hygiene as a public ritual: abandoning a book that confirmed his priors to buy the opposite, reading a transcript in solitude before letting Twitter form his opinion, praising others for admitting they were empirically wrong. The exception to the equable manner is a small set of moral-aesthetic convictions where he becomes stubborn, unironic, and willing to litigate for days against near-universal pushback, then self-aware about having done so ("now a lot of people seem to think I hate fun, which is unfortunate — I mean, they're right"). Enthusiasm is the loudest register overall: "extremely fun," "learned a ton," "absolute blast," and frequent, specific credit to producers, colleagues, and friends.

Gravitates hardest toward the practitioner who will explain the actual mechanics of their work — the CEO willing to walk through how his balance sheet finances new store construction rather than talk his book, the flavor chemist, the bridge-collision expert on DM speed-dial, the debt-management-office alum, the sourcing guy who describes who calls whom before a deal prices — and holds as an article of faith that no profession is boring, scolding media people whose "eyes glaze over" when they hear what someone does for a living. Repeatedly and pedantically insists on being quoted accurately ("point me to where I said scary"), and treats strawmanning as the cardinal sin of the platform. Rejects conspiracism about official statistics and 3D-chess readings of policy on the grounds that they require assuming the world's most sophisticated market participants are gullible dupes — an explanation he says he dismisses out of hand. Irritated by people who can't reason past the first transaction (broken-window arguments) and by the daily genre of posting that everyone else is about to be left behind by AI. Poses as a neutral, bias-free journalist as a running bit while lodging plenty of opinions, and gets noticeably prickly and block-happy when his own work is the target.

preoccupations 558 words

Macroeconomic data is the spine of everything here: he live-posts CPI, PPI, non-farm payrolls, JOLTS, ISM, Conference Board and UMich sentiment, jobless claims, and Fed decisions in all-caps breaking format, then immediately interprets them, with a signature fixation on the 52-week moving average of non-seasonally-adjusted initial jobless claims as his personal tell. He repeatedly reopens the question of what actually caused the 2021-23 inflation and what brought it down, explicitly inviting takes, and argues the corollary that full employment and hot demand — not layoffs or recessions — are what force productivity-enhancing investment; he rejects the premise that business cycles and recessions are natural or necessary. Fed independence, dissent norms, the dot plot, and Powell's data-dependence paralysis occupy him heavily from 2024 onward, sharpening into constitutional argument (the Fed sits in Congress's branch, not the executive) once Trump moves on Lisa Cook and nominates Warsh. Tariffs dominate April 2025 and after, approached through concrete channels — Alcoa's job math, apparel sewing capacity, PC gaming margins, Canada retaliation as domestic political necessity — rather than theory. AI is the other giant subject and evolves from casual ChatGPT use to genuine obsession: he builds Havelock AI, a BERT classifier detecting "orality" in text, and posts about ELO tournaments, training data, and model comparison constantly; he insists LLM prose is genuinely bad and getting worse, is skeptical that "AI literacy" is a skill, and pushes back equally on doomers and on people who dismiss the tools. That project flows directly from a real intellectual obsession with media ecology — Walter Ong, McLuhan, Meyrowitz, Postman — which he uses as a master key for social media, grift culture, the collapse of the adult/child distinction, and political style. Crypto is a standing target: he argues the only realized use case is gambling, mocks the Strategic Bitcoin Reserve as incoherent because the government has no BTC-denominated liabilities, needles maxis with the observation that Dogecoin outperformed Bitcoin from 2014-2021, and builds an elaborate "rent paid by transactors to HODLers" model to show a valuation framework is possible. Housing he approaches almost always through financing and credit constraints rather than zoning — repeatedly citing Kevin Erdmann and his bandmate's work — and he is skeptical of YIMBY messaging that picks fights over beloved gardens and churches. Recurring smaller loops: energy (shale's unheralded boom, nuclear's financing problem, offshore wind's collapse, uranium, helium), China's industrial policy and internal party history (he reads Deng and Xi Zhongxun biographies and cites them constantly), freight and the trucking driver-shortage debate, restaurant and retail unit economics as clean macro reads, prediction markets as a news-consumption tool whose real test is exploitable mispricing, MMT and Warren Mosler, private credit stress, multi-strategy hedge fund comp structures, country music and his band Light Sweet Crude, Moby Dick, daily Tradle, and a running gag about "the net present value of all future S&P 500 free cash flows."

positions 704 words

Describes himself, half-ironically and half-seriously, as "a journalist, no ideology or bias," and genuinely withholds verdicts on contested policy ("I obviously have no views on whether real deficit reduction is a good idea") while holding hard positions on the mechanics beneath policy. Believes tight labor markets, not technological breakthroughs, are the real historical driver of productivity — booms force firms to invest in efficiency and crowd out businesses dependent on cheap labor, while recessions let companies hire cheaply and remove that pressure — and rejects the premise that recessions are natural or cleansing, calling the physiological analogy unconvincing and treating idle labor as modern economies' most recurring failure. Argues official economic anecdote-gathering is structurally biased toward management: the Beige Book and regional Fed surveys collected employer complaints about "unqualified workers" even in 2011-2013 at 8% unemployment, so we need "a worker's Beige Book." MMT-adjacent without claiming the label: the US has no reason to ever default, the debt ceiling is a problem of legal creativity (mint the coin), "anything we can actually do we can afford," and the binding constraint today is real resources, not spending authorization — which he says means consistent MMTers should not be perma-doves right now. On 2021-23 inflation he repeatedly reopens the question of causation, leans toward supply disruption and "seller's inflation" over pure overstimulation, and thinks the disinflation without recession should have raised central bank prestige rather than lowered it. Wants the Fed cutting when labor softens, criticized Powell for anchoring on "2019 levels" instead of trajectory, proposed a "labor market put," and treats loss of Fed independence as the era's biggest tail risk. On crypto: elaborately and consistently deflationary — the only realized use case is gambling and regulatory evasion, the 2020-22 critique of DeFi was "100% correct," Bitcoin is "three tech stocks in a trenchcoat," a Strategic Bitcoin Reserve is incoherent because neither the government nor the private sector has BTC-denominated liabilities, ETF success for crypto twitter means only "number go up," and if you entered in 2015 you should have bought Dogecoin. Constructs his own valuation frame anyway: HODLers as landlords, transactors renting block space, so you could model implied future "rent." Thinks mass equity ownership is the underrated impediment to any abundance, industrial-policy, or Made-in-America agenda, because "stock vigilantes" won't tolerate high investment intensity and price wars — the stock market is now the economy. YIMBY-sympathetic but insists financing, not just zoning, is the binding constraint (endorses Kevin Erdmann's argument that credit rules block starter homes), and finds it politically self-defeating for YIMBYs to make their brand about demolishing a beloved church or garden. Wrote a 3,300-word review of *Abundance* wanting to believe it, reading it as a call for normie liberals to adopt a materialist rather than identitarian lens, but unsatisfied by its silence on industrialization and finance. On tariffs: "some tariffs (on my competitors) are fine, but the tariffs on my suppliers have to go," and repeatedly presses supporters to say what "success" would look like given China's cost advantage and the time required to build replacement capacity. Thinks restrictions on gambling function as a de facto safety net cutting off left-tail outcomes for addicts, that the Pelosi insider-trading obsession is cope from people she out-trades, that taxation is legitimate and lower taxes may just mean lower revenue, and that since land is finite, ultra-wealthy accumulation does constrain everyone else. "Pro-nuclear" means nothing to him unless it means supporting the public balance sheet and electricity-market reforms that make nuclear financeable. On AI he holds both sides: uses it daily, built a linguistics classifier with Claude Code, defends its research and coding utility, and simultaneously defends the cynics, insists LLM prose is bad and noticeably so, notes that framing AGI as a Manhattan Project guarantees public money flows to private hands, and read the open-weights letter as everyone in tech finally admitting "Lina Khan was right." His deepest intellectual commitment is media ecology — Ong, McLuhan, Postman, Meyrowitz — holding that political structure is downstream of dominant media technology, that social media has returned us to conditions of orality (agonistic, formulaic, unabstracted), and that this, not moderation policy or misinformation, explains open grift, collapsed shame, and the collapsed distinction between adults and children.

causal_models 515 words

Media technology is his prime mover: he holds that political structure is always downstream of the dominant communications medium, and reaches for Walter Ong, McLuhan, Postman, Havelock and Meyrowitz before reaching for ideology, algorithms, or content moderation — wokeness, open grift, the collapse of the adult/child distinction (month-long adult Halloween), Trump's perceived authenticity (front-stage/back-stage consistency), and the "viral" as a marketing pitch are all treated as symptoms of a return to orality, not as things anyone chose. In macro he is a demand-side heterodox thinker: tight labor markets and hot economies cause productivity investment and force out firms dependent on cheap labor (recessions do the opposite), the 2021-23 inflation was mostly supply and transitory rather than Fed-defeated, deficits may be why things are good rather than a looming problem, and default is a legal question with zero economic risk. He rejects the premise that business cycles are necessary, naming the "recessions are cleansing" intuition as an unexamined analogy from physiology. His single most distinctive claim is that mass equity ownership — not regulation, not unions, not zoning — is the binding constraint on abundance, industrial policy, and reindustrialization, because "stock vigilantes" punish high investment intensity and price wars; he cites China's subordination of shareholders and provincial fiscal competition as the counterfactual, and notes Tesla and SpaceX built in California to argue the bottleneck is financial rather than regulatory. He extends this to housing, repeatedly favoring Kevin Erdmann's credit-restriction thesis and his bandmate's work on developer financing over pure zoning explanations, and to nuclear, where he insists the impediment is electricity market structure and the government's unlimited balance sheet, not endangered bees. Public-sector understaffing (permitting delays at Wendy's, restaurant openings) is a real economic bottleneck. He is systematically suspicious of employer testimony: official anecdotal data has a management-side bias, firms complained of skills shortages at 8% unemployment, and he wants a "worker's Beige Book." Prices get explained through specific supply mechanisms — seller pricing power, shelter, memory chips, bird flu, hazelnut supply, declining investment in structurally-shrinking commodities — rather than generalized monetary blame. Markets are informationally efficient enough that your first assumption should be that you're wrong and your second that you can't profit anyway; "the algos did it" is incoherent because algos were built by people with money at stake; the Pelosi insider-trading theory is cope from people she out-traded. He prefers boring incentive explanations to conspiracies (RTO mandates aren't a Vornado plot). On AI he offers a mechanism rather than a vibe: coding fell first because it has a huge public corpus plus instant feedback loops, so other white-collar work is further off; regulated industries will lag because they need interpretability; the arms-race framing guarantees public money flows in. He credits luck openly for his own path and for insurers' loss streaks, and is unusually insistent that multiple competing causal stories can coexist — hindsight is not 20/20, and merely describing what happened accurately is rare enough to be valuable.

temperament 420 words

Epistemically he runs on questions rather than claims: an enormous share of his output is genuine interrogatives — "What's the best counter-argument?", "Can someone explain this to me?", "Is that true?", "What am I missing here?" — posed without embarrassment about ignorance, including on topics he covers professionally. He declares neutrality constantly and semi-ironically ("I'm a journalist, no ideology or bias"; "I have no view on whether X is a good idea, I'll leave that to the economists"), and the disclaimer is half joke, half actual operating rule: on empirical matters (a data print, a price, what a survey says) he is flat and declarative, and on normative ones he retreats to "that's for other people." He argues almost never from principle and almost always from data, from analogy (bitcoin priced as rent paid by transactors to holders; restaurant markups as a model for capacity constraints), and from following an argument one step past where his interlocutor stopped — "ok, but then what?", "couldn't that logic cut the other way?" He concedes readily and cheaply — "Alright, I'll delete it, probably not fair," "Ok fair," "I see what you're saying now," "yeah, this is 100% fair" — and prizes public error-tracking in others, praising a writer for admitting an old argument was "empirically false." What he will not concede is being misquoted: when contradicted he becomes precise and litigious, demanding people point to where he said the thing, repeating "I literally never said that," "when did I say scary?", and refusing to let a paraphrase stand. He grows visibly irritated at motivated reasoning, at people who won't reason past the first-order effect, and at what he labels "cope," "charlatans and knaves," "complete gibberish"; the exasperation is intellectual rather than tribal. Humor is deadpan and structural — recurring bits (the mock-solemn "net-present value of all future free cash flows," "nobody is more pro-X than me, but...", pretending to have arrived at a conclusion by heroic independent reasoning) plus steady self-deprecation about his own posting, his voice, his obsessions. He has an admitted itchy block finger and occasional bouts of moralizing where he digs in for a full day while insisting he isn't worked up, then later mocks himself for it. He deliberately reads books that contradict his priors and asks to be praised for it. Emotionally he is sunny, effusive with credit to collaborators, easily delighted by small things, and allergic to grand pronouncements — offered a moment for a sweeping thesis, he instead posts a numbered list of unresolved tensions.

Prizes people who have memorized specific figures, dates and names, having concluded that the most interesting thinkers are invariably the ones carrying details in their heads; prizes equally the person who has spent years staring at one obscure public dataset nobody else reads, and recommends that as a career path. Respects making a falsifiable call, respects more the person who returns to say the old argument was empirically wrong, and openly enjoys arguments that flatter nobody's coalition — "AI will help the middle class" is endorsed partly on aesthetic grounds because both the tech and anti-tech factions have to reject it. Treats data amputation as hygiene failure: strip out tech to declare the market weak, or brandish U6 as "the real unemployment rate," and you are a charlatan. Detests the 3D-chess reading of policy chaos, the podcast-billionaire macro monologue, and the engagement-bait genre where the poster feigns naivety about an obvious stunt so he can post "signs of the top." Bristles at strawmanning and answers it by demanding, repeatedly and flatly, to be shown where he said the thing. Refuses to state policy positions and performs "I'm a journalist, no ideology or bias" as a running bit that is also sincere, which makes him allergic to being assigned a side. positions 672 words

He is a Portuguese anti-EU nationalist whose politics run through a single load-bearing claim: social reality is reflexive, so control of shared language and common belief is upstream of the maintenance and division of power — "the map *is* the territory" for anything social, and a king is king only insofar as everyone believes everyone believes he is. From this he derives that kings/sovereigns exist to solve coordination stalemates (drive left or right), that Plato's Noble Lie is a real and necessary technology, and that Elon opening freedom of speech is a genuine Chesterton's-fence gamble because noble lies have been the primary mechanism of societal coordination. He is against Freedom as a stated value, noting that the same people worship it and the Rule of Law, which is precisely the curtailment of it. On immigration he insists the analysis is not racist but structural: fertility collapse plus capital's demand for cheap labor plus parties naturalizing future voters equals privatized gains and socialized losses — the Portuguese left populist party removed the work-contract requirement, and he claims two million entered a country of ten million; he holds that stable demographic make-up, immigration, and democracy are a choose-two-of-three, that more dimensions of difference means lower social trust, and prefers "meta-homogeneous" places aesthetically. He tracks remigration in Sweden, Denmark, Ireland and Austria as inevitable, catalogues Romania's annulled election, Germany floating an AfD ban, Le Pen barred, and Polish arrests as a "state of play" in which Western European legitimacy comes only from the manipulation of procedural outcomes. He is a free-speech absolutist who treats "hate speech," "uncontrolled migration," "misinformation," and "fact-checking" as engineered phrases and beachheads — "my brother you are the cognitive security hole" — and celebrates Community Notes to the point of arguing you should now amplify misinformation so it gets corrected. Democracy for him is a scissor-statement engine that "takes the mean," where polarization is a feature because participation legitimizes the system, and where propaganda is not evil but a neutral map-alignment tool; America's is simply the best in the world, so other countries copy it rather than making bootleg versions, which is how Portugal ended up with LGBTQ+ ideology, hate speech laws, and DEI it never generated. He holds that being left-wing is the default for literate people because left ideas *are* nicer, and only real-world experience makes you right-wing — which means a society with enough right-wingers has already failed at insulating its members from reality. He offers a deflationary left-wing reading of right-wing conspiracy theories (Great Replacement is capital importing cheap labor; race-mixing is identity destabilization for market purposes), argues capitalism must dissolve identity to reassign and resell it, and holds a slavery thesis in which mass literacy made mind-reformatting cheaper than body-constriction, fiat money is itself a form of enslavement, and elites will push explicit population reduction once robotics is cheap. He despises schooling as twenty years of buckbreaking that fills heads with plug-and-play empty symbols and frees parents to work. causal_models 561 words

Treats social reality as constituted rather than described: a king is king only insofar as everyone believes everyone believes he is, which makes metapolitics — the shared imaginarium — strictly upstream of politics, and makes control of language the primary lever on reality. From this follows his signature move: coordination-game explanations for institutions. Driving left or right doesn't matter as long as everyone picks the same, so societies need a Sovereign to be the Schelling point; gods are "kings of kings" running the same trick one level up; monotheism is that trick escalated again; the Noble Lie is load-bearing, and Elon opening the "noble lies" fence is a Chesterton's-fence gamble. Naming is the other master mechanism — calling it "woke" was a tactical error because a name creates a Schelling point to coordinate against a phenomenon, so there is an "arbitrage window" between a thing existing and being nameable, and euphemism treadmills ("homeless"→"unhoused") are arms races between advocates wanting closeness and a public wanting distance. He locates the vulnerability precisely: people hold a false Augustinian folk theory of language in which words are stickers on jars, and that gap is exploited as "semantic arbitrage" via redefinition, category-boundary warfare ("you ain't Black"), and labels that bake in judgments. Prefers selection to design: military discipline is discovered scalable coordination technology, not aesthetics; social media reinvented 1980s WWE by convergent evolution under identical pressures; legible bad actors get ousted so only subtle ones remain, which is why gossip-as-triangulation is defensively necessary; the purpose of a system is what it does. In psychology and health he is aggressively physiological — carnivore/keto cleared his psoriasis and mood where years of psychological work failed, forced eggs at breakfast killed morning anxiety, CO2 buildup causes violent dreams, gut bacteria manufacture cravings — and generalizes it as "remove foot from brake, don't add gas." Suffering is caused by identification and craving, not circumstance: tension/resistance is what hurts, self and wanting co-arise, and the fix is dropping doing rather than adding intention. His own pet mechanism is subordination: everything from dancing to meditation to the burned-out executive is explained by what you are currently subordinated to, and modernity's meaning crisis is that we subordinated everything to Man and are now subordinated to nothing. Capitalism is a solvent that must dissolve identity so it can reassign it faster (bodybuilding forums leaked first because bodybuilders were already a fabricated identity; mental illness becomes identity once traditional bonds are gone; the economy sells what you want rather than what you like, asymptoting to brainrot). On migration he explicitly rejects racial-animus explanations as unnecessarily complex and reaches instead for fertility collapse plus privatized gains and socialized losses plus parties farming naturalized voters, and concludes you can pick two of stable demographics, immigration, and democracy. Democracy itself he models as a scissor-statement engine where polarization is a feature because participation legitimizes the system, and where politicians never predict but hyperstition. He notes his own drift from conflict theory toward structural tragedy — "propaganda is neutral," "there is no way to neutrally map the social world" — while still insisting mistake theory is often installed in victims to keep them self-blaming. Residual root causes he won't reduce further: care ("Elon is effective because he cares more"), agency (obtainable only via trauma plus luck or proximity to high-agency people), and luck/timing dressed up post hoc as skill.

temperament 431 words

Writes at compulsive volume and treats publication as thinking rather than as reporting a conclusion — "I'm never trying to say the Correct thing, I'm just trying to say the Next thing," and explicitly asks to be read as a running series rather than a set of claims, which functions both as genuine epistemic humility and as a standing exemption from being held to anything. Certainty is signalled typographically: the moment of insight arrives in all-caps, in strings of exclamation points sometimes running to hundreds of characters, in 🤯 and "(!!!!!)", so the loudest sentences are reliably the newest and least tested ones. Hedges cluster in exactly the opposite place — around direct experience reports and contemplative claims, where the register drops to "help me understand if I'm talking out of my ass," "I might be wrong," "please correct me." Evidence is overwhelmingly first-person phenomenology and analogy: a felt shift, a dream, a sensation during a sit, a metaphor to handstands or bicycles or snowglobes or microscopes, then generalization to everyone. Concedes small points instantly and warmly — "oh, mb," "fair enough," "this is a good point, thank you," "you perfectly got it" — and thanks correctors with real appetite; concedes almost nothing when the challenge is framed as a challenge, at which point the disagreement gets rediagnosed psychologically as projection, ego defense, trigger, or the interlocutor being at a lower developmental stage, a move deployed often enough that it works as a general-purpose immunity. Oscillates hard between grandiosity ("I'm very very smart," "expert navigator of the landscape of all possible takes," "I'm so goated") and abasement ("I'm not a very smart man," "I've been a fool," "very unwise"), sometimes in the same week, and the swing is itself narrated as growth. Humor runs on 4chan greentext self-mockery, deliberate cringe, provocation posted knowing it will start a fight, and jokes that puncture his own pretension a beat before someone else can — the shitpost is protective armor as much as it is play. Impatient in dispute: issues ultimatums ("Brother. Last chance to answer the question"), tells people to stop wasting his time, dismisses whole classes of interlocutors as midwits or vibes-only thinkers, and is visibly stung by being misread but frames the sting as an observation about others' cognition. Tracks his own reactivity as data, reports it dropping, and takes the drop as evidence for the practices that produced it. Underneath the bombast there's a persistent tender register — grief for his younger self, gratitude posts, unembarrassed declarations of love — delivered with the same absence of irony as the boasting.

affinities_aversions 471 words

Gravitates hard toward people who have paid for their knowledge in experience and can point at the referent rather than the word for it — meditators who make fine phenomenological distinctions between jhana levels, coaches who've resolved bottlenecks in hundreds of clients, the artist who kept his style whether four people or two thousand were in the audience — and toward anyone willing to deliver candid, costly feedback about a blindspot at no benefit to themselves, which he explicitly wishes had been done to him when younger. Rates people by checking their old takes on subjects he knows intimately: if they inferred correctly without the relevant experience, he trusts their process everywhere else. Loves shitposting, memes and trolling as genuine art forms, loves naming and coinage ("naming is a lost art"), loves gossip reframed as triangulation against subtle bad actors, and loves the person who reads something and simply acts on it — that being, in his telling, his only superpower. Especially venomous toward two adjacent species: the person who uses spirituality as attire — worshipping enlightenment in word while expelling anyone actually realized — and the self-identified artist whose objections to AI are transparently ego-maintenance dressed in whatever the community already codes as Bad. Political content is real and abundant but arrives overwhelmingly as reactive one-liners, quote-tweet commentary, and gestures at screenshots I cannot see; his positions on immigration, EU governance, democracy, and Portuguese politics are stated often enough to be confident about, but he almost never builds a sustained argument for them the way he does for coordination games or the picture theory of language, so the politics paragraph reflects assertion frequency rather than reasoning depth. Contemplative practice is by far the best-covered domain — hundreds of posts with dates, durations, techniques, teachers, and phenomenological reports — but all of it is self-report with no external check, and his claims about his own suffering dropping, reactivity collapsing, and stream entry cannot be corroborated from the text. preoccupations 541 words

Meditation dominates — not as lifestyle but as an engineering problem he narrates in public over years: 1-2 hour determination sits, involuntary kriyas running root-to-crown, Culadasa's *The Mind Illuminated* as primary text, TWIM vs metta vs "do nothing," Loch Kelly's "unhook local awareness" pointers, Shinzen, Adyashanti, Frank Yang, Daniel Ingram, and above all Jhourney retreats (which he shills with a discount code and calls "the most important secret in the world"), reporting jhana attainment, one cessation, a suffering ceiling "hard-capped at 75%," reactivity dropping until he "shoots blanks" trying to get angry at a rude stranger, and a recurring worry that the same drop makes him a worse poster and less able to enjoy flexing in a mirror; he builds his own vocabulary for it — "subordination" (what you're object to vs. subject over), "grounding," "non-doing" as ceasing rather than adding intention, "opening awareness" as dropping mental pressure rather than doing anything. Running second, and eventually consuming him as a book/Substack project, is language as weapon: rectification of names, the Augustinian/"picture theory" folk model of words as stickers on jars and how its wrongness is the attack surface, disparagement-by-neologism (Jhanabro, Doomer, Karen, techbro), the "arbitrage window" between a phenomenon existing and a name for it reaching critical mass (Woke Capital, YooKay), euphemism treadmills for the homeless, the Noble Lie, kings-as-coordination-devices and Schelling points, common belief vs. common knowledge, reflexivity (statements about social reality being performative, not descriptive), and "the map is the territory" for social facts. Third is diet and body, almost entirely carnivore/keto evangelism — psoriasis 90% cleared in a month, mood "shot through the roof," smelling his urine for ketones, no more normal bowel movements, later flirtations with Ray Peat, seed oils, Ayurveda, plus a long-running self-critique of having trained 25+ hours a week since fifteen while eating trash and mistaking "add gas" for "take foot off brake." Fourth is European decline read through Portugal specifically: the Socialist government importing Cuban/Brazilian/Cape Verdean/Timorese labor and granting one-year residency to any Portuguese speaker, 60% of Portuguese dependent on the state making reform "a democratic impossibility," ECB money-printing and the Cantillon effect, the Iberian blackout, EU comms surveillance, Romania's annulled election, AfD, and the flat claim that stable demographics, immigration, and democracy are a pick-two. Fifth is TPOT/Twitter as its own object — likes-guys, Elonbux payouts, viral mechanics, meme theft norms, the community archive, an extended public accusation that "Vivid Void" is a serial DM-leaker and mob-starter, and the thesis that TPOT is "anti-spiritual," worshipping spirituality in word while expelling actually realized people in deed. Recurring smaller obsessions: Ted Lasso seasons 2–3 as his canonical case study of ideology destroying a show (invoked repeatedly, with a full inventory of the retconned gay/poly/interracial plotlines); gossip as legitimate "triangulation" against subtle bad actors who cut horizontal communication; projection and ego-defense as the real content of nearly all online replies; Type 1/Type 2 errors as why both sides of any self-help discourse are correct; the midwit curve reinterpreted as a self-awareness curve; women as the drivers of politics and consumption, plus crude gender bits alongside earnest claims that women want to be seen rather than understood; and, latterly, AI as an economic substitute for human conversation that will make talking to people a luxury good.

positions 620 words

Portuguese, and everything political runs through that: he holds that the EU's genius is that Brussels is too complicated for smart people to understand, so decision-makers are insulated from the consequences of their decisions, that the ECB prints money and then "generously" gives it back to countries as a fractal Cantillon-effect theft, and that the EU's October client-side comms scanning is the same censorship beachhead as "age verification" and "hate speech is not free speech" — engineered phrases whose acceptance does the argumentative work. On migration he explicitly refuses the racial explanation as unnecessary ("I like non-racist explanations just because they're simpler"): fertility collapse makes importing people the easy fix, the left populist party changed Portuguese law so migrants arrive without a work contract because naturalization means future votes, and businesses get cheap labour — privatized gains, socialized losses, kicking the can to the future. His central formulation is that a country can have a stable demographic make-up, or immigration, or democracy, but only two of three; he values demographic stability instrumentally, because more dimensions of difference means more avenues for distrust means lower social trust, and he cites Vilnius (purses left unattended on the dancefloor, unstaffed shops) against a Portugal he says is going from one of the safest countries in the world to dangerous. He thinks democracy requires propaganda to manufacture legitimacy, is structurally a scissor-statement engine, that polarization is a feature because participation legitimizes the system, that modern democracy shifted the power meta to "how good are you at running civilian psyops," and that with over 60% of Portuguese dependent on the State, reform is a democratic impossibility; he catalogues Romania's annulled election, AfD ban talk, Le Pen's barring and Polish arrests as one "State of play." He defends propaganda anyway — it is performative utterance larping as declarative sentence, aimed at aligning maps, and it is the only mechanism by which social reality (kings, money, legitimacy) is constituted at all, since for social reality the map is the territory; hence his repeated worry that Elon opening the Chesterton's fence on Noble Lies is a gamble with no idea what follows. He is maximally pro-free-speech and hostile to "misinformation" framing ("my BROTHER YOU ARE THE COGNITIVE SECURITY HOLE"), argues you should amplify misinformation so Community Notes can sting people's egos, and thinks American cultural imperialism could turn net positive if what it exports is free-speech norms rather than the "hate speech" category Americans invented. He loves America the country and people, loathes the US government, deep state and foreign policy, and holds that America makes the world's best propaganda so every other country now piggybacks on it rather than making bootleg versions — hence hyperreality and everywhere becoming San Francisco. He was in LessWrong/EA, now recoils from LessWrong, thinks pause-AI people would rather die than actually try to win, calls alignment "speaking power to truth," despises utilitarians whose neutral calculations always land on AI child porn and never on castrating pedophiles, and treats infohazards as literal — refusing to run certain thought experiments and calling one prominent account's work "structurally Evil, and at a scale." He argues violence is not a priori bad, that rule of law just is the curtailment of freedom and someone maximally free is a criminal, that schooling exists to fill heads with dead men's symbols you can plug-and-play later, that losing religion lost us the referent for "bad" so people substitute "illegal," and that gossip is good because subtle bad actors survive by cutting horizontal communication. Over time he explicitly migrates from conflict theory to tragedy — "this is deliberately adversarial" to "this is a structural necessity" — while still asserting the elites are evil and the food supply is poisoning us.

causal_models 546 words

Explains nearly everything through reflexivity: in social domains belief is constitutive, so a king is king only insofar as everyone believes everyone believes he is, which makes metapolitics — the shared imaginarium — strictly upstream of politics, and makes controlling language a way of controlling reality; he ends a sixteen-post series by declaring "the map is the territory" for social reality, and treats the folk/Augustinian picture theory of language (words as neutral stickers on pre-existing jars) as the load-bearing mass error that creates the attack surface for semantic arbitrage — renaming, boundary-shifting, definition-swapping — and an "arbitrage window" during which a phenomenon operates unimpeded because it has no name (his worked examples are @WokeCapital manufacturing the term "Woke," the "YooKay" account attempting the same now, and the euphemism treadmill as an endless arms race between advocates supplying neutral terms and stigma reattaching). Second favorite mechanism is coordination: kings, gods, monotheism, Noble Lies, military discipline, human sacrifice, and the sovereign are all rediscovered solutions to left-or-right driving problems, valuable precisely because their contingency stays hidden; his fear is that Elon and universal-acid transparency are tearing down that Chesterton's fence. He has explicitly migrated from conflict theory toward tragedy — "all my updates in this area have been in going from 'this is deliberately adversarial' to 'this is a structural necessity'" — reaching for selection effects and convergent evolution before intent: only bad actors good at hiding survive community ousting, so all remaining bad actors are subtle; social media reinvented 1980s WWE because the selection pressures were identical; crazy people are overrepresented in content production, normies mimic, repeat. Yet he keeps conflict theory as a therapeutic tool, arguing some people are harmed and then installed with mistake theory plus an ugh field so they blame themselves forever and never heal. Materially he is a hard reductionist about the psychological: years of psychological intervention did nothing for his morning anxiety and eggs for breakfast fixed it; carnivore cleared 90% of his psoriasis and reorganized his whole mind-space; CO2 causes violent dreams; gut bacteria manufacture cravings; the correct move is almost always removing the foot from the brake (inflammation, tension, resistance) rather than adding gas (training, effort, therapy). Downstream of meditation he treats craving, reactivity, and mental tension as the root of suffering and treats beliefs and behaviors as symptoms; identity is a stack of "subordination relationships" and the meaning crisis is Modernity succeeding at subordinating everything to Man and discovering that being subordinate to nothing feels like hell. Economically he reaches for the want/like split — the system reliably optimizes for the legible (want) at the expense of the nebulous (like), so its logical endpoint is brainrot — and for capitalism dissolving identity in order to reassign it faster. On immigration he prefers the boring mechanism: fertility collapse plus private gains from cheap labor plus socialized losses plus naturalized voters, and explicitly resists anti-white-racism explanations as unnecessarily complex. He treats mass literacy, mass media, and mass democracy as the change that scaled elite intellectual traps to whole populations, treats democracy as a machine that surfaces and monetizes scissor statements, and treats each marginal law as added negative space for arbitrage. Agency has exactly two causes: trauma plus luck, or proximity to high-agency people. Everything else is bottlenecked by care.

temperament 390 words

Writes at maniac volume and treats the timeline as a thinking-out-loud surface, posting the raw epiphany rather than the finished thought — a habit he defends explicitly ("I'm never trying to say the Correct thing. I'm just trying to say the Next thing"), which functions as a standing pre-excuse for being wrong. Default register is declarative and overheated: ALL-CAPS emphasis mid-sentence, strings of exclamation marks and 🤯, "I FIGURED OUT WHY," "this is JUST CORRECT," "I'm 100% sure." Certainty is domain-dependent — flat and unhedged on social, political, and institutional matters, genuinely inquisitive and deferential in contemplative and phenomenological matters, where he asks strangers to correct him ("help me understand if I'm talking out of my ass") and cheerfully accepts being outranked by teachers. Does concede, sometimes loudly and in public ("This is WRONG, see comments below"; "HELP I'm becoming less conspiratorial"), and treats a reversal as a status gain — proof of live updating — which means concession is itself a performance rather than a cost. Humor runs on greentext self-owns (">be me"), deliberate shitposting theorized as an art form, edgelord slurs used as in-group tokens and squeamishness tests, and jokes that are actually the argument's delivery vehicle; he provokes on purpose and then complains about the discourse he provoked. Reasons almost entirely by analogy, metaphor, reframe, and first-person n=1 report — his own body, his own meditation logs, his own moods as primary evidence — and rarely by data or citation; takes visible pride in rederiving ideas independently and visible deflation on learning someone got there first. Under contradiction he splits sharply: effusive gratitude and "thank you king" toward people he rates, and contemptuous dismissal toward those he doesn't ("stop wasting my time," "you're not convincing anyone, not even yourself"). His characteristic move against disagreement is psychologizing — the objection is recoded as ego defense, projection, trigger, or coercive tone, so the object level never has to be met. Swings between grandiosity (assassination risk, world-historical books, "my impact is measured in centuries") and ostentatious self-diminishment ("I'm not a very smart man," "I'm very smart but very unwise"), often within days. Exasperation is a load-bearing rhetorical mode: "IDIOTS EXIST," "it pisses me off," "Really? You can't reason this out?" Enthusiasm is his baseline and epiphany his native genre, produced at a rate that erodes the value of each one.

affinities_aversions 480 words

Gravitates to people who have paid the price in reps rather than in reading — the coach who has resolved a thousand other people's bottlenecks, the soccer player who can read intention on a pitch because he played, the gymbro doing amateur self-experimentation with peptides, even the flat-earther, because at least he runs the experiment — and treats "have you actually had the experience" as the only credential that matters, which is why he prizes writers who coin new words or use old ones inventively ("you don't use your own words so I know you don't have your own thoughts") and dismisses anyone who can only relate words to other words, LLM-style, as a product of schooling that taught them never to dereference. Loves candor, being told plainly he is wrong, and the specific pleasure of being handed an atomic, composable piece he can build a worldview from; loves systematizers who give exact referents for interior states, guided-pointer teachers who talk *through* you rather than *to* you, prolific breadth-first shitposters, meme-makers who can state a point so cogently it becomes a Schelling point, and people who name things so well the naming does the work. Cannot stand vibes-only dismissal — "it's creepy," "it gives me the ick," "problematic," which he notes implies a problem without ever specifying it — nor the person who replies "Correct." as if self-appointed arbiter, nor "take incontinence" from those who cannot hold an opinion in. Reserves real contempt for the midwit: not the ignorant, but the one who misjudges that he knows when he doesn't, seething and confidently reproducing cultural backwater as original thought. Detects and despises the ego-defense reply — coming in aggro while conspicuously refusing to actually engage, because engaging would destabilize the narrative they need to tile over whatever your post made them feel — and reads mansplaining as the same structure, someone using you as a fungible prop in their private psychodrama, which is why it feels like your individuality is being erased. Hostile to anyone who asks "is this acceptable?" before "is this true?", to spirituality worn as attire by big egoic accounts who trivialize enlightenment in deed while worshipping it in word, to self-styled artists whose objections to AI are transparently ego maintenance dressed in whatever slur the community already codes as bad, and to disparagement-by-neologism generally — "jhanabros," "doomers," "-bros" as a suffix — because it lets you dismiss a whole category without engaging one argument. Suspicious of anyone whose funding determines their conclusion, of institutions that produce anti-knowledge, and of "reasonable" people who start at the golden mean and never leave it, preferring the fool who starts at an extreme where error isn't catastrophic and slowly inches toward truth. Warms to conflict theory over mistake theory in cases involving subtle bad actors, defends gossip as triangulation, and thinks anyone who wants to ban it wholesale is suspect.

preoccupations 573 words

Adjacent and equally constant is the claim that everyone is dreaming while awake, that the self is just another object in experience, that "backfilled" memory manufactures the illusion of continuous presence (the saccade analogy, deployed at least four times), and that thoughts are bullshit generated to keep you from bare experience — demonstrated by noticing thoughts spin up in an MRI machine and stop when the noise stops. The second obsession is language as a weapon: a multi-year "Project"/book about how public language has been corrupted by accumulated intentional interference, built from Confucius's rectification of names, the Augustinian/picture theory of language as the folk error that creates the attack surface, semantic arbitrage windows between a phenomenon emerging and a name reaching critical mass (@WokeCapital, "YooKay," "ragebait"), disparagement-by-neologism ("Jhanabro," "Doomer," "Karen"), the euphemism treadmill as an arms race between advocates and stigma, and reflexivity — the map *is* the territory in social reality, kings exist only because everyone believes everyone believes they do, so propaganda is performative utterance disguised as description. positions 622 words

Politically he is a Portuguese right-populist with a metapolitical bent: he holds that democracy, immigration, and a stable demographic make-up are a "choose two of three" trilemma, that Portugal's migration surge was engineered by the Socialists to naturalize future voters while private business arbitraged cheap labor (private gains, socialized losses via social security), and that this makes reform "a democratic impossibility" since over 60% of Portuguese depend on the State. He insists his objection is non-racist — low European fertility plus the easy solution of importing people explains it without positing anti-white animus — while also saying anti-white racism was normalized and he's glad it may fizzle, and that a demographically stable population matters because more dimensions of difference means lower social trust. He loathes the EU ("my blood boils"), reading Brussels' complexity as a deliberate device to insulate decision-makers from consequences, and tracks the annulled Romanian election, the AfD ban proposals, and the Le Pen bar as evidence Western European leaders derive legitimacy from manipulating procedural outcomes rather than consent. He hates the American Deep State and foreign policy while calling the American country and people world-class, and treats US propaganda as the world's best — arguing it's now cheaper for foreign governments to copy and translate American propaganda than to make their own, so everywhere becomes San Francisco (this is his stated reason Hanania is "an idiot" for calling neoreaction a San Francisco governance ideology). On speech he is maximalist: he says calling the phenomenon "woke" was a tactical gift to opponents because naming created a Schelling point, that "hate speech is not free speech" and "uncontrolled migration" are engineered phrases that pre-concede the argument, that "misinformation" framing is a censorship beachhead and anyone saying "don't spread misinformation" is themselves the cognitive security hole, and that given Community Notes the correct move is to *share* suspected misinformation so it gets noted. He argues violence is not a priori bad, that Grey Tribe was right to sit out the escalation even as he understood Red Tribe engaging, and he came out explicitly "Against Freedom" on the grounds that Rule of Law just is the curtailment of freedom and that deliberately foregoing individual liberties (driving right rather than straight) is what makes coordination work. His central theoretical commitments are that kings exist to solve coordination games by being whoever gets to decide, that Plato's Noble Lie is load-bearing infrastructure Elon is gambling by dismantling, that social reality is constituted by common belief so the map *is* the territory in social domains, and that the folk/Augustinian "picture theory" of language — words as neutral stickers on pre-existing things — is a philosophical error that leaves people open to semantic arbitrage. He thinks left-wing ideas are the default for literate people because they're nicer and only real-world experience makes you right-wing, that being midwit (thinking you know when you don't) is the actual shape of the Dunning-Kruger curve, and he defends gossip as necessary triangulation against subtle bad actors who cut horizontal communication. On health he is a carnivore/keto evangelist who credits it with clearing 90% of his psoriasis and transforming his mood, is warm to Ray Peat's heresies, and treats seed oils and vegetable nutrient decline as real. On spirituality he holds Buddhism is a science miscategorized as a religion, that jhanas are the most important secret in the world but only suicidal or terminally suffering people should pursue enlightenment ("better not start"), that TPOT is actively anti-spiritual because big egoic accounts wear spirituality as attire and expel actually realized beings, and that occultism probably contains something real since intention and energy check out. He believes if magic works at all the powerful must already be using it, because competitive pressure dictates it.

causal_models 730 words

Social reality is his master mechanism: he holds that things like kingship, legitimacy, money, and political power exist only insofar as a critical mass believes that everyone believes they exist, which makes belief-alignment — not material force — the actual lever, and makes the metapolitical (the shared imaginarium) strictly upstream of the political (who holds and deploys power). From this he derives kings as Schelling points that solve coordination games with arbitrary equilibria (drive left or right), gods as "kings of kings" applying the same trick one level up, and Plato's Noble Lie as load-bearing rather than cynical — he explicitly worries that Elon and the general collapse of centralized narrative control are tearing down a Chesterton's fence. Language is the second master mechanism and it is causal, not descriptive: because nearly everyone holds an Augustinian "picture theory" (words are stickers on pre-existing jars), a gap opens between what language actually does and what people think it does, and that gap is the attack surface — he calls exploiting it "semantic arbitrage," and traces the arbitrage window from a phenomenon existing unnamed, through competing neologisms, to a name reaching critical mass and thereby creating the Schelling point that lets people finally coordinate against the thing (his worked examples: "woke" as a naming own-goal by its proponents, "Woke Capital," "YooKay," "Jhanabro," "Doomer," and the euphemism treadmill as a literal predator-prey arms race between fresh names and reattaching stigma). Third, he reasons in explicit selection terms almost everywhere: obvious bad actors get expelled by communities so only subtle, plausible-deniability bad actors remain (hence gossip-as-triangulation is prosocial); politicians are selected for loyalty first, and since most people are incompetent this filters for incompetence; engagement algorithms select categorical takes over nuanced ones, so the nuanced version of any argument already exists but is invisible; memes are selected for fitness in a memetic environment, not truth. Fourth, and startlingly for someone this verbal, he is a hard physiological reductionist about mind: carnivore/keto cleared his psoriasis and, he claims, changed "everything" about his mental life at once ("a new cluster in mind-space"); eggs at breakfast fixed years of morning anxiety that psychological intervention hadn't; CO2 saturation causes violent dreams; gut bacteria drive cravings; muscle bulk in damaged men is literal armor. He generalizes this into a signature causal shape — the fix is usually removing the foot from the brake, not adding gas; you don't "melt," you stop tensing; you don't add an intention to meditate, you stop the competing ones — and applies it to meditation, motivation, cold showers, pleasure, and psychotherapy alike. Reactivity and craving are his deepest root cause for individual suffering, with the corollary that if a choice shows up to you *as* a decision, the error is already upstream and "improving your decision-making" only compounds it. Economically he runs a want/like split: markets are high-modernist and optimize for the legible thing people want rather than the illegible thing they like, which asymptotes to addiction, brainrot, and porn-as-general-category (a shallow substitute you consume until you get the real thing); capitalism must dissolve identities so it can reassign them faster, with bodybuilders as first victims because their identity was already fabricated. On immigration and demographic change — a recurring subject — he pointedly refuses racial-animus explanations as unnecessarily complex and instead stacks structural ones: collapsing fertility, businesses arbitraging cheap labor while socializing losses onto social security, and parties naturalizing future voters; he treats "stable demography, immigration, democracy — pick two" as a hard constraint and Brussels' illegibility as the deliberate mechanism insulating decision-makers from consequences. He notes his own drift from conflict theory toward tragedy — "~all my updates have been going from 'this is deliberately adversarial' to 'this is a structural necessity'" — but retains conflict theory as a therapeutic tool, arguing that abusers install mistake theory plus an ugh-field in victims so they blame themselves forever and never heal. Agency he thinks has exactly two causes, trauma-plus-luck or proximity to high-agency people, and effort he thinks is downstream of care ("why is Elon effective — he cares more"). Finally, he treats Type 1/Type 2 error as a structural fact that generates most discourse: you cannot drive both to zero, so any categorical advice that saved one person by correcting their overshoot necessarily invalidates the person who just corrected the opposite overshoot, and the resulting mutual invalidation is what the algorithm amplifies.

temperament 429 words

Writes at a permanent pitch of revelation — the default register is the all-caps mid-sentence breakthrough, the parenthetical "(!!!!!)", the "OH", "Holy shit", "just figured out", "Omg just got it," delivered several times a day for years, often about a thing he announced figuring out eighteen months earlier. Certainty is loud but structurally provisional: he explicitly adopted "strong opinions, lightly held" from online and notes with amusement that it makes people think he never changes his mind when in fact he does so constantly, and he will post "This is WRONG, see comments below" directly above his own thread, or open with "I think I might be wrong???" mid-project. Hedging appears mostly as invitation — "help me understand if im talking out of my ass," "lmk if you find out," polls, crowdsourcing, tagging people to adjudicate — and he genuinely does update and thank people effusively when they land something. Self-assessment swings violently and without embarrassment between "I'm not a very smart man," "it took me YEARS to grok," "I'm slow but systematic" and "I'm very very smart," "I'm so goated," "my job? expert navigator of the landscape of all possible takes." Argument runs almost entirely on analogy, personal phenomenology, and structural isomorphism — noticing that two unrelated domains have the same shape is his signature move and functions as proof; n=1 bodily self-report is treated as strong evidence and generalized outward immediately. Humor is greentext ">be me," deliberate shitposting, pastiche, and constant self-mockery, and it does double duty as a pressure valve and as deniability for claims he half-means. He is highly reflective about his own conduct after the fact, writing long public post-mortems assessing whether he was effective, whether he should have escalated, what he learned. Under contradiction he splits: with people he respects he engages at exhausting length and often concedes; with people he doesn't he reaches for a ready psychological diagnosis — you're triggered, that's ego defense, that's projection, you're stabilizing a worldview — which conveniently makes disagreement evidence of the other person's malfunction rather than of error in himself. Irritation, when it breaks, is contemptuous and profane ("go fuck yourself," "stop being a fucking fag and debating online," "IDIOTS EXIST deal with it"), usually followed days later by a calmer reframing. He is unembarrassed by emotional exposure — reports crying, heartbreak, loneliness, dread, shame — and treats the ability to report these as itself a credential. Compulsively coins and renames things, is aggrieved when others' language is imprecise, and repeatedly frames his own decreasing reactivity as the proof that the whole project is working.

affinities_aversions 450 words

Gravitates hardest toward practitioners over commentators — the person who has actually done the thing and can point at the referent, versus the one who can only shuffle the vocabulary around it; his highest compliment is that someone made a distinction so fine he can feel it in his own experience, and his stock complaint about intellectual scenes is "there's no heavy lifting being done here." Loves coiners and namers, treats "naming is a lost art" as a creed, and rewards anyone who supplies an atomic, composable, load-bearing concept he can build a worldview from — he explicitly prefers "lower level takes since they are more composable." Warms to categorical, extreme, un-hedged formulations because he thinks the golden-mean take never spreads and never corrects anyone; correspondingly he sneers at people who start at a reasonable midpoint and never move from it, and at the reply that says "Correct." as though the replier were king of correctness. Adores voluminous self-exposure — writing every thought publicly, screaming into the void, shitposting, anon accounts as temperature-raisers on collective thought — and detests curation-as-cowardice, the person who only speaks once safe. Deep, persistent contempt for the midwit, deployed as a universal diagnostic: the one who mistakes knowing about for knowing, who thinks he knows when he doesn't, whose speech is "nails on a chalkboard." Equally contemptuous of vibes-only reasoning — "it's creepy," "it gives me the ick," "-coded," "reminds me of" — which he classes as associative thinking incapable of structural thought, and of the move that dismisses an idea by inventing a suffix-slur for its adherents rather than engaging the claim. Reflexively diagnoses aggressive-but-non-engaging replies as ego defense and projection: someone who came in hot but won't answer the question is stabilizing a narrative, not arguing, and he says so to their face. Cannot stand asking "is it acceptable" before "is it true," cannot stand "problematic" as a word that asserts harm without naming it, and cannot stand black-box pseudo-explanation — the doctor who says your bad breath is caused by halitosis. Reviles spirituality-as-attire, big accounts grifting on enlightenment while structurally expelling anyone who actually has it; also reviles the sanctimonious Buddhism-enjoyer as against the actually-realized person, whom he describes as the chillest guy you'll meet. Holds a live grudge against credentialed sycophancy — academics paid to reach a predetermined conclusion, NGOs funneling public money into self-serving comms — and against utilitarians whose neutral math conveniently lands on the permissive side. Retains a debt to formal rationalism while physically recoiling from its current aesthetic. Prizes non-reactivity as the actual mark of quality, and calls people who need everyone to co-sign their self-lie unpleasant because there is always an implied coercion in the room.

coverage 319 words
