preoccupations 465 words

Financial infrastructure mechanics dominate everything else: the plumbing of payments, deposits, and consumer credit, always approached the same way — take a behavior civilians read as malice or stupidity (overdraft fees, savings accounts paying nothing, banks closing accounts with no explanation, wire delays, checkout-page friction), then reconstruct the incentives, regulatory constraints, and org-chart pathologies that made it locally rational, usually concluding that the distributional analysis consumer advocates run is backwards. He returns obsessively to Reg E and its coverage of Zelle, to interchange (arguing at 31-tweet length against The Atlantic that the rich subsidize the poor, not the reverse), to ACATS and medallion signature guarantees, to AML/KYC as "a second intelligence-community-sized force of agents at banks," to the fact that friction in a process is often load-bearing because it forces a fraudster to lie to a bank and thereby manufacture the elements of wire fraud. Adjacent and nearly as heavy: crypto skepticism, with a specific and repeated thesis that Alameda's real business was "legal/compliance risk arb" — telling banks what they needed to hear on Tether's behalf — plus running takedowns of Michael Lewis for not knowing debt from equity, a two-year losing put position on MicroStrategy, and Binance as the industry's nominated sin-eater. Third is the Dangerous Professional method: how to escalate inside a bureaucracy, when you are talking to a persuadable human versus a state machine, who has "the button," why contemporaneous written logs terrify legal departments, and the observation that "this decision is final" is four words of no particular import. From roughly late 2024 onward, LLMs consume a growing share: not as an abstract AI-risk topic but as mundane utility — debugging resin prints, geolocating photos, drafting reimbursement memos, arguing with a model about the optics of a mirror, and eventually 162,000 lines of code in 24 days for a roguelike side project, framed as evidence that anyone still hedging on capabilities is misremembering their own 2015 skepticism. He repeatedly relitigates pandemic-era policy through VaccinateCA: California's inability to count vaccine doses, Newsom threatening licenses over queue-jumping, and explicit racial redlining of vaccine access, which he calls "cartoonishly evil" and insists nobody has paid for. Persistent secondary threads: class as an illegible-by-design sorting mechanism (bank regulators' CEO approvals as "a class check," the hoodie/dress arbitrage at First Republic, shadow org charts that lose power once mapped); illegal hiring discrimination in tech, citing the YouTube recruiting emails and WSJ corroboration at least six separate times as proof the industry openly wrote down unlawful plans; Japan-vs-US comparative bureaucracy delivered as dialogue with his wife Ruriko; small-business and bootstrapped SaaS economics via MicroConf; an annual exhortation to buy term life and own-occupation disability insurance; Factorio, Balatro, and D&D as recurring metaphor sources; and far-UVC as his pick for the most underrated technology of the century.

positions 753 words

Money is the unit of power and financial infrastructure is where society's real preferences get revealed, so most of his positions start there. Debt collectors, contingent on there being a dispute, violate the FDCPA "as close to 100% as makes no difference," not from malice but because incentives, operational constraints and engineering reality make compliance impossible. Credit bureaus did more for financial inclusion and against racial/religious discrimination in lending than every law combined; people who want to hobble underwriting are thinking magically, imagining that making good underwriting impossible produces no underwriting rather than handshake-and-last-name underwriting. He rejects the standard consumer-advocate scorecard: savings-account interest is the cheapest fee there is and falls mostly on richer retirees who are buying an "envelope" and don't care what it costs, while NSF and minimum-balance fees are what advocates should chase; overdraft fees descriptively subsidize people with student loans at the expense of people who didn't go to college; and The Atlantic's claim that interchange redistributes from poor to rich is straightforwardly wrong, since the rich pay more net interchange (citing OCC data showing both tails of the FICO distribution subsidizing the middle) and debit interchange is what buys unbanked and low-income Americans a checking account that isn't $12/month. Reg E applies to Zelle, full stop, and banks arguing that fraudulently-induced transactions fall outside it are simply wrong. Wire fraud operationally means any interstate crime involving money and electrons, and AML/KYC makes far more sense read as guaranteeing that most crime has all the elements of wire fraud sitting in one document at a bank than as interdiction; the resulting apparatus is a second, intelligence-community-sized force making transaction-level decisions, which he thinks the American polity would decline to buy if it were ever described in as many words — but he does not advocate ending it tomorrow. Debanking is real, predates crypto, and disproportionately hits people who will never read a compliance circular; Operation Choke Point genuinely happened and the postmortems were written by civil servants protecting other civil servants' pensions; "Choke Point 2.0" is an advocacy term crypto uses to launder unrelated grievances, and some crypto claims about it are straight-up invented. On crypto he is a die-hard skeptic: the slow databases were never the point, Tether is the conspicuously absent center of the FTX story, Alameda's real business was knowing what to tell U.S. banks and selling that as "legal/compliance risk arb," Binance practiced "Bond villain compliance," and Bitcoin treasury companies have reinvented exchange tokens (he held MSTR puts for two years and publicly booked the loss). Michael Lewis, he argues from the text, does not know the difference between an interest rate and carry, or debt and equity. On the pandemic he is furious and specific: California policy stripped licenses from doctors for delivering care, which caused doctors to destroy lifesaving medicine; medical redlining by zip code as a race proxy was actual policy, cartoonishly evil, and applauded by ethicists and the political class; being bad at counting is a policy choice, and California admitted its shot numbers were lies but couldn't say so publicly. He holds that the White House using AppAmaGooBookSoft as catspaws for speech restriction is unconstitutional point blank, that misinformation is a demand problem not a supply problem, and that you cannot sensibly demand someone believe no untrue things. He believes the law binds tech companies and that industry wrote down explicitly illegal hiring plans (YouTube being the public example) which will produce consequences. Build homes, or you will never be happy about who owns them; modest units anchor prices rather than crowding out better ones. Capitalism embeds an ethical system and capitalists earn their keep by telling founders their option value is zero and releasing them from obligation; index funds are among the best technologies finance ever produced; press the deploy button even when it disemploys heart surgeons or capitalists. Buy term life and own-occupation disability insurance while young and healthy — his annual exhortation. Credit cards are a good deal for disciplined people, roughly 2% back for ten minutes of work. He is bullish on AI to the point of saying there will be no non-AI SaaS companies, that computing is a meta-skill in the weight class of literacy, and that people should be careful about arbitrary protein synthesis while not building MiniTrue into every system. Institutional decisions labeled final are usually reversible; most invocations of "there's nothing we can do" are ad-libbed rather than authorized; and class functions specifically by resisting legibility, because being legible would destroy it.

causal_models 449 words

Explains outcomes structurally first and by character last: brokenness is almost always the equilibrium a system was configured to produce, not evidence of villains, so the question is never "who is evil" but "who has a button to do this, what are they scored on, and which competing societal demand won at the margin." Bank account closures, unhelpful phone reps, and payment systems with Reset next to Submit are all attributed the same way — the branch manager has the button and is measured on complaints while Tier One is measured on cases per hour; payments sits in an organizational backwater where no ambitious person can build a career, so nobody ever said "no, WTF, no." Treats legibility as a first-class causal force: securities losses get a Very Concerning Chart and loan losses of similar size do not, fraud is illegible while anti-fraud spend is legible, advocates attack savings-account interest because "savings" is in the name while ignoring NSF fees, and class functions precisely by resisting being made legible, since a written-down rule would destroy its power. Assumes cross-subsidy everywhere and that price is decoupled from cost — savings accounts are priced as envelopes, checking is free because debit interchange pays for it, the 680-FICO cohort is subsidized by both tails, and price-insensitive customers pay more, which is a fact about demand rather than a conspiracy. Believes friction is often load-bearing: it embeds proof of work, a velocity limit, or a deliberate tripwire, and AML/KYC is best understood not as interdicting crime but as guaranteeing all the elements of wire fraud sit in one bank document. Rejects both conspiracy and pure absolution — his stated test is that a real conspiracy would leave documents forthrightly stating the aim, and since it usually doesn't, "the usual structural reasons" win; but he insists scaled fraud is a business process requiring many uncorrelated screwups, and that specific humans with names commit crimes and write down mens rea. Holds that professed ethical frameworks are usually sincere and that sincere people do terrible things, so affinity-fraud-from-childhood explanations are bad priors. Regards some technologies as strictly dominant with no tradeoff — math, writing, credit bureaus, index funds, solar, far-UVC, LLMs — and thinks making good underwriting impossible yields bad underwriting, not none. Sees state capacity failures as human capital and metric-choice problems: you get the pathology your KPI predicts, pipelines must narrow somewhere, and nobody in a billion-dollar apparatus spent thirty seconds asking whether there are millions of pharmacies. Credits individual agency heavily as the missing input — one lynchpin person must hypercommit or an organization does nothing, caring is itself alpha, and reading more of the Internet than anyone thinks reasonable substitutes for credentials.

temperament 326 words

Writes with flat, high confidence and almost no throat-clearing, then bolts on precision retroactively: parentheticals that narrow a claim, corrections of his own phrasing mid-thread, and self-imposed disclaimers ("I am not a lawyer," "opinions are my own," "no strong opinion on legislation I learned about two minutes ago"). The signature device is the self-interrogation — he quotes an imagined skeptic ("Patrick, that seems idealized," "Patrick you were pretty sympathetic to regulators in your previous writing") and answers, which lets him pre-empt objections while appearing to concede ground he never surrenders. Certainty is graded and often numeric: he assigns probability mass, offers $50 charity bets with explicit resolution criteria and time horizons, pre-commits to updating on a date, and then actually returns to announce a loss and invite mockery. He docks himself points for miscalibration without prompting. Arguments run on mechanism and incentive first ("solve for the equilibrium"), documented artifact second, and firsthand operational anecdote third; he almost never argues from values alone, and when he does he flags it as an aesthetic preference others may not share. Contradiction produces "politely disagree" followed by a restatement of the causal chain rather than volume, though on a handful of subjects he drops the hedges entirely into flat authority — "Full stop," "That is simply not the risk the process is concerned with." Humor is load-bearing: deadpan understatement, absurd escalation, bracketed stage directions (*Lawyer: still dead*), and self-mockery about his own compulsions, all deployed to make tedious procedural material readable and to signal insider fluency. Anger is rare, specific, and recurrent on a few sore topics, where the register shifts abruptly to moral language and censored profanity. He extends conspicuous grace to individual low-level workers while being withering about the systems employing them. Delight is unembarrassed — "I love this stuff, how can you not," "pip pip," "Internet fist bump" — and he is comfortable saying "I don't know," "jury still out," and "I'd have to look that up."

causal_models 437 words

Explains nearly everything structurally: outcomes fall out of incentives, org design, and metrics rather than the moral fiber of participants. Debt collectors violate the FDCPA in essentially 100% of disputed cases not because they are villains but because incentives, operational constraints, and engineering realities make compliance impossible; payment systems are badly designed because they sit in an organizational backwater where no ambitious person can build a career, so nobody ever cared enough to say "No, WTF, no." His default move on a bureaucracy is "who has a button to?" — behavior is predicted by which role holds which authority and what that role is scored on (branch managers on sales targets plus complaint escalations, phone reps on cases per hour), not by empathy or malice. He treats organizations as state machines with formal transitions and no emotional response, and treats "this decision is final" as four words of no particular import unless spoken by someone who can commit the institution. Metrics are causally upstream of program pathology: California counted vaccine doses badly because counting wasn't important until Bloomberg published a league table, anti-fraud spend is legible while fraud is not, and "being bad at counting things is a policy choice." He is aggressively anti-conspiratorial as a first pass — education bureaucrats are not hypercompetent decades-long dissemblers, the plural of "idiosyncratic reasons" is "systemic reasons," and his stated test for a real conspiracy is whether documents forthrightly stating the aim exist — but he insists that when such documents do exist (Newsom's December 2020 pharmacist threat, medical redlining memos, YouTube recruiting emails directing racial exclusion), the written policy is the actual cause and must be named. Society, not banks, is the root cause of banking dysfunction: "society wants a complicated basket of goods from banks, some of those priorities compete, and the brokenness observed is us getting what we want." Friction is load-bearing and usually deliberate — proof of work, velocity limits, tripwires that force a fraudster to lie to a bank so wire fraud elements exist in one document. Technology is frequently just overpowered with no tradeoff (math, writing, credit bureaus, solar, LLMs), and information systems beat statutes: credit bureaus did more for financial inclusion than every law combined. Class and power are real causal forces that actively resist legibility because legibility would destroy their function; regulator language about "judgement and temperament" is a class check. He credits individual agency heavily — organizations don't decide, one lynchpin person hypercommits — and credits sincere belief as compatible with fraud rather than assuming long cons. Reads adverse selection, cross-subsidy, and solving-for-the-equilibrium into consumer finance disputes where advocates read predation.

temperament 378 words

Speaks almost entirely in flat declaratives, and treats hedging as a failure of nerve rather than a virtue — when uncertainty is genuine he quantifies it instead ("finger to the wind, 40% of that distribution," "95%, and if it slips, 80% known unknown") and then precommits to being scored later, publicly logging losing positions years afterward so onlookers can chortle on schedule. Disagreement is converted into a wager almost reflexively: $50, settlement to a charity of the winner's choice, you pick the odds and the resolution criteria, with a mirrored play-money market for anyone else who wants in. Concedes facts instantly and cheerfully — corrects his own dates, screenshots the file that proves he misremembered, docks himself a cookie for a miscalibrated prior — but almost never concedes the frame; contradicted, he restates the claim more narrowly and with more citation rather than softening it. Argues predominantly from mechanism and worked example, walking the reader through the incentives of each actor in a chain until the pathology falls out as arithmetic, with a recurring rhetorical device of interviewing himself in quoted skeptic voice ("You're not a lawyer, Patrick, how do I know—", "This seems unjust—", "That sounds mean") to preempt the objection and then answer it flatly. Ritual disclaimers are load-bearing and self-aware: opinions are his own, he's short no banks for social reasons, he holds homeopathic positions purely to have escalation rights, and there is a stated no-subtweet rule that he announces when breaking. Humor is deflection and rapport: elaborate absurdist bureaucratic dialogues, self-mockery about dumping character points into unmarketable skills, cheerful admission that he is a walking edge case, the pleasure of a rhyming aside dropped mid-explanation. Underneath the unruffled salaryman politeness — which he narrates as a split, the external courtesy over the internal screaming — sits real moral heat that occasionally punches through the register entirely, in short declaratives about things being wrong, counterproductive, and almost cartoonishly evil, and in the admission that certain professional experiences drove him near madness and sometimes slip out unbidden. Expresses genuine delight rather than weariness at procedural minutiae, calling out competence when he finds it and tipping his cap in public, and treats the discovery that a system works as newsworthy in the same breath as its failures.

affinities_aversions 416 words

Reveres the person who has actually done the job and can speak from mechanism rather than vibes — the compliance analyst who knows why the memo exists, the small-bank CEO who understands the FedWire interface, the barber who knows the industrial organization of his own chair, the GC who takes the customer aside for a hard private conversation — and treats "you can just Google it, read more of the internet than anyone thinks reasonable, call the pharmacies yourself, email the author" as the highest civic virtue, repeatedly praising people who confidently declare a specific thing a failure from a place of knowledge. Has an unusually specific hatred of institutions that refuse to keep a score, and a corresponding respect for anyone who publishes an honest postmortem or admits a supervisory failure. Warms enormously to the Matt Levine / Byrne Hobart mode — a wry, mechanism-first lens applied to any domain — and says openly he'd read a hundred more of them across energy, insurance, freight scheduling. Deeply allergic to the VC associate's apprenticeship-by-cold-email, the recruiter who can't do what the machine just did, the PM who fights over the 3% peanut content for a decade, the clerk who invents a rule on the spot and calls it final, and the phrase "there's nothing we can do." Detests moral posturing that has not priced its second-order effects — the advocate whose desired policy would end underwriting entirely, the medical ethicists who redlined vaccine access to thunderous applause — and reserves genuine venom for people who wrote down obviously illegal or lethal instructions and then claim nobody could have known. Prefers the person who says "I don't know, let me find out" and treats a well-calibrated probability as a moral act; will offer a $50 charity wager on the spot to anyone who states a strong claim, and considers refusing to be scored a tell. Practices a strict no-subtweet rule and dislikes reflexive cynicism, middlebrow dismissal, and status-defense dressed as principle, but has no patience whatsoever for the pretense that large organizations don't make decisions or that power isn't power.

preoccupations 536 words

Financial infrastructure occupies more of this sample than everything else combined, and almost always from one specific angle: the observed pathology is not villainy but the predictable output of incentives, org charts, IT constraints, and regulation, and the person explaining this is annoyed that both consumer advocates and industry critics keep getting the mechanism wrong. Recurring set pieces include Reg E's application to Zelle (repeated across at least six separate posts, always insisting banks are wrong to claim scam-induced transfers fall outside it), why savings accounts pay nothing (banks tested it, found consumers use them as "envelopes," and the fee falls mostly on retirees, unlike NSF and overdraft fees which advocates ignore), overdraft fees as a subsidy from non-college-graduates to student-loan holders with the mass affluent untouched, why interchange redistributes from rich to poor rather than the reverse (a 31-post rebuttal to an Atlantic piece, citing an OCC paper's net-interchange-by-FICO chart), ACATS and medallion signature guarantees, why $500M is easier to wire than $500k, debanking and SAR non-disclosure rules, and BSA/AML as "a second intelligence-community-sized force of agents at banks" that no polity would knowingly buy. Adjacent and nearly as frequent is the mechanics of extracting outcomes from bureaucracies: who has "the button," why phone support doesn't and a branch manager does, that "this decision is final" is four words of no import, that contemporaneously maintained timestamped logs terrify legal departments, and the specific script for calling a Congressional office (3 minutes 34 seconds, say "registered voter," cite the bill number). Crypto skepticism runs throughout, concentrated on Tether as the actual story everyone misses — he faults Zeke Faux's book for chasing travelogue and Michael Lewis's for not knowing debt from equity or carry from interest — plus Binance's "Bond villain compliance," Do Kwon's coconut line, and a two-year losing put position on MicroStrategy that he publicly marks to market. From mid-2024 onward LLMs become a dominant thread, always as mundane utility rather than futurism: debugging resin print failures, geolocating photos to 25 feet, expense memos, writing 162,282 lines of code in 24 days, "paraLLM construction" for dragnetting podcast transcripts in his SPLC reporting, and repeated insistence that people who dismiss the tools are the ones misinforming Washington. He returns compulsively to Japan/US institutional comparison — pension offices that pick up on the first ring, handwritten immunization booklets designed to be readable abroad, a doctor's visit costing $40 versus a database that won't admit he's a patient — usually staged as dialogue with his wife Ruriko ending in her bafflement. Persistent smaller obsessions: the VaccinateCA experience as proof that California literally couldn't count and that vaccine redlining by zip code was "cartoonishly evil" policy nobody has been billed for; YouTube's recruiters writing down explicitly illegal hiring plans, invoked at least five separate times as the canonical example that the industry broke employment law openly; an annual exhortation to buy term life and own-occupation disability insurance before you become uninsurable; class and legibility (the "Sort," shadow org charts, what a hoodie means at First Republic); small-business/bootstrapping economics and charging more; Factorio, D&D, and roguelikes as both hobby and metaphor; and his children's math homework, which he treats as a live front in a war against American pedagogy.

positions 872 words

Financial-consumer advocacy is his home ground and he draws its lines idiosyncratically: contingent on a dispute existing, debt collectors violate the law at a rate "as close to 100% as makes no difference," because incentives and operational realities make compliance impossible — but the credit bureaus, he insists, "did more for financial inclusion and decreasing racial and religious discrimination in lending than every law combined," and people who want to abolish good underwriting are thinking magically, imagining they'll get no underwriting rather than handshake-and-last-name underwriting. He repeatedly tells consumer advocates they are aiming at the wrong targets: savings-account interest is the cheapest fee in banking and falls mostly on price-insensitive retirees who want an "envelope," while NSF and minimum-balance fees actually bite; overdraft fees descriptively subsidize people who went to college at the expense of people who didn't, leaving the mass affluent untouched, and the Durbin debit interchange cap mechanically encouraged them, which banks warned about and nobody believed. He rejects The Atlantic's claim that credit card interchange redistributes from poor to rich, citing OCC data showing issuers earn positive net interchange from every FICO segment and that both tails subsidize the transitional middle, and notes debit interchange is what pays for free checking and Cash App. Reg E, he says flatly, absolutely covers Zelle including fraud-induced transactions, and banks arguing otherwise are very clearly wrong; wires are carved out, which is a different question. AML/KYC he reframes as making sense less as crime interdiction than as guaranteeing that a huge portion of crime has all the elements of wire fraud assembled in one document at a bank — wire fraud being operationally "any interstate crime involving both money and electrons" — and he notes the U.S. has stood up a second intelligence-community-sized force of bank employees making transaction-level decisions, which no configuration of the American polity would buy if proposed in those words, though he stops short of saying end it tomorrow. On debanking he splits the difference against everyone: Operation Choke Point was real and crypto describes it more forthrightly than civil-servant postmortems written by people whose colleagues' pensions were at stake; "Choke Point 2.0" is an advocacy label crypto attaches to anything it wants discredited, and crypto advocates have straight up invented claims; people were debanked in 2007 for demographic reasons and had no idea why; SAR confidentiality is a legal gag, and the brokenness is society getting the competing basket of goods it asked for. He is a die-hard crypto skeptic who thinks the slow databases were never the point: Alameda's real business was knowing what to tell U.S. banks to allay suspicion and facilitating Tether operations for a cut ("legal/compliance risk arb"); Bitcoin treasury companies have reinvented exchange tokens because assets flow to wherever the most non-recourse leverage bolts onto them; stablecoins' actual use case is recruiting replaceable proxies with clean track records to transact with banks; and he held OTM MSTR puts for two years, publicly precommitting to and then reporting the loss. He thinks Michael Lewis doesn't know debt from equity or carry from interest, and that the truth about FTX is worse than the government's filings, which are worse than the public narrative — while insisting skeptics underrate that professed ethical frameworks are often sincere, since "sincerely motivated and capable of acting badly" is not new. On housing: "If you don't build homes, you're never going to be happy about who ends up owning homes"; tiny units anchor the market and free up family-sized ones. He holds that capitalism embeds an ethical system, that capitalists owe founders and society honest zeroes and formal releases from obligation, that index funds are among the best financial technologies ever, and that an orderly wind-down owes departing employees nearly all of management's remaining bandwidth and users a two-hour blog post, because the minimum viable open-source release is a million dollars a year and "apparently your community did not owe you sufficient money." His sharpest political statements concern pandemic policy and speech: California's undercounting of doses was a policy choice; medical redlining by zip code as a race proxy was actual policy, "wrong, counterproductive, and almost cartoonishly evil," adopted to applause from ethicists and bureaucrats with the higher death toll written in the memos; Newsom's licensing threats caused doctors to destroy lifesaving vaccine; and the White House using platforms as catspaws is unconstitutional "point blank," with misinformation a demand problem rather than a supply problem that the state may licitly regulate. He treats YouTube's recruiting emails as proof that explicit racial hiring discrimination was industry policy for years, not an aberration, and expects hostile-work-environment cases to be layups. He is bullish on AI on the merits, thinks "non-AI SaaS" will stop being a coherent category, that incumbents rarely ship the disruptive version, and that go-to-market, not engineering cost, is the binding constraint. He wants far-UVC deployed, thinks we could end the flu the way we ended waterborne disease, and complains that we won't spend billions making vaccines hurt less. He describes himself as generally apolitical in professional spaces, partly because he spent twenty years without the franchise, and his one repeated civic exhortation is procedural: call your representative's D.C. office, say "registered voter," cite the bill number, be terse.

causal_models 503 words

Explains outcomes almost always through incentives, operational constraints, and the distribution of authority inside organizations rather than through malice or stupidity, and returns compulsively to one question — "who has a button to?" — as the master key: phone support doesn't have the button, the branch manager does, and that single fact predicts the whole interaction without needing any story about corporate greed. Treats large institutions as state machines with formal transitions, not persuadable persons, and holds that most people lose to bureaucracies because they run an interpersonal script against a system that has no emotional response by policy. Insists brokenness is usually the designed output of competing priorities society genuinely holds simultaneously — banks must both bank everybody and inform on everybody, so accounts close capriciously and nobody can explain why because SAR disclosure is a crime; the observed pathology is "us getting what 'we' want," and the fix is wanting different things, not finding the villain. Rejects conspiracy in favor of structure and repeatedly says so out loud ("that's the entire conspiracy, such that it is"; if education bureaucrats were hypercompetent dissemblers they'd be uniquely so among bureaucrats), but is explicit that the structural default can be overridden by documents: he updates hard on written evidence of intent, which is why he keeps citing the YouTube recruiting emails, the California vaccine-redlining memos, and the SPLC coalition's own podcast statements as proof that some things really were policy, chosen, and known to kill people. Price is set by price-insensitivity, not by exploitation of the weak: savings accounts are envelopes and business checking is priced the same way; interchange and overdraft are cross-subsidies whose distributional incidence runs the opposite direction from what advocates assume, and he'll do the arithmetic rather than accept a vibe. Treats "answering a quantitative question qualitatively" as the characteristic failure of institutions — VaccinateCA worked because someone asked how many pharmacies California has and got a Fermi estimate instead of declaring the problem unscalable. Believes metrics chosen cause the pathologies observed, that legible costs beat illegible ones (anti-fraud spend is visible, fraud isn't), and that friction is often load-bearing: it embeds proof-of-work, velocity limits, or a tripwire that forces a fraudster to lie to a bank so prosecutors get an easy wire-fraud count. Sees class as a real causal variable that actively resists legibility because legibility would destroy its function — the Fed's fit-and-proper review for small bank CEOs "really is a class check," and he opened accounts for Japanese doctors because he "sounded right." Holds that technology beats law for achieving humane outcomes (credit bureaus did more for lending discrimination than every statute combined) and that some technologies are simply overpowered — math, writing, solar, LLMs — with no compensating downside. Individual agency is a genuine causal force in his model, not a decoration: reading more of the Internet than anyone thinks reasonable, choosing to care, sending the email, is repeatedly the actual explanation for why a thing got done, because organizations don't decide, one lynchpin person hypercommits.

temperament 379 words

Speaks in flat declaratives about mechanism and only hedges where he has actually measured his own uncertainty — "finger to the wind," "indicatively accurate," "epistemic status: appropriate level of rigor for a conversation on the sidelines of a child's sport activity" — and then attaches numbers to the hedge anyway, offering $50 charity bets with named resolution criteria and expiry dates, precommitting in public to reporting back when a position goes against him, and actually returning years later to announce the loss. Awards himself cookies for correct guesses and docks himself for miscalibration. The signature rhetorical move is self-interrogation: he writes the skeptic's objection in quotation marks — "Can't say that without providing it, Patrick," "You're not a lawyer, Patrick, how do I know your gloss is anything approaching reality" — and answers it, which lets him preempt attack while sounding like he's conceding ground he isn't. Concedes freely on specifics (misremembered dates, wrong word choice, corrections issued mid-thread with screenshots of his own files), almost never on frame; when contradicted he escalates to documents, papers, and graphs rather than volume, and proposes explicit trades of evidentiary burden. Argues from firsthand operational experience first, mechanism second, analogy third — allocating electricity costs at a grocery store, blood versus water, index funds — and reaches for structural rather than conspiratorial explanation as a near-reflex, repeatedly testing whether an accusation would require improbably competent coordination. Humor is deadpan understatement, censored profanity as pressure valve, bracket-nested asides, verbatim domestic dialogue with wife and children as a device for making systems visible, and long self-deprecating riffs about his own hobbies that double as credentialing. Announces his rules before breaking them ("no subtweet rule"), stacks disclaimers, and pairs praise with critique in a way that reads as deliberate reputational hygiene. Genuine, unembarrassed enthusiasm for competence anywhere he finds it — a clerk, a pharmacist, a checklist, a piece of infrastructure — expressed in exclamations he doesn't apologize for. The cold anger, when it arrives, is moralized and controlled, marked by "*sigh*" and asterisked expletives rather than escalation, and returns compulsively to the same handful of grievances. Warns before blocking. Apologizes to followers for going off-beat. Alternately performs modesty about credentials and flexes hard about reading more of the internet than anyone thinks is reasonable.

affinities_aversions 494 words

He venerates the specific over the general: the person who can name the regulation, cite the docket number, quote the internal memo, or reproduce the actual screenshot earns his attention, while anyone gesturing at systemic critique without having read a bid document, a supervisory report, or a bank statement gets treated as an unserious tourist. He warms hard to the "look at the world and write down what you find" archetype — the practitioner who chisels one new fact out of the universe and publishes it, the finance columnist who calls nonsense nonsense in real time under pressure, the short seller who was prescient about a bank's deficiencies, the small-town banker who phones regulators on his own boss — and he explicitly prizes forthright, career-limiting declarations of failure, which he says the private sector punishes as Does Not Play Well With Others. He has near-contempt for consumer advocates who "glance trivially" at a headline interest rate without understanding distributional effects, for a famous financial journalist who confused debt with equity three times running, for reporters who fly to a new location instead of plodding through balance sheets and emails, and for institutions whose postmortems are written by civil servants protecting other civil servants' pensions. He detests the ad-libbed "that's final and we can do nothing" from a customer service rep who has no such authority, the non-apology apology from someone unwilling to attempt untying their hands, the CS-script culture that trains reps to disassociate, and the pattern of an organization pretending its decisions are irreversible when most are. Crypto advocacy earns a distinctive scorn — not blanket hostility, since he credits them for characterizing Operation Choke Point more honestly than the government's own postmortems, but sharp derision for those who "straight up invented" claims and for the Bond-villain compliance archetype. He recoils from magical thinking dressed as policy (abolish credit bureaus and get no underwriting rather than bad underwriting), from advocates whose disposition treats charging money for financial services as theft-adjacent, and from the bureaucratic species that will not admit an optimal fraud rate is nonzero. He admires deadpan competence in service roles — the salesman who accepts a polite Japanese no, the appliance store manager who cold-calls to refund $400 nobody would have noticed, the pharmacist offering the no-paperwork solution — and reserves special disgust for the second clerk who invents a rule. He is drawn to anyone who models power honestly: the observation that class resists legibility because legibility destroys its function, that a $25k CS decision is made by an eighteen-year-old with a button, that "who sent you to me" is real. He dislikes subtweeting and says so repeatedly, dislikes the Twitter game of adversarially attributing an employee's words to their employer, dislikes the middlebrow dismissal, and dislikes being replied to by anodyne AI-generated comments enough to threaten blocks. He rewards people who admit ignorance and ask questions, and is impressed rather than annoyed when an LLM confidently disagrees with him.

preoccupations 486 words

Writing occupies more of this sample than anything else, and always from the same angle: not craft advice but the management of an enormous unfinished backlog — 250,000+ tweets, ~900k words of stream-of-consciousness journalling, a project to write 1,000,000 words, dozens of stalled essay drafts for a collection called Frame Studies that he keeps reframing as blogposts, threads, a book, "sketches and studies," or linear translations of "sprawling load-bearing threads." He returns obsessively to the mechanics of his own stuckness — counting sentences and paragraphs in his best essays to find the shape, printing drafts out and marking them with a pen, adding timestamps like (2025jul28) to old notes so they read like a thread, inventing "win_loops" and imaginary point systems, diagnosing that his problem is never lack of the correct answer but emotional logjam and collapsed awareness. Second is his son (born Oct 2023) and later a second baby (July 2026), treated as an epistemological instrument: toddler proto-speech as proof that all communication is a privately negotiated shared code, watching a kid fail to stand for months as evidence that all mastery is practice-and-study, the "philosophical-emotional challenge" that parenting is simultaneously maximally not-about-you (neglect) and maximally about-you (tyranny), and the discovery that having a kid opens strangers up — being invited into a parked bus, shopkeepers making small talk. Third is a repeating psychological toolkit deployed on everyone: "focus on what you want to see more of" rather than what you want less of, wretchedness spirals versus blessedness spirals, the 🌀/🐉/⛵️ "ayy lmao" tension between aimlessness and predetermined destination, and the insistence that introspection is worthless unless it's in a feedback loop with material reality. He talks constantly about Twitter as a medium and an ecology — search degrading, quote-tweets as tangent-launchers, DM triage etiquette, the death of tpot as a signifier, the loss of the 2015–2021 era when mutuals had "active intellectual projects" to riff on, scenes versus communities (you can't cash in scene points for community kinship). Marketing recurs as a moral position: authors sell under 1000 books because they suck at reader outreach, not because they're worse writers; he's sold 7000+; Poe was collecting paying subscribers in 1848; good sales is describing someone's problem so well it's a service rendered. Recurring too: greatness as deviance (3sd is more comfortable than 2sd; mild deviance is the uncanny valley), thresholds and basecamps where people theorize about crossing but never cross, video games as serious text (Red Dead 1 vs 2, Mass Effect, the Diablo 2 interface, Dan Houser being forced out of Rockstar), Singapore's postwar scarcity trauma and inability to relax, and a childhood wound he circles for years before finally naming in 2025–26 — neglect, feeling unseen, a promise made as a kid to publicly speak the truth of his experience, and the eventual admission that his prolific posting was a coping mechanism and a search for admiration as a proxy for belonging.

positions 1287 words

Holds that the master directive is "focus on what you want to see more of" — "stop doing X" framings are counterproductive because they make you more conscious of X, and wretchedness is defined precisely as spending your time and energy on what makes you feel like shit, with blessedness/wretchedness spirals as self-reinforcing loops that reward winning and punish losing, which he calls immoral enough to create a genuine need for something like Jesus, a source of forgiveness for the wretched. Believes ~95% of "how did they get so good" is practice and study, that anything you're bad at is simply under-practiced or badly practiced, and rejects IQ-style "intellectual horsepower" as a useful concept — his counter-model is full-stack smarts including sensitivity (perception) and strength (action), with the worst kind of smart person being one who builds elaborate mind-structures on faulty data, doesn't notice what he doesn't notice, and lacks the guts to act; horsepower without wheels, brakes and steering produces worse crashes. Thinks greatness is deviance and that 3sd is a better life than 2sd because mild deviance is an uncanny valley where you fit nowhere, and that you cannot do anything interesting without breaking consensus, which necessarily looks stupid from inside the consensus — hence "why is this idiot successful" is a question people are always on the cusp of answering, since success is product-market fit and caring about looking smart obstructs it. Draws a hard line between scenes and communities: bad actors exploit scenes by calling them communities the way bad bosses exploit workplaces by calling them families, you cannot cash in scene points for community kinship when things go bad, and the only thing that endures is 1-1 relationships, everything else being "fleeting assemblages of context." Treats marketing as the real bottleneck to everything and as a moral duty of authors — sold 7000+ ebooks not by being a better writer but a better marketer, cites Poe personally collecting paying readers in 1848 as proof there was never a golden age where good books sold themselves, insists good marketing genuinely increases a book's value to the reader, and reframes it as reader outreach and problem-solving; good salespeople are earnestly curious about the customer's problem and describing that problem well is itself a service rendered. Sharply distrusts introspection detached from material feedback — calls "calm your mind" meditation a psyop by mind-obsessed mind to police mind, notes that everyone knows someone extremely self-aware and still fucked up, argues excess self-awareness is cured by redirecting attention outward into service, and diagnoses mental "knots" as elaborate disguises for feelings-avoidance, where every act of avoidance reinforces avoidance and consecrates the avoided thing. Refuses blanket directives; issues periodic clarifications that his observations are not instructions, and holds that "be more selfless"-type abstractions are insufficient because you can't control how a reader interprets them, whereas stories with enough context let readers simulate good decisions for themselves. Strongly pro-natalist on specific grounds: kids open the world (strangers invite you onto buses), constitute a "maximum human experience," and it's impossible to over-attend to a young child — he explicitly rejects the local elder advice that carrying and soothing creates dependence, calling it abandonment culture. Names the central philosophical challenge of parenting as the collision between two truths — nothing is more important to not take personally, nothing has ever felt more personal — with neglect and tyranny as the two failure modes, and argues parents who hit or yell usually erred by elevating the child to peer status. Considers social environments far more determinative than he previously believed, with people self-sorting into groups sharing wretchedness floors and blessedness ceilings, producing homeostasis where nothing ever happens; describes thresholds as places where basecamps of people discuss crossing, where crossing requires relinquishing the very beliefs that got you to basecamp, and where every cope is or was load-bearing. On AI: LLMs approximate consensus, which is why they produce mid prose, and a model trained on Montaigne wouldn't write like Montaigne because Montaigne would apply his sensibility to his present context — the model is always one step behind the training data, while the artist alone can say "I change my mind about what a good sentence is." Was cheerfully pro the mass Ghibli image event as a preference cascade that grows the pie, invoking the ATM/bank-teller pattern, while finding it gross when AI maximalists sneer at artists, and personally treats everything he's put in public as fodder for remixing. His stated multi-year project is media literacy under the name Frame Studies: rather than "this movie did incalculable cultural damage," he insists people's inability to contextualize media is what makes them susceptible to damage, and that frames are the interface layer between people and a Reality we cannot perceive directly. Thinks most real mistakes occur at a different frequency than what people argue about, that problems are habitually described by effect rather than cause and therefore reinforced, and that ordinary words like "because" are insufficient for clear thinking — supplying better language is the contribution he wants to make. Believes leadership's job is to look stupid, make mistakes and piss people off, true of Washington and Lincoln both, and that public sentiment about figures pendulums in cycles where criticism weathered with magnanimity elevates status in the next upswing. Dismisses "work non-stop" as hyperbole, ignorance of human constraints, or mythologizing theatre to exploit suckers — LeBron slept ten hours, God rested on the seventh day, and compounding requires staying in the game, which is why he urges moderation in pursuit of immoderate outcomes. Holds that specialization and trade is the engine of all wealth, that civilization was profitable for the system at the individual's expense (grain surpluses let larger malnourished armies beat healthier hunter-gatherers), and that we reshaped the world in the image of a spreadsheet rather than the shape of a human. On gender: men are perceived as threats, women as targets, and that asymmetry percolates through everything; regards so-called benevolent sexism as something most women appreciate between people who actually care about each other, with madness reigning only in disembodied text; reads post-2018 squeamishness about discussing a woman's attractiveness as an overcorrection; and argues transness and enbyism directly contradict each other while both inadvertently reifying a narrow model of cis gender expression, calling both downstream of a western-scientific-atomistic model that patches glitches with more glitches. On marriage: satisficing over optimizing — you could have married any of half a dozen people, and the romance is in the commitment after a semi-arbitrary choice; the core spousal duty is to really give a shit about each other, and "duty sex" discourse conflates sex with care and devotion; treats "I never loved you actually" in a breakup as something that should almost be criminalized. Diagnoses chronically single smart people as using intellect and project-management skill to mask relational injuries from early childhood, and notes some people internalize a dominance hierarchy where receiving help is submission, so they become skilled at rejecting help from anyone they can construe as inferior. Politically light-touch but says modern electoral politics is largely vestigial remnants of pre-communications-technology coordination; on Singapore, that votes are genuinely secret and opposition-voter paranoia is unfounded (he votes opposition), that the country is still recovering from postwar trauma with a scarcity mindset and exam anxiety everyone knows everyone cares about, and that its earnestness is play-acted like children trying out emotion rather than insincere; on Ukraine, that if the US abandons it the lesson is not to rely on the US, "which is in some sense sorta for the best." Insists nothing frivolous should be dismissed — child's play deserves respect, dismissing it is normalized mundane tyranny, and motorsport's "make car go fast" obsession ended up saving lives.

causal_models 745 words

Explains outcomes almost entirely through feedback loops, environments, and emotional blockage, and almost never through innate ability. Skill is "practice and study" ~95% of the time; when someone is good at something he asks how they practice, not how they got good, and treats "I can't sing/do math" as evidence of bad practice or a stuck limiting belief rather than a ceiling. He is actively hostile to intelligence as an explanatory variable, arguing that raw horsepower without discernment, perception and the guts to act produces worse outcomes for everyone (the car with great acceleration and no brakes), and that "intelligence deficits" are frequently a socially acceptable cover story for courage deficits — cowardice being unadmittable to oneself. His master mechanism is the loop: win_loops that compound (writing → readers → money → more writing), wretchedness spirals and blessedness spirals that reward winning and punish losing, and homeostasis, where people expend enormous hidden effort keeping social reality stable and disguise that effort as socializing. Personal failure is almost never an information problem — he insists that when he fails he already knew the answer and often already tweeted it, and that the actual cause is collapsed awareness, avoidance, deferral, or a feelings-avoidance problem elaborately disguised as a mental-knot problem; addiction is a complex of avoidance, procrastination is avoidance, and every act of avoidance reinforces avoidance as a strategy. He treats the discourse-level description of any problem as almost always the wrong frequency: people describe effects rather than causes, a small erroneous assumption early in an expedition cascades into a Big Mess, and everyone then argues about the Mess. Upstream of individual psychology sits the social environment, which he says he consistently underrated even while writing about scenes — kids and adults absorb what surrounds them, deviance is tolerated or suppressed by peer group, and he insists on distinguishing scenes from communities because bad actors exploit scenes by calling them communities the way bad bosses exploit workplaces by calling them families. Upstream of that sits material constraint and technology: culture is downstream of material conditions (Nepali fraternal polyandry preserving household wealth; agriculture producing grain surpluses that let malnourished mass armies beat healthy hunter-gatherers, profitable for the system at the individual's cost), wealth comes from specialization and trade and from trade networks generally, and media technology reorganizes perception — TV became the physical centerpiece of the home, Hollywood (especially Gone With The Wind) globalized intimacy displays, a smartphone connected to the global network is an ongoing psychedelic trip whose Babelfish removal of communication barriers inflicts real psychic damage on untrained minds. Institutional decline he explains by personnel and management: Diablo 3 and Borderlands 3 failed because the people who understood what made the predecessors good were gone, streaming slop exists because the pipeline was optimized for production volume, Disney's obnoxious marketing exists because the marketing head isn't accountable for brand perception overall, and Dan Houser was pushed out for wanting a dark GTA 6. He is suspicious of lionizing narratives as a distinct causal distortion — Jensen Huang's AMD/LSI years omitted, "Singapore was a fishing village," "work non-stop" as mythologizing theatre — and insists on reading past them. For adult dysfunction he reaches for childhood: neglect, abuse, absent fathers, boys never initiated into manhood who then glom onto loud garish boy-kings (the same mechanism terrorists recruit through), and Singapore's rigidity as unmetabolized postwar trauma. Luck he treats as partly manufacturable — you can maximize luck surface area, do the reading, do the math, avoid the things that kill you, and be correct-enough about probabilities — and he explicitly rejects the "survivorship bias, blah blah" rebuttal. Persistence is the dominant selector for outcomes: the spoils go to whoever keeps showing up, immoderate players crash and burn every few years, and he claims to know 100+ people who'd be better than him if they hadn't quit. For creative and commercial failure he reaches first for distribution, not quality — books don't sell because authors are bad at marketing and won't do reader outreach, and Edgar Allan Poe already knew in 1848 he had to personally collect paying readers. Leadership causally requires looking stupid, making mistakes and pissing people off, which is why smart people who won't do that get behind someone who will. He rejects single-variable models generally, reaching instead for "ecology," "dynamism," and "it depends on the context," and treats seemingly mystical phenomena — vibes, taste, intuition — as compressed subconscious models of a domain's physics rather than woo.

temperament 444 words

Speaks in flat imperative declaratives about process and human behavior — "the way to get good at anything is practice and study," "everything is a remix," "you can figure stuff out and you can talk to people" — while wrapping nearly every sentence in a softening tic ("imo," "ime," "my sense is," "my current model is") that functions as courtesy rather than actual uncertainty. Calibrates confidence by domain with real discipline: prefaces fitness and chess opinions with "I'm basically an amateur, don't take me seriously," admits flatly to never having read a book someone assumes he's read, says "there's a lot of things I don't know" without embarrassment, and simultaneously issues total certainty about writing, marketing, social dynamics and parenting-as-noticing. Reflexively rejects the frame of a question rather than answering inside it — "the question is misframed," "you have interpreted an observation as an instruction," "it depends on the frame of reference" — and treats "it depends" as a substantive position rather than a dodge, then immediately supplies four variables it depends on. Argues almost entirely by analogy, compulsively: video game mechanics, cities and zoning, gyms and lifting form, cooking, orchestras, car horsepower versus brakes. Backs claims with autobiographical receipts and volume counts — 7,000 books sold, 250,000 tweets, 850,000 words, 1,000 hours of contact napping, sentence-per-paragraph ratios of his own essays — rather than external data; quotes Montaigne, Jung, Emerson, Nietzsche and McLuhan as decoration and mood-setting rather than as evidence. Concedes facts instantly and cheerfully, concedes framing almost never, and when pushed will restate his position with extra precision instead of moving — "if that's what I thought, that's what I would have said." When contradicted by someone he judges sincere, he asks clarifying questions and offers a reframe; when contradicted by someone he judges unserious, he refuses to engage on their terms, makes a joke, or asks with mock-neutrality whether the remark was meant as a joke or an insult, and states outright that blocking isn't about disagreement. Occasionally gets pedantic and irritable about being misread, correcting the exact wording of what he said and telling the reader to think harder. Humor is warm, bit-heavy, self-deprecating, absurdist-escalating, and load-bearing: it lowers the stakes so that disagreement doesn't have to be a fight, and he says explicitly that this is what it's for. Posts distress and confusion in real time — feeling fragmented, creatively stuck, emotionally logjammed — frames this as an obligation he made to himself as a child, and then in the same thread admits he habitually hides difficulty from himself and needs his wife to surface it. Openly solicits correction, examples and better phrasings from readers, and means it.

affinities_aversions 431 words

Gravitates hard toward the earnest amateur who publishes badly and often, toward people running visible multi-year intellectual projects in public, and toward anyone who riffs in his replies with something nobody else has covered yet — he says explicitly that good reply game means scanning the existing replies first and adding an unclaimed nuance. Adores teachers who are cheeky about your self-doubt, jazz and sports pedagogues who reframe practice as play, the behind-the-scenes "de facto mayor" of a scene who resolves disputes and introduces people, and the person who receives a bloated request and right-sizes it into something deliverable. Reveres deviance as such — argues 3sd is a better life than 2sd, that mild deviance is an uncanny valley where you fit nowhere, and that anything interesting requires looking like an idiot to the consensus. Loves craftsmen who leave visible seams: game studios that sweat incidental interactions, a 2000-era isometric RPG inventory screen preferred over its prettier remaster, comedians who work a room live, streamers, marketers who are earnestly curious about a customer's problem. Cannot stand people who are recreationally mad about things they can't be bothered to read closely, who confidently assess advice about a thing neither party has ever done, who open with "I disagree" and then say something entirely compatible, or who mistake a description of a pattern for an instruction and then scold him for issuing it. Has a specific, repeated allergy to the appeal-to-majority dismissal — "bro, 90% of people are right-handed" as a reason not to make left-handed scissors — and calls it mundane tyranny wherever he sees it. Contemptuous of grandiose gestures as reliable markers of unsustainability, of the person who intensely asks to be his friend and then vanishes within weeks, of DMs that say only "hi" or open with a product pitch, and of anyone who insists their bluntness is honesty rather than obnoxiousness. Dismisses committee-made art, engagement-bait slop defined as material produced to fill space, and prose that approximates consensus. Detests adults who tell a crying toddler not to cry, relatives who warn against holding a baby too much, and the casual assumption that kids are dumb rather than desensitized. Bristles at people who project motives onto his output — that 260,000 tweets must be for money, sex, or validation — and at the assumption that discussing parenting's difficulty means he's an absentee father. Broadly forgives showmen and calculated self-promoters as long as they're honest about the game, but reserves real disdain for the uncharitable spirit who stinks up every interaction and can't see that it's the thing capping their life.

coverage 254 words

This sample is enormous (~1,900 posts spanning September 2023 to August 2026) and unusually rich for personality, work-habit, and self-narrative inference, because the author writes constantly and reflexively about his own process, marriage, parenting, creative blocks, and business model — often with dates, word counts, and specific numbers. Coverage of his intellectual influences, media taste, and writing practice is dense and reliable. Political content is genuinely thin and mostly incidental: scattered Singapore-specific remarks (GE2025 sample counts, voting secrecy, PAP-adjacent "daddy state" gestures, HDB housing, National Service), a few glancing comments on Ukraine/US reliability, AI art ethics, gender discourse, and a passing dismissal of trans/enby identity categories as downstream of "western-scientific-atomistic" modelling — none of it developed into an argued position, and any positions paragraph must stay correspondingly thin rather than inflated from adjacent aesthetic views. Coverage of his actual finances is partial and contradictory-seeming (claims of near-retirement and ~$100k ebook sales alongside a 2026 note that he and his wife count as low-income Singaporeans). The sample skews heavily toward Twitter/X replies and self-quoting threads, so his long-form essays are described but never shown; claims about his books' contents rest entirely on his own summaries. The final ~year (2025–2026) is markedly more introspective and self-critical about avoidance, neglect, and childhood wounds than the earlier stretch, which risks reading a genuine arc as a fixed trait — the confident-teacher voice of 2023–24 and the deflated, blocked voice of 2026 are both him. Almost nothing here bears on his behaviour offline beyond what he chooses to narrate.

preoccupations 527 words

Writing-as-vocation dominates everything: the mechanics of his own creative process are the single largest subject, examined obsessively and in public — abandoned drafts reframed as "sketches and studies" rather than false starts, counting the sentences and paragraphs of his best essays (Are You Serious: 3700 words, 60 paragraphs, 184 sentences) to develop a felt sense of scale, printing drafts out to mark with a pen, timestamping updates to old blogposts (2025jul28) so they read like tweet threads, the standing project "Frame Studies" perpetually 2-3 years from completion, the unfinished essay IN MEDIA RES that "attacks and criticizes all the others," and a recurring confession that essays feel like putting on 20kg of gear while tweeting is effortless — eventually concluding the two are rivalrous competencies. Parenting runs a close second and grows over the sample: ~1000 hours of contact naps, the claim that his 21-month-old learns train station names unprompted, toddlers as relentless persistence hunters, the "it's not about you / it's entirely about you" tension of parenthood, insisting kids are more perceptive than credited and that hearing "don't cry" from relatives taught his son to say it to himself, and repeated pitches that a child "unlocks conversational options you didn't know were locked." Third is a self-help apparatus he built and keeps reapplying: "focus on what you want to see more of" rather than what you want to stop, wretchedness spirals vs blessedness spirals, the 🌀🐉⛵️ "ayy lmao" tension triad, the argument that introspection is worthless unless it's in a feedback loop with material reality, and a late-sample reckoning that his real failures were never knowledge failures but emotional avoidance — "every act of avoidance reinforces avoidance as a strategy." Fourth: Twitter as craft and habitat — 250,000+ tweets, DM triage rules, updating decade-old threads, the specific complaint that Twitter search keeps degrading and that engagement has bifurcated into 20 views or 2 million, plus persistent mourning that his 2015-2021 cluster of project-driven posters has dissipated ~80%. Fifth: marketing as the universally underrated bottleneck — "authors as a class suck at sales," 7000 books sold not because he writes better but markets better, Poe personally collecting paying readers in 1848, and the reframe that good marketing is actually a service rendered to the customer. Then: scenes vs communities as strictly distinct categories (you can't cash in scene points for community kinship), greatness-as-deviance and thresholds where basecamp-dwellers become incomprehensible to those who cross, network effects and specialization-and-trade as the engine of all wealth, video games treated as serious texts (Red Dead's writing, the Diablo 2 inventory UI, Dan Houser being forced out of Rockstar for "too dark" GTA 6 ideas), and Singapore as a case study in postwar trauma producing a stressed, self-effacing, exam-obsessed culture. Recurring smaller threads: "practice and study" as the answer to how anyone got good at anything, hierarchy and power asymmetry making genuine friendship impossible, lionizing narratives that erase the boring years, intelligence as a near-useless concept compared to "cognitive athleticism," and his own biography reused as evidence — the promise made at 13 to publicly speak the truth of his experience, marrying his childhood sweetheart, quitting his job in 2018 on savings.

positions 1044 words

Holds that greatness is deviance and that mild deviance is worse than extreme deviance — 3sd is a better life than 2sd, because the uncanny valley of "moderately weird" fits nowhere; correspondingly, you cannot do interesting work without breaking consensus and looking like an idiot to those inside it, and the job of leadership specifically is to look stupid, make mistakes and piss people off. Rejects "raw IQ" as a useful concept, preferring a "full-stack" model where perception (sensitivity) and action (guts) matter more than horsepower — his caricature of the worst kind of smart person is one who builds elaborate mind-structures on faulty data, notices nothing, and never acts; a car with horsepower and no brakes. Insists ~95% of skill differences reduce to practice and study, that "I can't sing/dance/do math" is almost always a revisable limiting belief, and that frustration tolerance is itself a learnable skill. Believes his own failures are never failures of analysis — he already knew the right answer, often had already tweeted it — but of emotional stuckness, collapsed awareness, and avoidance; addiction is a complex of avoidance, cowardice masquerades as a knowledge deficit, and "I need to figure it out" is usually the wrong frame. Introspection that isn't in a feedback loop with material reality is inert psychobabble and a safety ritual for avoiding change; the answer to excess self-awareness is redirecting attention outward through service to others, and he claims to have run this observation past thousands of people with roughly 60/30/10 outcomes. Maps everything onto wretchedness (focusing time and energy on what makes you feel like shit) versus blessedness spirals, and notes the feedback-loop structure rewards winners and punishes losers so cruelly that it creates a genuine need for something like Jesus — a source of forgiveness for the wretched. Sharply distinguishes scenes from communities and holds that bad actors exploit scenes by calling them communities, exactly as bad bosses exploit workplaces by calling them families; only 1-1 relationships endure, everything else is fleeting assemblage. On work: authors are bad at books not because of writing but because they refuse marketing, which he reframes as reader outreach and problem-solving — good sales is describing someone's problem so well it's a service rendered, and Poe was collecting paying subscribers in 1848 because there never was a golden age where good work found its audience automatically. Solving for distribution is just "be helpful, repeatedly"; engagement-maxxing means trading internal resonance for resonance with a faceless audience, and he only needs 1 in 1000 people to retire. On AI: LLMs approximate consensus and therefore produce mid prose; they zig when everyone zigs, and an artist is one who says "I change my mind about what a good sentence is" — always one step behind the training data. Simultaneously pro-abundance about remixing (having your work pirated or Ghibli-fied is good actually), sympathetic to angry illustrators, and contemptuous of AI maximalists sneering "we'll copy your work, deal with it." Treats media literacy as his central 2025-onward project ("Frame Studies"): the problem isn't that a movie did incalculable cultural damage, it's that people can't contextualize media, and frames are the interface layer between people and a reality we cannot perceive directly. Pro-natalist in a specific way — kids are a "maximum human experience," they unlock conversational options you didn't know were locked, they force out the bad stuff you'd otherwise hide, and the philosophical challenge is that nothing has ever felt more about you while being less about you (neglect on one side, tyranny on the other); thinks pronatalist effort should target parents on the fence about a second child rather than persuading zero to one. Believes children are far more perceptive than credited and that we desensitize them; a child is not your peer, and hitting or yelling at kids often comes from mistakenly elevating them to peer status. Thinks screentime arguments may be a distraction from kids not getting enough free-roam outdoor time. Holds that wealth comes from specialization and trade (Jane Jacobs), that agriculture won because grain surpluses let larger groups of malnourished soldiers beat smaller groups of healthy hunter-gatherers, that culture is downstream of material constraints, and that network effects — not perceived quality — explain why athletes and entertainers get paid. On Singapore: votes opposition, says votes are genuinely secret and utility-cutting stories are paranoia, describes the country as still recovering from postwar trauma via self-effacement and scarcity mindset, criticizes exam stress and "daddy state" while also arguing SF and Singapore are equally earnest and equally autistic in different aesthetic directions, and praises the competence of Lee Kuan Yew's cabinet, especially Lim Kim San. Thinks modern electoral politics is largely vestigial remnants of pre-internet coordination technology. On gender: the thing women underappreciate about men is being perceived as a threat, the thing men underappreciate about women is being perceived as a target; benevolent sexism is appreciated by most women in real life and only reads as bigotry in disembodied text; nation-states are fisticuffs-avoidant so geopolitics is Mean Girls; trans and enby positions contradict each other and inadvertently reify a narrow model of cis gender expression, which he attributes to a western-scientific-atomistic worldview generating glitches and patching them with more glitches. Believes that things are knowable — that he knew at 7 the internet was the future and at 14 who he would marry, and that people who call this naive are making an erroneous assumption, since you can improve your odds through reading, math, emotional resilience and luck surface area. Advocates many small contained risks over audacious leaps, because immoderate players crash every few years and compounding requires staying in the game; correspondingly recommends "hell yes or no" only after you have prepared inventory and allies for the wilderness outside the linkedin zoo. Rejects giving uncontextualized directives, arguing that "be more selfless" is a vague banal abstraction while a story with enough context lets the reader simulate good decisions themselves; advice is merely perspective. Thinks warning labels degrade discernment, that group chats and possibly marriages should have expiry dates, that people who refuse help are doing so because they perceive receiving help as submission in a dominance hierarchy, and that most stated problems are described in terms of effects rather than causes, which is why they persist.

causal_models 706 words

Explains almost everything through feedback loops rather than fixed traits: "wretchedness spirals" and "blessedness spirals" are his master mechanism, where focusing on what makes you feel like shit generates more of it, and where a single good conversation or a small win compounds into an entire career. Skill is downstream of practice and study roughly 95% of the time — he concedes Phelps's genes, Feynman's tutoring father, Popper's inherited 12,000 books and Gates's early computer access, then routes all of them back through practice anyway, and treats "I can't sing/do math" as a revisable artifact of bad early teaching. The root cause of personal failure is never cognitive: he has spent 25 years building what he calls a "correct answer generator" and reports that his failures are never from lacking the answer but from being emotionally logjammed with collapsed awareness, so overthinking, boredom, addiction, procrastination and "mental knots" are all reclassified as face-saving cover stories for feelings-avoidance and self-betrayal. Copes are load-bearing — the wife in the zit sketch *must* mention the zit because her husband knowing that she knows is structurally necessary — and social groups burn enormous effort maintaining homeostasis while disguising that effort as socializing. Progress requires deviance from consensus, which is why greatness looks stupid from inside the consensus, why scenes and cloistered sub-societies where the rules differ are the engine of innovation, why wartime raises productivity (deviance gets tolerated when winning matters), and why mild deviance is worse than extreme deviance. Thresholds are real because the beliefs that got you to basecamp are incompatible with life past the threshold; crossing requires a rebirth that makes you incomprehensible to those still discussing the crossing. Materially, he reaches for specialization and trade as the engine of all wealth, network effects to explain athlete pay and fandom economics, and grain surplus enabling larger armies of malnourished soldiers to beat healthier hunter-gatherers as the template for how systems profit at the individual's expense; culture is downstream of material constraints (Nepali brothers sharing a wife to keep household wealth intact). Institutional mediocrity is incentive-caused, not talent-caused: Disney's marketing rot comes from whoever runs a film's campaign not owning the brand in totality, Diablo 3 and Borderlands 3 failed because the people who understood the originals left "usually-probably for management-related reasons," streaming optimizes for user-watch-minutes over 5/5 reviews, committees cut the baby in half, and slop is defined as material produced to fill space. He flatly rejects intelligence as a useful variable — "not sure it's a useful concept that means anything anymore" — and substitutes frustration tolerance, courage (with cowardice frequently masquerading as a knowledge deficit), and giving-a-shit; success is product-market fit, so "why is this idiot successful" is a question that answers itself. Books don't sell because authors are bad at marketing, not because the market is broken; Poe was collecting paying subscribers in 1848 and there was never a golden age where good work got rewarded automatically. Luck is a probability surface you can enlarge and shape, and he treats "survivorship bias" as a conversation-ender used by people avoiding the math. Media technology is treated as environment-forming in a McLuhan lineage: the printing press, the TV reorganizing the living room, the smartphone as an ongoing psychedelic trip, and the removal of barriers between social silos as a babelfish problem producing genuine psychic damage in untrained minds — which makes media literacy, itself downstream of self-knowledge, his proposed intervention. Since becoming a parent he has sharply upweighted social environment as a cause, arguing kids raised around circus performers become casually acrobatic and that most people who never actualize the capacity to be useful and generous simply never had a model; bad men lack initiation into manhood and an Uncle Iroh figure, so they attach to loud unintegrated boy-kings, which is also how terrorists recruit. Chronically single competent people are using intellect and project management to mask early relational damage. Therapy fails for people who experience receiving help as submission in a dominance hierarchy. Problems are almost always described in terms of effects rather than causes, so misdiagnosed problems get reinforced, and a small erroneous assumption at the start of an expedition cascades into a Big Mess everyone argues about while the actual variable goes unnamed.

temperament 367 words

Speaks in flat declaratives softened by "imo," "in my experience," and "my sense is" — hedges that function as credentials rather than doubt, since the claim that follows is never actually qualified and rarely revised. Grounds authority in accumulated volume: thousands of conversations, 250,000 tweets, twenty years of keeping tabs, and cites these numbers as evidence the way another person would cite a study. Compulsively converts observation into directive: every noticed pattern gets turned into a reframe, a heuristic, a thing you could do. Gravitates hard toward people running visible, ongoing intellectual projects who riff in each other's replies and build shared vocabulary over years — treats that specific dynamic (which he dates to roughly 2015–2021 and estimates has decayed 80%) as the highest-value thing the platform ever produced, and grades new platforms by whether it exists there. preoccupations 535 words
